\chapter{Atomes}
\section{Basic tools}

we start the book by building logic step by step.block by block in first it
might seem a little annoying but I advice you to be patient.book will show its
power soon. like euclidian geometry this book has undefined concepts. I tried
to explain them for you to understand.but they don't have any definition.
\begin{undefined}
    \textbf{Urelements} are the most fundamental entities in a logical.
    They cannot be constructed, defined, or described in terms of other things.
    Their existence is primitive—accepted without internal structure or relational properties.
    Urelements simply are.
\end{undefined}

In this book, we will denote individual urelements using lowercase letters $u$,
$v$, $w$,$...$

\begin{undefined}
    \textbf{Type} parallel lines used to show that an object exists
    (you will undrestnd what is object soon.)
    (we will show you how to use them).

    \begin{tikzpicture}
        % Vertical lines
        \draw (0,0) -- (0,4);
        \draw (1,0) -- (1,4);
        \draw (2,0) -- (2,4);
        \draw (3,0) -- (3,4);
        % Labels between lines
        \node at (-0.5,3) {1.};
        \node at (-0.5,2) {2.};
        \node at (-0.5,1) {3.};
        \node at (0.2,3) {$u$};
        \node at (1.2,2) {$v$};
        \node at (2.2,1) {$w$};
    \end{tikzpicture}
\end{undefined}

\begin{Rule}
    \textbf{fusion} it's the Rule that explain how sets made.
    (this rule is schema.it means it works everywhere for every object.)

    \begin{tikzpicture}
        % Vertical lines
        \draw (0,0) -- (0,5);
        \draw (1,0) -- (1,5);
        % \draw (1.5,3.6) -- (-0.1,3.6) -- (-0.1,0.5) -- (1.5,0.5);
        % Labels between lines
        \node at (-0.5,4) {$m.$};
        \node at (-0.6,2) {$m+n.$};
        \node at (-0.5,1) {$o.$};
        \node at (0.2,4) {$u_{0}$};
        \node at (0.2,3) {$\vdots$};
        \node at (0.2,2) {$u_{n}$};
        \node at (2,1) {$\{u_{0} , u_{1}... u_{n}\}$};
    \end{tikzpicture}
\end{Rule}

\begin{undefined}
    \textbf{Set} we can't say what exactly sets are but we know sets can be made by fusion
    (if ``$a$'' is a set we write ``$set(a)$''
    from now we denote sets with uppercase letters like $A$, $B$, $C$, ...).
\end{undefined}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Object} urelements and sets.(we write obj instead of object).
    (from now we denote objects with lowercase letters like $a$, $b$, $c$, ...).

\end{definition}

\begin{Rule}
    \textbf{fission} it show us how to break an obj.
    (this rule is scheme it means it works everywhere for every obj.)

    \begin{tikzpicture}
        % Vertical lines
        \draw (0,-1) -- (0,4);
        \draw (1,-1) -- (1,4);
        % \draw (1.5,3.6) -- (-0.1,3.6) -- (-0.1,0.5) -- (1.5,0.5);
        % Labels between lines
        \node at (-0.5,3) {$o.$};
        \node at (-0.5,2) {$m.$};
        \node at (-0.6,0) {$m+n.$};
        \node at (2,3) {$\{a_{0} , a_{1}... a_{n}\}$};
        \node at (0.2,2) {$a_{0}$};
        \node at (0.2,1) {$\vdots$};
        \node at (0.2,0) {$a_{n}$};
    \end{tikzpicture}

\end{Rule}

\begin{example}
    \textbf{} $\{a,b,c\} /\therefore \{a,b\}$
    (prove if $\{a,b,c\}$ exists then $\{a,c\}$ exists)

    \begin{tikzpicture}
        % Vertical lines
        \draw (0,-2) -- (0,4);
        \draw (1,-2) -- (1,4);
        % \draw (1.5,3.6) -- (-0.1,3.6) -- (-0.1,-1.5) -- (1.5,-1.5);
        % Labels between lines
        \node at (-0.5,3) {1.};
        \node at (-0.5,2) {2.};
        \node at (-0.5,1) {3.};
        \node at (-0.5,0) {4.};
        \node at (-0.5,-1) {5.};
        \node at (1.7,3) {$\{a,b,c\}$};
        \node at (0.2,2) {$a$};
        \node at (3,2) {fi1};
        \node at (0.2,1) {$b$};
        \node at (3,1) {fi1};
        \node at (0.2,0) {$c$};
        \node at (3,0) {fi1};
        \node at (1.7,-1) {$\{a,c\}$};
        \node at (3,-1) {fu2,3,4};
    \end{tikzpicture}

\end{example}

\begin{example}
    \textbf{} $\{a,b\} /\therefore \{b,a\}$

    \begin{tikzpicture}
        % Vertical lines
        \draw (0,-1) -- (0,4);
        \draw (1,-1) -- (1,4);
        % \draw (1.5,3.6) -- (-0.1,3.6) -- (-0.1,-0.5) -- (1.5,-0.5);
        % Labels between lines
        \node at (-0.5,3) {1.};
        \node at (-0.5,2) {2.};
        \node at (-0.5,1) {3.};
        \node at (-0.5,0) {4.};
        \node at (1.7,3) {$\{a,b\}$};
        \node at (0.2,2) {$a$};
        \node at (3,2) {fi1};
        \node at (0.2,1) {$b$};
        \node at (3,1) {fi1};
        \node at (1.7,0) {$\{b,a\}$};
        \node at (3,0) {fu2,3};
    \end{tikzpicture}
\end{example}

\chapter{propositional logic (zeroth-order logic)}

\section{What is proposition?}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{predicate}  ``$set()$'' and identity ``$=$'' and membership ``$\in$'' symbols are predicates.
    ``$set()$'' is unary and identity and membership  are binary.
\end{definition}

capital letter ``$(P,Q,R)$'' showes \textbf{predicates}.

%
\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Atomic proposition} we define atomic propositions like this.

    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item if ``$a$'' is an obj then ``$set(a)$'' is an atomic proposition.
        \item if ``$a,b$'' are obj then ``$a=b$'' is an atomic proposition.
        \item if ``$a,b$'' are obj then ``$a \in b$'' is an atomic proposition.
    \end{enumerate}
    we denote atomic propositions with ``$p_{i}$''.
\end{definition}

%
\begin{undefined}
    \textbf{logical operators} ($\neg ,\to ,\land ,\lor$) tool that used between
    atoms to make more complex propositions
    (all of the opratores are binary except "$\neg$" that is unary ).
\end{undefined}

you can easily see that formula are made up by atomic propositions.in general
we say formula is an atomic formula or combination of them.

%
\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Propositions} these are propositions.

    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item atomic propositions ``$(p_{i})$''.
        \item if ``$p$'' is a formula then ``$\neg p$'' is a formula too.
        \item if ``$p ,q$'' are two propositions then ``$p \bigcirc q $'' is a formula
              too.($\bigcirc $ can be ``$\to,\land ,\lor$'').
    \end{enumerate}

\end{definition}
for example ``$(p_0 \to p_1 ,\neg p_3)$'' \\
we denote propositions with ``$p ,q ,r ,...$''.

\section{Rules of propositions.}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{proposition schema(variable proposition)} a proposition that is unknown
    for us and it works as variable.(we show them with ``$\varphi ,\psi ,\theta ,... $'')
\end{definition}

\begin{undefined}
    \textbf{Assumption for conditional proof(acp)} is a tool for assuming a proposition.
    (any proposition can't get out but it can get in. right line must be shoter than left one.(it can't fall out).  and every hypothesis that opens must be closed.)
    % (you will undrestnd what is proposition soon.)

    \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
        \open
        \have {1} {}
        \open
        \have {2} {}
        \open
        \have {3} {}
        \have {4} {}
        \close
        \have {5} {}
        \close
        \have {6} {}
        \close
    \end{fitchproof}

\end{undefined}

\begin{Rule}
    \textbf{rules of logical operations} $(\to  I, \to E ,\land I ,\land E ,\lor I ,\lor E ,\neg I ,\neg E,\bot I ,\bot E )$
    this rule is scheme it means it works everywhere for every propositions.
    (thats why we use $\varphi, \psi $ instead of $p,q$)

    \begin{tabular}{|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|}
        \hline
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \open
            \have [ ] {1} {\varphi}
            \have [ ] {2} {\vdots}
            \have  [ ] {2} {\psi}
            \close
            \have  [ ]{2} {\therefore \varphi \to  \psi} \by{$\to I$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt} &
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \hypo [ ]{1} {\varphi \to \psi}
            \hypo [ ] {2} {\varphi}
            \have  [ ]{2} {\therefore \psi} \by{$\to E$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt}   \\
        \hline
    \end{tabular}
    \begin{example}
        \textbf{} for  ``$p,q,r$'' we can prove these.
        \begin{enumerate}
            \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
            \item  $p \rightarrow q, q \rightarrow r /\therefore p \rightarrow r$
            \item  $q /\therefore p \rightarrow q$
        \end{enumerate}

    \end{example}

    \begin{tabular}{>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}}

        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \have {} {p \to q} \by {$ass$} {}
            \have {} {q \to r} \by {$ass$} {}
            \open
            \have {} {p} \by {$acp$} {}
            \have {} {q} \by {$\to E  1,3$} {}
            \have {} {r} \by {$\to E  2,4$} {}
            \close
            \have {} {p \to r} \by  {$\to I$} {}

        \end{fitchproof}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt} &
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \have {} {q } \by {$ass$} {}
            \open
            \have {} {p} \by {$acp$} {}
            \have {} {q} \by {$ass$} {}
            \close
            \have {} {p \to q} \by {$\to I $} {}

        \end{fitchproof}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt}   \\

    \end{tabular}

    \begin{tabular}{|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|}
        \hline
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \hypo [ ]{1} {\varphi}
            \hypo [ ] {2} {\psi}
            \have  [ ]{2} {\therefore \varphi \land \psi} \by{$\land I$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt} &
        \begin{minipage}[t]{\linewidth}
            \centering
            \begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\linewidth}
                \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
                    \hypo [ ]{1} {\varphi \land \psi}
                    \have [ ]{2} {\therefore \varphi}  \by{$\land E$}{}
                \end{fitchproof}
            \end{minipage}
            % \hfill
            \begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\linewidth}
                \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
                    \hypo [ ]{1} {\varphi \land \psi}
                    \have [ ]{2} {\therefore\psi} \by{$\land E$}{}
                \end{fitchproof}
            \end{minipage}
        \end{minipage}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt}   \\
        \hline
    \end{tabular}

    \begin{example}
        \textbf{} for  ``$p,q,r$'' we can prove these.
        \begin{enumerate}
            \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
            \item $p \wedge q /\therefore q \wedge p$
            \item $(p \wedge q) \wedge r /\therefore p \wedge(q \wedge r)$
            \item $p \rightarrow q /\therefore(p \wedge r) \rightarrow q$
            \item $(p \wedge q) \rightarrow r /\therefore p \rightarrow(q \rightarrow r)$
            \item  $p \rightarrow(q \rightarrow r) /\therefore(p \wedge r) \rightarrow q$
        \end{enumerate}

    \end{example}

    \begin{tabular}{>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}}
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \have {} {p \land q} \by {$ass$} {}
            \have {} {p} \by {$\land E,1$} {}
            \have {} {q} \by {$\land E,1$} {}
            \have {} {q \land p} \by {$\land I,2,3$} {}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt} &
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \have{1}{(p \land q) \land r}      \by{$ass$}{}
            \have{2}{p \land q}               \by{$\land E,1$}{}
            \have{3}{r}                       \by{$\land E,1$}{}
            \have{4}{p}                       \by{$\land E,2$}{}
            \have{5}{q}                       \by{$\land E,2$}{}
            \have{6}{q \land r}               \by{$\land I,3,5$}{}
            \have{7}{p \land (q \land r)}     \by{$\land I,4,6$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt}   \\
    \end{tabular}

    \begin{tabular}{>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{5cm}|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{5cm}|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{5cm}}

        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \have {} {p \to q} \by {$ass$} {}
            \open
            \have {} {p \land r} \by {acp} {}
            \have {} {p} \by {$\land E,2$} {}
            \have {} {q} \by {$\to E,1,3$} {}
            \close
            \have {} {(p \land r) \to q} \by {$\to I$} {}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt} &
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \have {} {(p \land q) \to r} \by {$ass$} {}
            \open
            \have {} {p} \by {$acp$} {}
            \open
            \have {} {q} \by {$acp$} {}
            \have {} {p \land q} \by {$\land I 2,3$} {}
            \have {} {r} \by {$\to E  1,4$} {}
            \close
            \have {} {q \to r} \by {$\to I$} {}
            \close
            \have {} {p \to (q \to r)} \by  {$\to I$} {}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt} &
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]

            \have{1}{p \rightarrow (q \rightarrow r)} \by{$ass$}{}
            \open
            \have{2} {p \land q} \by{$acp$}{}

            \have{3}{p} \by{$\land E,2 $}{}
            \have{4}{q \rightarrow r} \by{$\to E,1,3$}{}
            \have{5}{q} \by{$\land E,2 $}{}
            \have{6}{r} \by{$\to E,4,5$}{}
            \close
            \have{7}{(p \land q) \rightarrow r} \by{$\to I $}{}

        \end{fitchproof}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt}
        \\

    \end{tabular}

    \begin{tabular}{|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|}
        \hline
        \begin{minipage}[t]{\linewidth}
            \centering
            \begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\linewidth}
                \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
                    \hypo [ ]{1} {\varphi}
                    \have  [ ]{2} {\therefore \varphi \lor \psi } \by{$\lor I$}{}
                \end{fitchproof}
            \end{minipage}
            % \hfill
            \begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\linewidth}
                \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
                    \hypo [ ]{1} {\varphi}
                    \have  [ ]{2} {\therefore  \psi \lor \varphi} \by{$\lor I$}{}
                \end{fitchproof}
            \end{minipage}
        \end{minipage}

        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt} &
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \have [ ] {1} {\varphi \lor \psi}
            \open
            \have [ ] {1} {\varphi}
            \have [ ] {2} {\vdots}
            \have  [ ] {2} {\theta}
            \close
            \open
            \have [ ] {1} {\psi}
            \have [ ] {2} {\vdots}
            \have  [ ] {2} {\theta}
            \close
            \have  [ ]{2} {\therefore \theta} \by{$\lor E$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt}
        \\
        \hline
    \end{tabular}

    \begin{example}
        \textbf{} for  ``$p,q,r$'' we can prove these.
        \begin{enumerate}
            \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
            \item $p \vee q /\therefore q \vee p$
            \item $(p \vee q) \vee r /\therefore p \vee(q \vee r)$
            \item $ p \wedge(q \vee r) /\therefore(p \wedge q) \vee(p \wedge r)$
            \item  $(p \rightarrow q) \wedge(r \rightarrow s) /\therefore(p \vee r) \rightarrow(q \vee s)$
        \end{enumerate}
    \end{example}

    \begin{tabular}{>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}}

        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]

            \have{2}{p \lor q} \by{$ass$}{}
            \open
            \have{3}{p} \by{$acp$}{}
            \have{4}{p \lor q } \by{$\lor I,2$}{}
            \close
            \open
            \have{5}{q} \by{$acp$}{}
            \have{6}{q \lor p} \by{$\lor I,4$}{}

            \close
            \have{8}{q \lor p } \by{$\lor E,1$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt} &
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \have{1}{(p \lor q) \lor r} \by{ass}{}
            \open
            \have{2}{p \lor q} \by{$acp$}{}
            \open
            \have{3}{p} \by{$acp$}{}
            \have{4}{p \lor (q \lor r)} \by{$\lor I,3$}{}
            \close
            \open
            \have{5}{q} \by{$acp$}{}
            \have{6}{q \lor r} \by{$\lor I,5$}{}
            \have{7}{p \lor (q \lor r)} \by{$\lor I,6$}{}
            \close
            \have{8}{p \lor (q \lor r)} \by{$\lor E,2$}{}
            \close
            \open
            \have{9}{r} \by{$acp$}{}
            \have{10}{q \lor r} \by{ $\lor I,9$}{}
            \have{11}{p \lor (q \lor r)} \by{ $\lor I,10$}{}
            \close
            \have{12}{p \lor (q \lor r)} \by{ $\lor E,1$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt}
        \\

    \end{tabular}

    \begin{tabular}{>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}}

        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \have{1} {p \land (q \lor r)} \by {ass} {}
            \open
            \have{2} {p} \by {$\land E 1 $} {}
            \have{3} {q \lor r} \by {$\land E 1$} {}
            \open
            \have{4} {q} \by {$acp$} {}
            \have{5} {p \land q} \by {$\land E 2,4$} {}
            \have{6} {(p \land q) \lor (p \land r)} \by {$\lor I 5$} {}
            \close
            \open
            \have{7} {r} \by {$acp$} {}
            \have{8} {p \land r} \by {I\(\land\) 2,7} {}
            \have{9} {(p \land q) \lor (p \land r)} \by {$\lor I 8$} {}
            \close
            \have{9} {(p \land q) \lor (p \land r)} \by {$\lor I 8$} {}
            \close
            \have{10} {(p \land q) \lor (p \land r)} \by {$\lor E 3$} {}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt} &
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \have{1} {(p \to q) \land (r \to s)} \by {ass} {}
            \have{1} {(p \to q) } \by {$\land E 1$} {}
            \have{1} { (r \to s)} \by {$\land E 1$} {}
            \open
            \have{2} {p \lor r} \by {$acp$} {}
            \open
            \have{4} {p} \by {$acp$} {}
            \have{5} { q} \by {$\to E 2,5$} {}
            \have{6} {q \lor s} \by {$\lor I,6$} {}
            \close
            \open
            \have{7} {r} \by {$acp$} {}
            \have{8} {s} \by {$\to E 3,8$} {}
            \have{9} {q \lor s} \by {$\lor I 9$} {}
            \close
            \have{9} {q \lor s} \by {$\lor E,4$} {}
            \close
            \have{10} {(p \lor r) \to (q \lor s)} \by {$\to I $} {}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt}
        \\
    \end{tabular}

    \begin{definition}
        \textbf{$\bot$} : $\varphi  \land \neg \varphi$
    \end{definition}

    \begin{tabular}{|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|}
        \hline
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \open
            \have [ ] {1} {\varphi}
            \have [ ] {2} {\vdots}
            \have  [ ] {2} {\bot}
            \close
            \have  [ ]{2} {\therefore \neg\varphi } \by{$\neg I$ }{}
        \end{fitchproof}

        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt} &
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \open
            \have [ ] {1} {\neg\varphi}
            \have [ ] {2} {\vdots}
            \have  [ ] {2} {\bot}
            \close
            \have  [ ]{2} {\therefore \varphi } \by{$\neg E$ }{}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt}
        \\
        \hline
    \end{tabular}

    \begin{definition}
        \textbf{schema of prove} you've got knowed by now what is schema of a rule.
        if you want to prove a theorem that it's schema form.you can't prove it directly.
        here we use a technique calls \textbf{"schema of prove"}.
        it let us prove for all situations at the same time.
    \end{definition}

    \begin{theorem}
        \textbf{$(schema)$} $(\bot I , \bot E)$

        \begin{tabular}{|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|}
            \hline
            \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
                \hypo [ ]{1} {\varphi}
                \hypo [ ] {2} {\neg\varphi}
                \have  [ ]{2} {\therefore \bot} \by{$\bot I$}{}
            \end{fitchproof}
            \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt} &
            \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
                \hypo [ ] {2} {\bot}
                \have  [ ]{2} {\therefore \varphi} \by{$\bot E$}{}
            \end{fitchproof}
            \\
            \hline
        \end{tabular}

        \begin{tabular}{>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}}
            \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
                \have {1} {\varphi}                    \by{$ass$}{}
                \have {2} {\neg\varphi}             \by{$ass$}{}
                \have {1} {\varphi \land \neg\varphi } \by{$\land I$}{}
                \have  {2} { \bot}              \by{$def.3$}{}
            \end{fitchproof}
            \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt} &
            \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
                \have {2} {\bot} \by{$ass$}{}
                \open
                \have  {2} {\neg \varphi} \by{$acp$}{}
                \have  {2} { \vdots}
                \have  {2} { \bot} \by{$rp 1$}{}
                \close
                \have  {2} { \varphi} \by{$\neg E$}{}
            \end{fitchproof}
            \\
        \end{tabular}

    \end{theorem}

    \begin{Attention}
        \textbf{}Intuitionists can skip the ``$\neg E$'' rule that is the ``$RAA$'' rule in old notation
        and use ``$\bot E$'' as a rule instead.
    \end{Attention}

    \begin{example}
        \textbf{} for  ``$p,q,r$'' we can prove these.
        \begin{enumerate}
            \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
            \item  $p \rightarrow q, \neg q /\therefore \neg p$
            \item  $p \rightarrow q /\therefore \neg p \vee q$
        \end{enumerate}
    \end{example}

    \begin{tabular}{>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}}
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \have {} {p \to q} \by {$ass$} {}
            \have {} {\neg q} \by {$ass$} {}
            \open
            \have {} {p} \by {$acp$} {}
            \have {} {q} \by {$\to E,1,3$} {}
            \have {} {\bot} \by {$\bot I,2,4$} {}
            \close
            \have {} {\neg p} \by  {$\neg I$} {}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt} &
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \have{1}{p \to q} \by{$ass$}{}
            \open
            \have{2}{\neg(\neg p \lor q)} \by{$acp$}{}
            \open
            \have{3}{p} \by{$acp$}{}
            \have{4}{q} \by{$\to E,1,3$}{}
            \have{5}{\neg p \lor q} \by{$\lor I,4$}{}
            \have{6}{\bot} \by{$\bot I,2,5$}{}
            \close
            \have{7}{\neg p} \by{$\neg I$}{}
            \have{8}{\neg p \lor q} \by{$\lor I,7$}{}
            \have{9}{\bot} \by{$\bot I,2,8$}{}
            \close
            \have{10}{\neg p \lor q} \by{$\neg I$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt}
        \\
    \end{tabular}

\end{Rule}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{$\varphi \leftrightarrow \psi$} : $(\varphi \to \psi) \land (\psi \to \varphi)$
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}
    \textbf{$(schema)$} $(\leftrightarrow I , \leftrightarrow E)$

    \begin{tabular}{|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|}
        \hline
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \open
            \have [ ] {1} {\varphi}
            \have [ ] {2} {\vdots}
            \have  [ ] {2} {\psi}
            \close
            \open
            \have [ ] {1} {\psi}
            \have [ ] {2} {\vdots}
            \have  [ ] {2} {\varphi}
            \close
            \have  [ ]{2} {\therefore \varphi \leftrightarrow \psi} \by{$\leftrightarrow I$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt} &

        \begin{minipage}[t]{\linewidth}
            \centering
            \begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\linewidth}
                \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
                    \hypo [ ]{1} {\varphi \leftrightarrow \psi}
                    \hypo [ ] {2} {\varphi }
                    \have  [ ]{2} {\therefore \psi } \by{$\leftrightarrow E$}{}
                \end{fitchproof}
            \end{minipage}
            % \hfill
            \begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\linewidth}
                \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
                    \hypo [ ]{1} {\varphi \leftrightarrow \psi}
                    \hypo [ ] {2} {\psi}
                    \have  [ ]{2} {\therefore \varphi} \by{$\leftrightarrow E$}{}
                \end{fitchproof}
            \end{minipage}
        \end{minipage}

        \\
        \hline
    \end{tabular}

    \begin{tabular}{>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}}
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \open
            \have  {1} {\varphi}                                            \by{acp  }{}
            \have [ ] {2} {\vdots}
            \have  [n] {2} {\psi}
            \close
            \have   {2} {\varphi \to \psi }                              \by{$\to, I$ }{}
            \open
            \have {1} {\psi}                                            \by{acp  }{}
            \have [ ] {2} {\vdots}
            \have  [m] {2} {\varphi}
            \close
            \have   {2} {\psi \to \varphi }                              \by{$\to, I$}{}
            \have  {2} {(\varphi \to \psi)\land (\psi \to \varphi)}     \by{$\land I$}{}
            \have  {2} {\varphi \leftrightarrow \psi}                     \by{$def.,m+3$ }{}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt} &
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \have {1} {\varphi \leftrightarrow \psi }                   \by{ass}{}
            \have {2} {\varphi }                                        \by{ass}{}
            \have  {2} {(\varphi \to \psi)\land (\psi \to \varphi)}     \by{$def. 1$}{}
            \have  {2} {\varphi \to \psi }                              \by{$\land E 3$}{}
            \have  {2} {\psi}                                           \by{$\to E 1,4$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt}
        \\
    \end{tabular}

\end{theorem}

\begin{exercise}
    \textbf{} for  ``$p,q,r$'' prove.
    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item  $p /\therefore p$
        \item  $/\therefore p \vee \neg p$
        \item  $\neg p, p \vee q /\therefore q$
        \item  $\neg p /\therefore p \rightarrow q$
        \item  $p \rightarrow q \therefore /\therefore \neg q \rightarrow \neg p$
        \item  $(p \vee q) \wedge(p \vee r) \therefore /\therefore(p \vee(q \wedge r)) $
        \item  $\neg p \wedge \neg q \therefore /\therefore \neg(p \vee q)$ (De morgan)
        \item $\neg p \vee \neg q \therefore /\therefore \neg(p \wedge q)$  (De morgan)
        \item  $(\neg p \rightarrow q) /\therefore((p \rightarrow q) \rightarrow q)$
        \item  $/\therefore(p \wedge q \rightarrow r) \wedge(p \wedge s \rightarrow r) \rightarrow(p \wedge(q \vee s) \rightarrow r)$
        \item $/\therefore(p \wedge q \rightarrow r) \vee(p \wedge s \rightarrow r) \rightarrow(p \wedge(q \wedge s) \rightarrow r)$
        \item $/\therefore(p \rightarrow(q \rightarrow r)) \rightarrow((p \rightarrow q) \rightarrow(p \rightarrow r))$
        \item $/\therefore(p \rightarrow q) \rightarrow((q \rightarrow r) \rightarrow(p \rightarrow r))$
    \end{enumerate}
\end{exercise}

\begin{Attention}
    we can rewrite \textbf{Example} and \textbf{Exercise} propositions and
    proofs with proposition schemas and schema of proves.so they are all rules.
\end{Attention}

\chapter{first-order logic}

\section{what is formula?}

obj are two types.we know them and we know what exactly are we talking about
and unknowns are mystery.all the obj we have used until now were known ones we
call them \textbf{"constants"}.

\begin{undefined}
    \textbf{variables} A variable is a symbol
    (usually a letter like $x, y, or z$)
    that represents an unknown obj.
\end{undefined}

% \begin{definition}
%     \textbf{variables sets} A variable that is a set
%     (usually a letter like $X, Y, or Z$)
%     that represents an unknown set.
% \end{definition}
\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Atomic formula} we define atomic formulas like this.

    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item if ``$x$'' is an obj(constant or variable) then ``$Set(x)$'' is an atomic
              formula.
        \item if ``$x,y$'' are obj(constant or variable) then ``$x=y$'' is an atomic formula.
        \item if ``$x,y$'' are obj(constant or variable) then ``$x \in y$'' is an atomic
              formula.
    \end{enumerate}
    we denote atomic formulas with ``$A_{i}$''.
\end{definition}

%
\begin{definition}
    \textbf{well formed formula(WWF)} these are formulas.

    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item atomic formulas $(A_{i})$.
        \item if $A$ is a formula then $\neg A$ is a formula too.
        \item if $A ,B$ are two formulas then $A \bigcirc B $ is a formula too.($\bigcirc $
              can be $\to,\land ,\lor$).
    \end{enumerate}

\end{definition}
for example $(A_0 \to A_1 ,\neg A_3)$ \\ we easily call WWF ``formula'' and
also we denote WWF with $A ,B ,C ,...$.

%
\begin{definition}
    \textbf{open formula} formulas that variables used in one of its atomic formulas.
\end{definition}

the formulas that are not open call \textbf{``closed formulas''} or
\textbf{``sentence''}. Now question is ``are very closed formulas
proposition?'' (propositions in classic logic are sentences that are true or
false?) the answer is unfortunately \textbf{No}.

%
\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Substitution for obj} we define Substitution an obj with variable  like this.
    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item for variables if $x=y$ then $y[a/x]:=a$ and if $x \not= y$ then $y[a/x]:=y$.
        \item for constants $c[a/x]:=c$.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Substitution for formulas} we define Substitution for a formula like this.
    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item for atomic formulas we define it like this. if it's a single object we do it as
              we explain in ``\textbf{Substitution for obj}''. if it's like $x = y$ or $x \in
                  y$ ($x,y$ can be both constants and variables). we define it like this.
              \[ Set(y)[a/x] := Set(y[a/x]) \]
              \[ (x=y)[a/z] := (x[a/z] = y[a/z]) \]
              \[ (x \in y)[a/z] := (x[a/z] \in y[a/z]) \]
        \item if formula $A$ is complex we define it like this.
              \[ (B \bigcirc C)[a/x] := (B[a/x] \bigcirc C[a/x]) \]
              ($\bigcirc $ can be $\to,\land ,\lor$).
              \[ (\neg B)[a/x] := (\neg B[a/x]) \]
    \end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

simply means in a formula put an obj like $a$ instead of a variable like $x$.we
denote it by $A[a/x]$ or $A[\frac{a}{x}]$

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Open proposition} assume formula ``$A$'' with variables ``$x_0,x_1,..,x_n$'' in it and a set ``$U$''.
    we say formula ``$A$''is a ``open proposition in $U$'' when for every obj ``$a_0,a_1,..,a_n$'' in ``$U$''
    \[A([a_0/x_0][a_1/x_1]...[a_n/x_n]) \]
    is a proposition we call it open proposition and denote it with ``$(P(x)
        ,Q(x,y) ,..)$''

\end{definition}

%
\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Big ``AND'' and ``OR''} assume a set like ``$ \{a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},...,a_{n}\}$''
    we define \textbf{big ``AND''} like this.
    \\
    \[ \bigwedge\limits_{i=0}^n P(a_{i}) : P(a_{0}) \land P(a_{1}) \land P(a_{2}) \land... \land P(a_{n})\]
    \\
    and \textbf{big ``OR''}
    \\
    \[ \bigvee\limits_{i=0}^n P(a_{i}) : P(a_{0}) \lor P(a_{1}) \lor P(a_{2}) \lor... \lor P(a_{n})\]

\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Universe of discourse} we specify a set like ``$ \{a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},...,a_{n}\}$''
    as universe of a Logic world.

\end{definition}

\begin{Rule}
    \textbf{use of open propositions} assume universe of discourse is ``$ \{a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},...,a_{n}\}$''
    \\
    \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
        \have [c] {1} {P(x)}
    \end{fitchproof}
    means :
    \\
    \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
        \have [c_{0}] {1} {P[a_{0}/x]}
        \have [c_{1}] {1} {P[a_{1}/x]}
        \have [c_{2}] {1} {P[a_{2}/x]}
        \have [ ] {2} {\vdots}
        \have [c_{n}] {1} {P[a_{n}/x]}
    \end{fitchproof}
    \par
    if $Px$ was been assemed then like this
    \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
        \open
        \have [c] {1} {P(x)}
        \have [ ] {2} {\vdots}
        \close
    \end{fitchproof}
    means :
    \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
        \open
        \have [c_{0}] {1} {P[a_{0}/x]}
        \have [ ] {2} {\vdots}
        \close
        \open
        \have [c_{1}] {1} {P[a_{1}/x]}
        \have [ ] {2} {\vdots}
        \close
        \open
        \have [c_{2}] {1} {P[a_{2}/x]}
        \have [ ] {2} {\vdots}
        \close
        \open
        \have [c_{n}] {1} {P[a_{n}/x]}
        \have [ ] {2} {\vdots}
        \close
    \end{fitchproof}
\end{Rule}

%

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{predicate schema(variable predicate)} a predicate that is unknown
    for us and it works as variable.(we show them with $\phi ,\psi ,\theta ,... $)
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Universal quantification} assume universe of discourse is ``$ \{a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},...,a_{n}\}$''
    we define ``$\forall x P(x)$''
    \\
    \[ \forall x \phi(x) : \bigwedge\limits_{i=0}^n \phi[a_{i}/x] \]
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Existential quantification} assume universe of discourse is ``$ \{a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},...,a_{n}\}$''
    we define ``$\exists x Px$''
    \\
    \[ \exists  x \phi(x) : \bigvee\limits_{i=0}^n \phi[a_{i}/x] \]
\end{definition}

as you can see ``$\forall x \phi (x) $'' and ``$\exists x \phi (x) $'' are
sentences.

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Free variables} variables that are not bound by a quantifier.
    (you can easily see that every formula with no free variables are sentences)
    (previously we sayed that every sentence is not necessary a proposition)
\end{definition}

\section{Rules of first order logic.}

\begin{theorem}
     \textbf{**$(schema)$**} $(\forall I,\forall E,\exists I,\exists E)$

    Assume that the universe of discourse is
    \[
        D=\{a_0,a_1,a_2,\ldots,a_n\}.
    \]

    \begin{tabular}{|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|}

        \hline

        Rules
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt} &
        Terms and Conditions
        \\

        \hline

        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \hypo [ ] {2} {\phi(x)}
            \have [ ] {2} {\therefore \forall x\,\phi(x)}
                \by{$\forall I$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt} &

        \begin{enumerate}
            \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt}
            \item $x$ is a variable.
            \item $x$ is arbitrary.
            \item $x$ does not occur free in any 
            assumption on which $\phi(x)$ depends.
        \end{enumerate}

        \\

        \hline

        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \hypo [ ] {2} {\forall x\,\phi(x)}
            \have [ ] {2} {\therefore \phi[a/x]}
                \by{$\forall E$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt} &

        \begin{enumerate}
            \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt}
            \item $a$ is an object in the universe of discourse.
            \item $a$ is free for $x$ in $\phi(x)$.
        \end{enumerate}

        \\

        \hline

        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \hypo [ ] {2} {\phi[a/x]}
            \have [ ] {2} {\therefore \exists x\,\phi(x)}
                \by{$\exists I$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt} &

        \begin{enumerate}
            \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt}
            \item $a$ is an object in the universe of discourse.
            \item $a$ is free for $x$ in $\phi(x)$.
        \end{enumerate}

        \\

        \hline

        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \have [ ] {1} {\exists x\,\phi(x)}
            \open
            \have [ ] {1} {\phi(x)}
            \have [ ] {2} {\vdots}
            \have [ ] {2} {\theta}
            \close
            \have [ ] {2} {\therefore\theta}
                \by{$\exists E$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt} &

        \begin{enumerate}
            \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt}
            \item $x$ does not occur free in any assumption on which $\theta$ depends.
            \item $x$ does not occur free in $\theta$.
            \item $\phi(x)$ is assumed only inside the subproof.
        \end{enumerate}

        \\

        \hline

    \end{tabular}
    % \printindex

    \begin{tabular}{>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}}
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \have  {2} {\phi (x)}                                         \by{$ass$  }{}
            \have  {2} {\phi [a_{0}/x]}                                     \by{$def.1$ }{}
            \have [ ] {2} {\vdots}
            \have [n] {2} {\phi [a_{n}/x]}                                       \by{$def.1$}{}
            \have {2} {\bigwedge\limits_{i=0}^n \phi [a_{i}/x] }          \by{$\land I,2,3,...,n+2$}{}
            \have  {2} { \forall x \phi (x)}                  \by{$def. n+3$ }{}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt} &
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \have  {2} {\forall x \phi (x)}                         \by{$ass$ }{}
            \have  {2} {\bigwedge\limits_{i=0}^n \phi [a_{i}/x]}      \by{$def.1$}{}
            \have  {2} { \phi [a/x]}                         \by{$\land E 2$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}

        \\

        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \have  {2} {\phi [a/x]}
            \have  {2} {\bigvee\limits_{i=0}^n \phi [a_{i}/x]}   \by{$\lor I 1$}{}
            \have  {2} { \exists x \phi (x)}         \by{$def 2$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}

        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt} &

        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \have  {1} {\exists x \phi (x)}
            \have  {1} {\bigvee\limits_{i=0}^n \phi [a_{i}/x] }
            \open
            \have  {1} {\phi [a_{0}/x]}                  \by{acp  }{}
            \have [ ] {2} {\vdots}
            \have  [n] {2} {\theta}
            \close
            \open
            \have  {1} {\phi [a_{1}/x]}                \by{acp  }{}
            \have [ ] {2} {\vdots}
            \have  [m] {2} {\theta}
            \close
            \have [ ] {2} {\vdots}
            \open
            \have [l] {1} {\phi [a_{2}/x]}                    \by{acp  }{}
            \have [ ] {2} {\vdots}
            \have  [r] {2} {\theta}
            \close
            \have  {2} {\theta }                    \by{$\exists E,3$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}

    \end{tabular}

\end{theorem}

\begin{Rule}
    \textbf{$(schema)$} $( = I , = E)$

    \begin{tabular}{|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|}
        \hline
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \have  [ ]{2} {\therefore a = a } \by{$ = I $}{}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt} &
        \begin{minipage}[t]{\linewidth}
            \centering
            \begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\linewidth}
                \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
                    \hypo [ ]{1} {a = b }
                    \hypo [ ] {2} {\phi(a) }
                    \have  [ ]{2} {\therefore  \phi(b) } \by{$ = E $}{}
                \end{fitchproof}
            \end{minipage}
            % \hfill
            \begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\linewidth}
                \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
                    \hypo [ ]{1} {a = b }
                    \hypo [ ] {2} { \phi(a)}
                    \have  [ ]{2} { \therefore  \phi(a)} \by{$ = E$}{}
                \end{fitchproof}
            \end{minipage}
        \end{minipage}
        \\
        \hline
    \end{tabular}

\end{Rule}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Uniqueness}
    we define ``$\exists ! x \phi(x)$''
    \\
    \[ \exists !  x \phi(x) : \exists   x (\phi(x) \land \forall y(\phi(y) \to x=y)) \]
\end{definition}

\begin{example}
    \textbf{}for P,Q,R we can prove these.Proof of last ones Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.\twemoji{1f600}

    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items

        \item   $ \forall x(P(x) \wedge Q(x)) \therefore / \therefore \forall x P(x) \wedge \forall x Q(x) $ (D F $\forall \land$)(Distribution and Factorization of $\forall \land$)
        \item   $ \exists x(P(x) \vee Q(x)) \therefore /\therefore \exists x P(x) \vee \exists x Q(x) $ (D F $\exists \lor$)
        \item   $ \forall x P(x) \vee \forall x Q(x) /\therefore \forall x(P(x) \vee Q(x)) $   (F $\forall \lor$)
        \item   $ \exists x(P(x) \wedge Q(x)) /\therefore \exists x P(x) \wedge \exists x Q(x) $   (D $\exists \land$)
        \item   $ \forall x(P(x) \rightarrow Q(x)) /\therefore  \forall x P(x) \rightarrow \forall x Q(x) $  (D $\forall \to$)
        \item   $\exists x P(x) \rightarrow \exists x Q(x) /\therefore \exists x(P(x) \rightarrow Q(x)) $  (F $\exists \to$)

    \end{enumerate}

    \begin{tabular}{>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}}

            \have {} {Q(x)} \by {$\land E$} {}

            \have {} {\forall x  Q(x)} \by {$\forall I$} {}

\begin{exercise}
    \textbf{}for P,Q,R prove.
    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item   $ \forall x P(x) \wedge Q \therefore /\therefore \forall x(P(x) \wedge Q) $
        \item   $ \forall x P(x) \vee Q \therefore /\therefore \forall x(P(x) \vee Q) $
        \item   $ \exists x P(x) \wedge Q \therefore /\therefore \exists x(P(x) \wedge Q) $
        \item   $ \exists x P(x) \vee Q \therefore /\therefore \exists x(P(x) \vee Q) $
        \item   $Q \rightarrow \forall x P(x) \therefore /\therefore \forall x(Q \rightarrow P(x)) $
        \item   $Q \rightarrow \exists x P(x) \therefore /\therefore \exists x(Q \rightarrow P(x)) $
        \item   $\forall x P(x) \rightarrow Q \therefore /\therefore \exists x(P(x) \rightarrow Q) $
        \item   $\exists x P(x) \rightarrow Q \therefore /\therefore \forall x(P(x) \rightarrow Q) $
        \item   $ \exists x \neg P(x) \therefore /\therefore \neg \forall x P(x)  $  (De morgan)
        \item   $ \forall x \neg P(x) \therefore /\therefore \neg \exists x P(x) $ (De morgan)
        \item   $ \exists x \Big(P(x) \wedge \exists y Q(y) \rightarrow \forall z R(z)\Big) /\therefore \forall y \forall z \Big(\forall x P(x) \wedge Q(y) \rightarrow R(z)\Big) $
        \item   $ \forall x \exists y \Big(P(x) \wedge Q(y) \rightarrow \forall z R(z)\Big) /\therefore \forall z \Big(\exists x P(x) \wedge \forall y Q(y) \rightarrow R(z)\Big) $
        \item   $\forall x P(x) \rightarrow \exists y Q(y) /\therefore \exists x \exists y \Big(P(x) \rightarrow Q(y)\Big) $
        \item   $ \forall x \forall z \exists y \exists w \Big(P(y,z) \vee Q(z) \rightarrow R(x,w)\Big) /\therefore \Big(\exists z \forall y P(y,z) \vee \exists z Q(z)\Big) \rightarrow \forall x \exists w R(x,w) $
        \item   $ \exists x P(x) \vee \exists x \Big(Q(x) \wedge R(x) \Big) /\therefore \exists x \Big(P(x) \vee Q(x) \Big) \wedge \exists x \Big(P(x) \vee R(x)\Big) $
        \item   $\exists x \exists z \forall y \Big(P(x,y) \rightarrow Q(z) \vee R(x,y) \Big) /\therefore \forall x \exists y P(x,y) \rightarrow \Big(\exists z Q(z) \vee \exists x \exists z R(x,y)\Big) $
        \item   $ \forall x \exists y \Big(\exists z P(x,y,z) \wedge Q(x,y)\Big) \vee \forall x \exists y \exists z \Big(P(x,y,z) \wedge R(x,y)\Big) /\therefore \forall x \exists y \exists z \Big(P(x,y,z) \wedge(Q(x,y) \vee R(x,y))\Big) $
    \end{enumerate}
\end{exercise}

\begin{Attention}
    we can rewrite \textbf{Example} and \textbf{Exercise} propositions and
    proofs with proposition schemas and schema of proves.so they are all rules.
\end{Attention}

\chapter{The beginning of set theory}

In language of set theory if we are talking about ``$\forall X (P(X))$'' it
means ``$\forall x (Set(x) \to P(x))$'' and when we are talking about
``$\exists X (P(X))$'' it means ``$\exists x (Set(x) \land P(x))$''. similarly
if we are talking about ``$\forall x \in A \quad P(x)$'' it means ``$\forall x
    (x \in A \to P(x))$'' and when we are talking about ``$\exists x \in A \quad
    P(x)$'' it means ``$\exists x (x \in A \land P(x))$''.

and we have a definitions.
\[ Type_0(X) : \exists Y \quad X \in Y \]

\[ Type_1(X) : \neg \exists Y \quad X \in Y \]

\section{Axioms of set theory}
we emphasis before that set has no definition.but we can describe what set is
with rules and axioms.

\begin{axiom}
    \textbf{Existence of universe of set theory}
    There exists a set $V_2$ (we denote it as $V_2$.it simply means the set of all sets), known as the Von Neumann Universe, which contains every set.
    (this axiom can't be formolized in first order logic).
    (there must be another axiom let us use logic for set theory but we ignore that).
    (from now on when we are talking about proposition ``$\phi$'' in ``set theory'' we mean
    proposition ``$\phi$'' in ``$V_2$'').
\end{axiom}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Subset} a subset is a set where every element is also an element of another, larger set called the superset
    \[ A \subseteq B: \forall x(x \in A \rightarrow x \in B) \]
    or i.e.
    \[ \forall x \in A \quad x \in B \]
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}
    \textbf{$(schema)$} $(\subseteq I , \subseteq E)$

    \begin{tabular}{|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|}
        \hline
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \open
            \have [ ] {1} {x \in A}
            \have [ ] {2} {\vdots}
            \have  [ ] {2} {x \in B}
            \close
            \have  [ ]{2} {\therefore A \subseteq B  } \by{$\subseteq I$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}

        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt} &
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \hypo [ ]{1} {A \subseteq B}
            \hypo [ ] {2} {a \in A}
            \have  [ ]{2} {\therefore  a \in B} \by{$\subseteq E$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt}
        \\
        \hline
    \end{tabular}

    \begin{tabular}{>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}}
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \open
            \have {1} {x \in A}                                         \by{acp}{}
            \have [ ] {2} {\vdots}
            \have  [n] {2} {x \in B}
            \close
            \have  {2} {x \in A \rightarrow x \in B }              \by{$I\rightarrow,1,n$ }{}
            \have  {2} {\forall x(x \in A \rightarrow x \in B )}   \by{$\forall I,n+1$ }{}
            \have  {2} {A \subseteq B  }                           \by{$def.n+2$ }{}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt} &
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \have {1} {A \subseteq B}                               \by{ass }{}
            \have  {2} {a \in A}                                   \by{ass }{}
            \have  {2} {\forall x(x \in A \rightarrow x \in A )}   \by{$def.1$ }{}
            \have  {2} {a \in A \rightarrow a \in B }             \by{$\forall E,4$ }{}
            \have  {2} {  a \in B}                                   \by{$E\rightarrow 3,5$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt}
        \\
    \end{tabular}

\end{theorem}

\begin{example}
    \textbf{} $A \subseteq B , B \subseteq C/\therefore A \subseteq C$
    \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
        \have{1} {A \subseteq B }                 \by{ass}{}
        \have {2} {B \subseteq C}                  \by{ass}{}
        \open
        \have   {2} {x \in A }                     \by{$acp$}{}
        \have   {2} {x \in B }                     \by{$\subseteq E ,1,3$ }{}
        \have {1} {x \in C  }                       \by{$\subseteq E ,2,4$}{}
        \close
        \have  {2} {A \subseteq C }             \by{$\subseteq I ,3,5$}{}
    \end{fitchproof}
\end{example}

\begin{axiom}
    \textbf{Extensionality}Two sets are equal (are the same set) if they have the same elements.
    \\
    \[  \forall X,Y (\forall x ( x \in X \leftrightarrow x \in Y ) \rightarrow X = Y )  \]
\end{axiom}

so we can rewrite axiom of Extensionality like this.

\[ \forall X,Y ((X \subseteq Y \wedge Y \subseteq X) \rightarrow X=Y)\]

\begin{theorem}
    \textbf{$(schema)$} $( = I,  = E)$

    \begin{tabular}{|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|}
        \hline
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \open
            \have [ ] {1} {x \in A }
            \have [ ] {2} {\vdots}
            \have  [ ] {2} {x \in B}
            \close
            \open
            \have [ ] {1} {x \in B}
            \have [ ] {2} {\vdots}
            \have  [ ] {2} {x \in A}
            \close
            \have  [ ]{2} {\therefore A = B } \by{$ = I$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt} &
        \begin{minipage}[t]{\linewidth}
            \centering
            \begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\linewidth}
                \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
                    \hypo [ ]{1} {A = B }
                    \hypo [ ] {2} {a \in A }
                    \have  [ ]{2} {\therefore  a \in B } \by{$ = E$}{}
                \end{fitchproof}
            \end{minipage}
            % \hfill
            \begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\linewidth}
                \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]

                    \hypo [ ] {2} { a \in B}
                    \have  [ ]{2} { \therefore  a \in A} \by{$ = E$}{}
                \end{fitchproof}
            \end{minipage}
        \end{minipage}
        \\
        \hline
    \end{tabular}

    \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
        \open
        \have  {1} {x \in A }                                                                 \by{acp  }{}
        \have [ ] {2} {\vdots}
        \have  [n] {2} {x \in B}
        \close
        \have   {2} {A \subseteq B }                                                        \by{$\subseteq I,1,n$  }{}
        \open
        \have  {1} {x \in B}                                                                \by{acp }{}
        \have [ ] {2} {\vdots}
        \have  [m] {2} {x \in A}
        \close
        \have  {2} {B \subseteq A }                                                          \by{$\subseteq I,n+2,m$ }{}
        \have  {2} {\forall X, Y((X \subseteq Y \wedge Y \subseteq X) \rightarrow X=Y)}   \by{Extensionality }{}
        \have  {2} {(A \subseteq B \wedge B \subseteq A) \rightarrow A=B}                 \by{$\forall E,m+2$ }{}
        \have  {2} {A \subseteq B \wedge B \subseteq A}                                       \by{$\land I,n+1,m+1$}{}
        \have  {2} {A=B}                                                                        \by{$E\rightarrow,m+3,m+4$}{}
    \end{fitchproof}
    \par

\end{theorem}

\begin{axiom}
    \textbf{Weak comprehension} $(schema)$ this axioms says for every open proposition
    like ``$\phi (x,x_0 , x_1, ... , x_n)$'' there is a set like ``$X$'' such that every ``$x$'' with ``$Type_0$'' is a
    member of that set if and only if it holds
    ``$\phi (x,x_0 , x_1, ... , x_n)$''.
    \\
    \[ \forall x_0 , x_1, ... , x_n \exists X \forall x  (Type_0(x) \land \phi (x,x_0 , x_1, ... , x_n) \leftrightarrow x \in X) \]
    \\
    by axiom of extentionality we can show that $X$ is unique.
    so we denote it as ``$\{ x \mid  \phi (x,x_0 , x_1, ... , x_n)\}$''.
    sometimes instead of ``$\{ x \mid x \in A \land \phi (x,x_0 , x_1, ... , x_n)\}$'' we write
    ``$\{ x \in A \mid  \phi (x,x_0 , x_1, ... , x_n)\}$''.
\end{axiom}

% this axiom show that why Russell's paradox doesn't happened here because if ou
% assume ``$R=\{x|x \notin x\}$'' is a set. you can't prove or disprove ``$R \in
%     R$'' so ``$x \notin x$'' is not a proposition in ``set theorhy''. (we don't
% need to prove that now.we will prove it in``\textbf{'MetaLogic'}''). (it's not
% obvios in ``$x \notin x$'' witch set holds and witch not). (and likely ``$\{ X
%     \mid a \in X \}$'' is not a set either).

% \section{definitions of set theory}
% Let's talk about some definitions in set theoty.These definitions will come in
% handy later.

Let's talk about some axioms in set theoty.These daxioms will come in handy
later.

\begin{axiom}
    \textbf{Existence} there is a ``Set'' with no member i.e..
    \[ \exists x \quad Type_0(x) \]
\end{axiom}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Empty set} also known as the null set,
    is the unique mathematical set containing no elements at all.
    (uniqueness can be proven.)
    \[ \emptyset:=\{x \mid \bot\} \]
    therefore.
    \[ \forall x(x \in \emptyset \leftrightarrow  \bot \land Type_0(x) ) \]
    therefore.
    \[ \forall x(x \notin \emptyset ) \]
\end{definition}

\begin{example}
    \textbf{} $/\therefore \forall X(\emptyset \subseteq X)$
    \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
        \open
        \have{1} {x \in \emptyset}                                \by{acp}{}
        \have {2} {\forall x(x \notin \emptyset)}                  \by{$def$ }{}
        \have   {2} {x \notin \emptyset}                           \by{$\forall E ,2$}{}
        \have   {2} {\bot}                                          \by{$\bot I ,1,3$ }{}
        \have {1} {x \in X  }                                        \by{$\bot E ,4$}{}
        \close
        \have  {2} {\emptyset \subseteq X}                       \by{$\subseteq I ,1,5$}{}
        \have  {2} {\forall X(\emptyset \subseteq X)}             \by{$\forall I ,6$}{}
    \end{fitchproof}
\end{example}

\begin{lemma}
    \textbf{} $A = \emptyset \therefore / \therefore \forall y( y \notin A)$
    \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
        \have  {1} {\forall y (y \notin A )}                                \by{ass}{}
        \open
        \have  {1} {y \in A }                                               \by{acp}{}
        \have  {1} {y \notin A }                                            \by{$\forall E,1$}{}
        \have  {2} {\bot}                                                  \by{$\bot I,2,3$}{}
        \have   {2} {y \in \emptyset }                                       \by{$\bot E,4$ }{}
        \close
        \open
        \have   {2} {y \in \emptyset }                                      \by{acp}{}
        \have  {1} {\forall y (y \notin \emptyset  )}                        \by{def.}{}
        \have {2} {y \notin \emptyset  }                                    \by{$\forall E,7$  }{}
        \have   {2} {\bot}                                                 \by{$\bot I,6,8$ }{}
        \have   {2} {y \in A}                                                 \by{$\bot E,9$}{}
        \close
        \have  {2} {A = \emptyset }                                                    \by{$I=$}{}
    \end{fitchproof}

    \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
        \have  {2} {A = \emptyset }                                                    \by{ass}{}
        \have  {1} {\forall y (y \notin \emptyset )}                                \by{def.}{}
        \have  {1} {\forall y (y \notin A )}                                \by{$= E,1,2$}{}
    \end{fitchproof}
\end{lemma}

\begin{theorem}
    \textbf{$(schema)$} $( \emptyset\not= I ,  \emptyset \not= E ,\emptyset= I , \emptyset= E)$

    \begin{tabular}{|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|}
        \hline
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \hypo [ ] {2} {a \in A}
            \have  [ ]{2} {\therefore A \not= \emptyset} \by{$\emptyset\not=  I$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt} &
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \have [ ] {1} {A \not= \emptyset }
            \open
            \have [ ] {1} {x \in A}
            \have [ ] {2} {\vdots}
            \have  [ ] {2} {\theta}
            \close
            \have  [ ]{2} {\therefore\theta } \by{$\emptyset \not= E $}{}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt}
        \\
        % \hline
        % \end{tabular}
        % \begin{tabular}{|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|}
        \hline
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \open
            \have [ ] {1} {x \in A}
            \have [ ] {2} {\vdots}
            \have  [ ] {2} {\bot }
            \close
            \have  [ ]{2} {\therefore A = \emptyset } \by{$\emptyset= I$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt} &
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \hypo [ ] {2} {A = \emptyset }
            \have  [ ]{2} {\therefore a \notin A} \by{$\emptyset= E$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt}
        \\
        \hline
    \end{tabular}

    \begin{tabular}{>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}}
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \have  {2} {a \in A}                         \by{ass}{}
            \have  {2} {\exists x(x \in A)}             \by{$\exists I,1$ }{}
            \have  {2} {\neg (\forall x(x \notin A))}   \by{De Morgan $2$ }{}
            \have  {2} { A \not= \emptyset}             \by{lemma $4.4.1 , 3$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}

        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt} &
        \\
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \open
            \have{1} {x \in A}                           \by{acp}{}
            \have [ ] {2} {\vdots}
            \have  [n] {2} {\bot}
            \close
            \have {1} {x \notin A  }             \by{$\neg I $ }{}
            \have  {2} {\forall x(x \notin A)}   \by{$\forall I ,n+1$}{}
            \have  {2} {A = \emptyset }             \by{lemma $4.4.1 , 1$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}

        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt} &
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \have  {2} { A = \emptyset}             \by{ass}{}
            \have  {2} {\forall x(x \notin A)}   \by{lemma $4.4.1 , 1$}{}
            \have  {2} {a \notin A}               \by{$\forall E 2$  }{}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt}
        \\

    \end{tabular}
\end{theorem}

\begin{axiom}
    \textbf{Existence}
    \[  Type_0(\emptyset) \]
\end{axiom}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Pairing set}
    \[ \{a_{0}, a_{1}, a_{2}, ... ,a_{n} \}:=\{x \mid x=a_{0} \lor x=a_{1} \lor x=a_{2} \lor ... \lor x=a_{n} \} \]
    therefore.
    \[\forall x( x \in \{a_{0}, a_{1}, a_{2}, ... ,a_{n} \} \leftrightarrow  ( Type_0(x) \land x=a_{0} \lor x=a_{1} \lor x=a_{2} \lor ... \lor x=a_{n} )) \]
\end{definition}

        \end{fitchproof}

\begin{axiom}
    \textbf{Pairing}  $(schema)$
    \[  Type_0(\{a_{0}, a_{1}, a_{2}, ... ,a_{n} \}) \]
\end{axiom}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Union and Intersection} \\
    Intersection:
    \[ A \cap B:=\{x \mid x \in A \wedge x \in B \} \]
    therefore.
    \[\forall x( x \in A \cap B  \leftrightarrow  (x \in A \wedge x \in B )) \]
    Union:
    \[ A \cup B:=\{x \mid x \in A \lor x \in B \} \]
    therefore.
    \[\forall x( x \in A \cup B  \leftrightarrow  (x \in A \lor x \in B )) \]
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}
    \textbf{$(schema)$} $(\cap I , \cap E , \cup I , \cup E)$

        \end{fitchproof}

        \end{fitchproof}

        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \hypo [ ] {2} {a \in A   }
            \have  [ ]{2} { \therefore  a \in  B \cup A } \by{$\cup I$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}

        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt} &

sometimes all members of a set is also set we call it ``set of sets''.

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Big Union and Intersection} if ``$A$'' is ``set of sets'' and ``$A \not= \emptyset$'' we define
    \\
    Big Intersection:
    \[  \bigcap A:=\{x \mid \forall X (X \in A \rightarrow x \in X) \} \]
    i.e.
    \[  \bigcap A:=\{x \mid \forall X \in A \quad x \in X \} \]
    therefore.
    \[\forall x \Big( x \in \bigcap A  \leftrightarrow (Type_0(x) \land   \forall X (X \in A \rightarrow x \in X))\Big) \]
    Big Union:
    \[  \bigcup A:=\{x \mid \exists X (X \in A \wedge x \in X) \} \]
    i.e.
    \[  \bigcup A:=\{x \mid \exists X \in A \quad x \in X \} \]
    therefore.
    \[\forall x \Big( x \in \bigcup A \leftrightarrow  ( Type_0(x) \land  \exists X (X \in A \wedge x \in X))\Big) \]
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}
    \textbf{$(schema)$} $(\bigcap I , \bigcap E , \bigcup I , \bigcup E)$

    \begin{tabular}{|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|}
        \hline
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \open
            \have [ ] {1} {X \in A }
            \have [ ] {2} {\vdots}
            \have  [ ] {2} {a \in X}
            \close
            \have  [ ]{2} {\therefore a \in \bigcap A } \by{$\bigcap I$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt}                       &
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \hypo [ ] {2} {a \in \bigcap A }
            \hypo [ ] {2} {B \in  A  }
            \have  [ ]{2} {\therefore  a \in  B} \by{$\bigcap E$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt}
        \\
        \hline
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \hypo [ ] {2} {a \in B  }
            \hypo [ ] {2} {B \in A  }
            \have  [ ]{2} {\therefore  a \in \bigcup A } \by{$\bigcup I$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt}  \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt} &
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \have [ ] {1} { a \in \bigcup A  }
            \open
            \have [ ] {1} {a \in X  }
            \have [ ] {1} { X \in A }
            \have [ ] {2} {\vdots}
            \have  [ ] {2} {\theta}
            \close
            \have  [ ]{2} {\therefore\theta } \by{$\bigcup E$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt}
        \\
        \hline
    \end{tabular}
    \\
    \\
    \\
    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.\twemoji{1f600}
\end{theorem}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Complement}
    \[   A^c:=\{x \mid x \notin A  \} \]
    therefore.
    \[\forall x( x \in A^c \leftrightarrow Type_0(x) \land  x \notin A ) \]
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}
    \textbf{$(schema)$} $(()^c I, ()^c E)$

        \end{fitchproof}

            \have [ ] {2} {\vdots}

\begin{observation}
    \textbf{}we can show Universal set with...
    \[   V=\{x \mid \top \} \]
    therefore.
    \[\forall x( x \in V \leftrightarrow Type_0(x) \land  \top ) \]
    therefore.
    \[\forall x( x \in V ) \]
\end{observation}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Subtraction}
    \[ A - B:=\{x \mid x \in A \wedge x \notin B \} \]
    therefore.
    \[\forall x \Big( x \in A - B  \leftrightarrow  ( Type_0(x) \land  x \in A \wedge x \notin B ) \Big) \]
\end{definition}

    \textbf{} $/ \therefore  A - B = A \cap B^c$
    \\
    \\
    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.\twemoji{1f600}

\begin{axiom}
    \textbf{Separation}
    \[ \forall X,Y \quad  Type_0(X) \to Type_0(X \cap Y) \]
    therefore.
    \[ Type_0(A) \to  Type_0(A \cap B) \]
    therefore.
    \[  Type_0(\bigcap A) \]
    therefore.
    \[ Type_0(A) \to  Type_0(A - B) \]
\end{axiom}

\begin{axiom}
    \textbf{union}
    \[ \forall X \quad  Type_0(X) \to Type_0(\bigcup X) \]
    therefore.
    \[ Type_0(A) \land Type_0(B)  \to  Type_0(A \cup B) \]
\end{axiom}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Powerset}
    \[ \mathcal{P}(A):=\{X \mid X \subseteq A \} \]
    therefore.
    \[\forall X \Big( X \in \mathcal{P}(A) \leftrightarrow  ( Type_0(X) \land  X \subseteq A )\Big) \]
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}
    \textbf{$(schema)$} $(\mathcal{P} I, \mathcal{P} E )$

    \begin{tabular}{|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|}
        \hline
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \hypo [ ] {2} { A \subseteq B }
            \have  [ ]{2} {\therefore   A \in  \mathcal{P}(B) } \by{$\mathcal{P} I$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt}  \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt} &
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \hypo [ ] {2} {A \in  \mathcal{P}(B) }
            \have  [ ]{2} {\therefore  A \subseteq B } \by{$\mathcal{P} E$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt}
        \\
        \hline
    \end{tabular}
    \\
    \\
    \\
    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.\twemoji{1f600}
\end{theorem}

\begin{axiom}
    \textbf{Powerset}
    \[ \forall X \quad  Type_0(X) \to Type_0(P(X)) \]
\end{axiom}

\chapter{Relations and Functions}

\section{Ordered pair and Cartesian product}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Ordered pair}
    \[ (x,y) := \{ \{x\},\{x,y\} \}. \]
\end{definition}

so $\forall x,y \quad Type_0((x,y))$

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Tuples}(n is a arbitrary number.)(this is a temporary definition, we will give a better one in the next chapter.)
    \[ (x_1,x_2,...,x_n) := ((x_1,x_2,...,x_{n-1}),x_n) \]
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}
    \textbf{} $/ \therefore  (x_0,y_0) = (x_1,y_1) \leftrightarrow ((x_0=x_1) \wedge (y_0=y_1))$
    \\
    \\
    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.\twemoji{1f600}
\end{lemma}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Cartesian product}
    \[ A \times B := \{ u \mid \exists x,y \Big((x,y)=u \land x \in A \land y\in B \Big)\}. \]
    or i.e.
    \[ A \times B := \{ (x,y) \mid x \in A \land y\in B \}. \]
\end{definition}

\begin{example}
    if
    $ A= \{ a,b,c \}$
    and
    $ B= \{ 0,1,2,3 \} $
    then
    \[ A \times B = \{ (a,0),(a,1),(a,2),(a,3),(b,0),(b,1),(b,2),(b,3),(c,0),(c,1),(c,2),(c,3) \} \]

            \hypo [ ] {2} {s \in B }
            \have  [ ]{2} {\therefore   (t,s) \in A \times B } \by{$\times I$}{}

            \have  [ ]{2} {\therefore   t \in A \times B }
            \open
            \have  [ ]{2} {   t = (x,y) }
            \have [ ] {2} {x \in A }
            \have [ ] {2} {y \in B }
            \have [ ] {2} { \vdots }
            \have [ ] {2} { \theta }
            \close
            \have [ ] {2} { \theta }     \by{$\times E$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt}

    \end{tabular}

    \\

\begin{exercise}
    \textbf{} for sets A, B, C, prove.
    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item  $/\therefore A \times (B \cap C) = (A \times B)\cap (A \times C)$
        \item  $/\therefore A \times (B \cup C) = (A \times B) \cup (A \times C)$
        \item  $ /\therefore A \times (B-C) = (A \times B) - (A \times C)$
    \end{enumerate}
\end{exercise}

\section{Relations}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Relation} we call a set like \textbf{R} a \textbf{Relation} when there are
    two sets like $X$ and $Y$ s.t. $R \subseteq X \times Y$.
    \[ Rel(R) : \exists X,Y (R \subseteq X \times Y). \]
\end{definition}

sometimes we use different notations in this book. you have to be smart and
recognize it by yourself. sometimes instead of ``$x \in R$'' we denote it by
``$R(x) $''. and instead of ``$(x_0, x_1 , ... , x_n) \in R$'' we denote it by
``$R(x_0, x_1 , ... , x_n) $''. if tuple is binary, instead of ``$(x,y) \in
    R$'' we denote it by ``$xRy $''. or ``$R(x,y)$'' instead of ``$R \big( (x,y)
    \big)$'' \\ so if we have a definition like ``$ R(x) : \phi(x)$'' s.t.
``$\phi(x)$'' is a first order proposition. ``weak comprehension'' show us
there is a straight relatioon between ``propositions'' and ``sets'' so you can
see it as ``$ R := \{ x \mid \phi(x) \}$''. and sometimes we might show a
formula in natural language for example below instead of ``$Rel(X)$'' we can
say ``$ X $ is relation''. if it is confusing so forget it.\twemoji{1f600}

% \begin{definition}
%     \textbf{structure} (this is a temporary definition, we will give a better one in the next chapter.)
%     ``$\mathfrak{M} $'' is a tuple defined like this.
%     \[\mathfrak{M}  := (M ,R_{0}, R_{1} ,...,R_{n}, c_{0}, c_{1} ,...,c_{m}) \]
%     ``$M$'' is a set shows our universe  and ``$R_i$'' are relations and ``$c_i$'' are constants.

% \end{definition}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{ِDomain and Range(also called Image)} \\
    Domain of a set ``$R$'' is all ``$x$'' that there exists a ``$y$'' s.t. ``$xRy$''
    \[ Dom (R) := \{ x \mid \exists y  \quad xRy\} \]
    Range(Image) of a set ``$R$'' is all ``$y$'' that there exists a ``$x$'' s.t.
    ``$xRy$''
    \[ Range (R) := \{ y  \mid \exists x \quad xRy\}. \]
\end{definition}

\begin{center}
    \includegraphics[scale=.4]{R-Im-Dom (1).pdf}
\end{center}

\begin{theorem}
    \textbf{$(schema)$} $(Dom I, Dom E )$

            \have [ ] {2} {\therefore t \in Dom(R) }  \by{$Dom I$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt} &
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \have  [ ]{2} {  t \in Dom(R) }
            \open
            \have  [ ]{2} {   t R y   }
            \have [ ] {2} { \vdots }
            \have [ ] {2} { \theta }
            \close
            \have [ ] {2} { \therefore  \theta }     \by{$Dom E$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt}
        \\
        \hline
    \end{tabular}
    \\
    \\
    \\
    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.\twemoji{1f600}
\end{theorem}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Inverse of a Relation} \\

    \[ 		R^{-1}=\{u \mid \exists x,y \Big( u=(x,y) \land yRx \Big)\}   \]
    or i.e.
    \[ 		R^{-1}=\{(x,y) \mid yRx\}   \]
    therefore.
    \[\forall x,y \Big( xR^{-1}y \leftrightarrow yRx \Big) \]

\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}
    \textbf{$(schema)$} $( ()^{-1} I, ()^{-1} E )$

    \begin{tabular}{|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|}
        \hline
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \hypo   [ ]{2} {   t R s }
            \have [ ] {2} {\therefore s R^{-1} t  }  \by{$()^{-1} I$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt} &
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \have  [ ]{2} {  t \in R^{-1} }
            \open
            \have  [ ]{2} {   t = (x,y) }
            \have  [ ]{2} {   y R^{-1} x   }
            \have [ ] {2} { \vdots }
            \have [ ] {2} { \theta }
            \close
            \have [ ] {2} { \therefore  \theta }     \by{$ ()^{-1} E $}{}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt}
        \\
        \hline
    \end{tabular}
    \\
    \\
    \\
    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.\twemoji{1f600}
\end{theorem}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Combination of a Relations} \\

    \[ 		S \circ R = \{u \mid \exists x,y \Big(u=(x,y) \land \exists z \big( xRz \wedge zSy \big)\Big)\}   \]

    or i.e.
    \[ 		S \circ R =\{(x,y) \mid \exists z \Big( xRz \wedge zSy \Big) \}   \]
    therefore.
    \[\forall x,y\Big( (x,y) \in S\circ R \leftrightarrow \exists z  \big( xRz \wedge zSy \big) \Big) \]
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}
    \textbf{$(schema)$} $( \circ I, \circ E )$

    \begin{tabular}{|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|>{\centering\arraybackslash}m{7.5cm}|}
        \hline
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \hypo   [ ]{2} {   t S s  }
            \hypo   [ ]{2} {  s R r }
            \have [ ] {2} {\therefore s R \circ S r  }  \by{$\circ I$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt} &
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \have  [ ]{2} {  t \in R \circ S }
            \open
            \have  [ ]{2} {   t = (x,y) }
            \have  [ ]{2} {   x S z  }
            \have  [ ]{2} {   z R y  }
            \have [ ] {2} { \vdots }
            \have [ ] {2} { \theta }
            \close
            \have [ ] {2} { \therefore  \theta }     \by{$ \circ E $}{}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt}
        \\
        \hline
    \end{tabular}
    \\
    \\
    \\
    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.\twemoji{1f600}
\end{theorem}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Image} \\
    \[ R[A] := \{y \mid \exists x \in A \quad xRy \} \]
    you can olso see by definition of ``inverse of a Relation'' and ``image'' this
    equation holds.
    \[ R^{-1}[B] = \{ x  \mid \exists y \in B \quad xRy \} \]
    we cal this ``\textbf{Pre-image}''

\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}
    \textbf{$(schema)$} $(R[] I, R[] E , R^{-1}[] I, R^{-1}[] E )$

            \hypo   [ ]{2} {   t R s }

        \end{fitchproof}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt} &
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \have  [ ]{2} {  s \in R[A] }
            \open
            \have  [ ]{2} {   x \in A   }
            \have  [ ]{2} {   x R s   }
            \have [ ] {2} { \vdots }
            \have [ ] {2} { \theta }
            \close
            \have [ ] {2} { \therefore  \theta }     \by{$R[] E$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}
        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt}
        \\
        \hline
        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \hypo   [ ]{2} {   s \in B }
            \hypo   [ ]{2} {   t R s }

        \rule[0pt]{0pt}{0pt} &

            \have  [ ]{2} {   y \in B   }
            \have  [ ]{2} {   t R y   }
            \have [ ] {2} { \vdots }
            \have [ ] {2} { \theta }
            \close
            \have [ ] {2} { \therefore  \theta }     \by{$R^{-1}[] E$}{}
        \end{fitchproof}
