    \textbf{Order in Natural numbers($\mathbb{N}$)}

    \[
        < := \{ (x,y) \in \mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}  \mid x \in y \}
    \]
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}
    \textbf{Order of Natural numbers($\mathbb{N}$)} prove
    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item        $ (\mathbb{N} , <  )  $ is linearly ordered. \twemoji{1f600}
        \item        $ \forall n \in \mathbb{N} \quad (n , <  )  $ is Well ordered.
        \item        $ (\mathbb{N} , <  )  $ is Well ordered.
        \item       $ \Big( \forall x \in \mathbb{N} \quad   \exists y \in \mathbb{N} \quad  x < y \Big)  $ (doesn't have right endpoint)
    \end{enumerate}

    proof.

    \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]

        \have  {2} { 0 \not< 0 }         \by{$def \emptyset $}{}
        \open
        \have  {2} {n\in \mathbb{N} }                                   \by{$acp $}{}
        \open
        \have  {2} { n \not< n }                                   \by{$acp $}{}
        \open
        \have  {2} { s(n) < s(n) }                                 \by{$acp $}{}
        \open
        \have  {2} { s(n) \in n }                                    \by{$acp $}{}
        \have  {2} {  n \in s(n)  }                                 \by{$ \cup I $}{}
        \have  {2} {  n \in n  }                                       \by{$ Transitive(\mathbb{N}) $}{}
        \have  {2} {\bot}                                              \by{$ 3 ,7 $}{}
        \close
        \open
        \have  {2} { s(n) \in \{ n \} }                              \by{$acp $}{}
        \have  {2} { s(n) = n  }
        \have  {2} { s(n) \not< s(n) }                               \by{$= E $}{}
        \have  {2} {\bot}                                              \by{$ 4 ,11 $}{}
        \close
        \have  {2} {\bot}                                             \by{$ \cup E $}{}
        \close
        \have  {2} { s(n) \not< s(n) }                                 \by{$ \neg I $}{}
        \close
        \close
        \have  {2} {\forall n \in \mathbb{N} \quad  n \not< n }         \by{ Induction theorem }{}

    \end{fitchproof}

    \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]

        \have  {2} {\forall n , m ,k  \in \mathbb{N} \quad  n < m < k  \to n < k }         \by{ Induction theorem }{}

    \end{fitchproof}

    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}\textbf{Induction ,second version}
    $ \Big( \forall  k < n \quad  P(k) \Big) \to P(n)  /\therefore \forall n\in \mathbb{N} \quad P(n)$
    \\
    proof.

\end{theorem}

\section{The Recursion theorem}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Tuple (new definition)-Finite sequence} is a function ``$a : n \to V$'' s.t. $n \in  \mathbb{N}$ we know we can show this
    by ``$(a_i)_{i \in n}$'' we can also show it by ``$(a_0 , a_1 ,a_2 , ... , a_{n-1})$''
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Sequence} is a function ``$a : \mathbb{N} \to V$''  we know we can show this
    by ``$(a_i)_{i \in \mathbb{N}}$'' we can also show it by ``$(a_i)_{i = 0}^{\infty}$''
\end{definition}

now we can redefine ``Cartesian product''. we define finite Cartesian product
``$\prod_{i \in n} A_i := \{ (a_i)_{i \in n} \mid a_i \in A_i \}$'' and define
infinite Cartesian product ``$\prod_{i \in \mathbb{N}} A_i := \{ (a_i)_{i \in
        \mathbb{N}} \mid a_i \in A_i \}$'' and define Cartesian product in general way
``$\prod_{i \in I} A_i := \{ (a_i)_{i \in I} \mid a_i \in A_i \}$''. and you
can easily see that from the definition of ``$B^A$'', ``$A^n$'' is the set of
all $n$-length finite sequences of $a \in A$ and ``$A^\mathbb{N}$'' is the set
of all infinite sequences of $a \in A$.

\begin{theorem}[Recursion]
    For any set $A$, any $a \in A$, and any function $g : A \times \mathbb{N} \to A$,
    there is a unique function $f : \mathbb{N} \to A$ such that:
    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt}
        \item $f(0) = a$.
        \item $\forall n \in \mathbb{N} \quad f(s(n)) = g(f(n),n)$.
    \end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}

    Define

    \[
        Compute_n(t_n) :  t_n  : s(n) \to A
        \ \land\ t_n(0) = a \ \land\ \forall k < n \ \big( t(s(k)) = g(t(k),k) \big) .
    \]

    \[
        F := \Big\{ t_n \ \Big|\ Compute_n(t_n) \Big\}.
    \]
    \textbf{Claim 0: $ \forall n \in \mathbb{N} \quad \exists t_{n}\in F$.}

    \textbf{Claim 1: $F \neq \emptyset$.}

    \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]

        \have {12} { t_0 \in F}

        \have {13} {F\neq\emptyset}

    \end{fitchproof}

    Define

    \[
        f := \bigcup F.
    \]

    \textbf{Claim 2: $f$ is a function.}

    \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]

        \open
        \have {1} {t_m,t_n\in F}
        \open
        \have {2} {Dom(t_m)=s(m)}
        \have {3} {Dom(t_n)=s(n)}
        \open
        \have {4} {m \le n}

        \have {5} {t_m(0)=a}
        \have {6} {t_n(0)=a}
        \have {7} {t_m(0)=t_n(0)}

        \open
        \have {12} { k < m }
        \open
        \have {8} {t_m(k)=t_n(k)}
        \have {9} {g(t_m(k),k)=g(t_n(k),k)}
        \have {9} {t_m(s(k))=g(t_m(k),k)}
        \have {10} {t_n(s(k))=g(t_n(k),k)}
        \have {11} {t_m(s(k))=t_n(s(k))}
        \close

        \have {12} {\forall k \leq m \quad t_m (k) = t_n (k)}
        \close
        \close
        \have {13} {t_m \subseteq t_n \lor t_n \subseteq t_m}
        \close

        \close
        \have {14} {\forall t_m , t_n \in F
            \quad
            (t_m\subseteq t_n \lor t_n\subseteq t_m)}

        \have {15} {Compatible(F)}
        \have {20} {Func(f)}
    \end{fitchproof}

    \medskip

    \textbf{Claim 3: $\text{Dom}(f) = \mathbb{N}$ and $\text{Range}(f) \subseteq A$.}

    \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]

        \open
        \have {21} {n\in\mathbb{N}}
        \have {22} {\exists t_n\in F}
        \have {23} {n\in Dom(t_n)}
        \have {25} {n\in Dom(f)}
        \close

        \open
        \have {27} {n\in Dom(f)}
        \open
        \have {28} { t_m \in F }
        \have {28} { n \in Dom(t_m)}
        \have {29} {Dom(t_m)=s(m)}
        \have {30} {n\in s(m)}
        \have {31} {n\in\mathbb{N}}
        \close
        \have {31} {n\in\mathbb{N}}
        \close

        \have {33} {Dom(f)=\mathbb{N}}

        \open
        \have {37} {x\in Range(f)}
        \have {34} {(n,x)\in f}
        \open
        \have {35} { t_m \in F }
        \have {35} { (n,x)\in t}
        \have {36} {t_m : s(m)\to A}
        \have {37} {x\in A}
        \close
        \have {37} {x\in A}
        \close

        \have {38} {Range(f) \subseteq A}

    \end{fitchproof}

    \medskip

    \textbf{Claim 4: $f$ satisfies the recursion equations.}

    \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]

        \have {27} {f(0)=a}
        \open
        \have {21} {n\in\mathbb{N}}
        \have {22} {\exists t_{n+1}\in F}
        \have {23} {t_{n+1}(s(n))
            =
            g(t_{n+1}(n),n)}
        \have {24} {t_{n+1}\subseteq f}
        \have {25} {f(s(n))
            =
            g(f(n),n)}
        \close

        \have {26} {\forall n\in\mathbb{N}
            \quad
            f(s(n))
            =
            g(f(n),n)}

    \end{fitchproof}

    \medskip

    \textbf{Claim 5 : Uniqueness.}

    \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]

        \open
        \have {28} {h:\mathbb{N}\to A}
        \have {29} {h(0)=a}
        \have {30} {\forall n\in\mathbb{N}
            \quad
            h(s(n))
            =
            g(h(n),n)}

        \have {31} {f(0)=h(0)}

        \open
        \have {32} {f(n)=h(n)}
        \have {33} {g(f(n),n) = g(h(n),n) }
        \have {33} {f(s(n)) = g(f(n),n)}
        \have {34} {h(s(n)) = g(h(n),n)}
        \have {35} {f(s(n))=h(s(n))}
        \close

        \have {36} {\forall n\in\mathbb{N}
            \quad
            f(n)=h(n)}

        \have {37} {f=h}
        \close

        \have {38} {\exists !f:\mathbb{N}\to A
            \quad
            \Big(
            f(0)=a
            \land
            \forall n\in\mathbb{N}
            \quad
            f(s(n))
            =
            g(f(n),n)
            \Big)}

    \end{fitchproof}

    \medskip

    Therefore, there exists a unique function $f : \mathbb{N} \to A$ such that
    $f(0) = a$ and $\forall n \in \mathbb{N} \ f(s(n)) = g(f(n),n)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Parametric Recursion Theorem]
    For any sets ``$A,P$'', any function ``$a:P\to A$'', and any function
    ``$g:P\times A\times \mathbb{N}\to A$'', there is a unique function
    ``$f:P\times\mathbb{N}\to A$'' such that
    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt}

        \item
              \[
                  \forall p\in P \quad f(p,0)=a(p)
              \]

        \item
              \[
                  \forall p\in P \ \forall n\in\mathbb{N}
                  \quad
                  f\big(p,s(n)\big)=g\big(p,f(p,n),n\big)
              \]
    \end{enumerate}

    \begin{proof}

        Fix $p\in P$.

        Define

        \[
            G_p(x,n):=g(p,x,n).
        \]

        \textbf{Claim 1 :}
        $G_p:A\times\mathbb{N}\to A$.

        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]

            \open
            \have {1} {p\in P}
            \open
            \have {2} {(x,n)\in A\times\mathbb{N}}
            \have {3} {g(p,x,n)\in A}
            \have {4} {G(p)(x,n)\in A}
            \close
            \have {5} {G(p):A\times\mathbb{N}\to A}
            \close

            \have {6} {\forall p\in P
                \quad
                G(p):A\times\mathbb{N}\to A}

        \end{fitchproof}

        \medskip

        For every $p\in P$, by the ordinary Recursion Theorem applied to $a(p)\in A$
        and $G(p):A\times\mathbb{N}\to A$, there exists a unique function

        \[
            F_p:\mathbb{N}\to A
        \]

        such that

        \[
            F_p(0)=a(p)
        \]

        and

        \[
            \forall n\in\mathbb{N}
            \quad
            F_p(s(n))
            =
            G(p)(F_p(n),n).
        \]

        Define

        \[
            f(p,n):=F_p(n).
        \]

        \medskip

        \textbf{Claim 2 :}
        $f:P\times\mathbb{N}\to A$.

        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]

            \open
            \have {10} {(p,n)\in P\times\mathbb{N}}
            \have {11} {F_p:\mathbb{N}\to A}
            \have {12} {F_p(n)\in A}
            \have {13} {f(p,n)\in A}
            \close

            \have {14} {f:P\times\mathbb{N}\to A}

        \end{fitchproof}

        \medskip

        \textbf{Claim 3 :}
        $f$ satisfies the recursion equations.

        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]

            \open
            \have {20} {p\in P}
            \have {21} {F_p(0)=a(p)}
            \have {22} {f(p,0)=a(p)}
            \close

            \have {23} {\forall p\in P
                \quad
                f(p,0)=a(p)}

            \open
            \have {24} {p\in P}
            \have {25} {n\in\mathbb{N}}

            \have {26} {F_p(s(n))
                =
                G(p)(F_p(n),n)}

            \have {27} {G(p)(F_p(n),n)
                =
                g(p,F_p(n),n)}

            \have {28} {f(p,s(n))
                =
                g(p,f(p,n),n)}
            \close

            \have {29} {\forall p\in P
                \ \forall n\in\mathbb{N}
                \quad
                f(p,s(n))
                =
                g(p,f(p,n),n)}

        \end{fitchproof}

        \medskip

        Define

        \[
            H_p(n):=h(p,n).
        \]

        \textbf{Claim 4 : Uniqueness.}

        \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
            \open
            \have {30} {h:P\times\mathbb{N}\to A}

            \have {31} {\forall p\in P
                \quad
                h(p,0)=a(p)}

            \have {32} {\forall p\in P
                \ \forall n\in\mathbb{N}
                \quad
                h(p,s(n))
                =
                g(p,h(p,n),n)}

            \open
            \have {33} {p\in P}
            \have {34} {H_p(0)=a(p)}

            \have {35} {\forall n\in\mathbb{N}
                \quad
                H_p(s(n))
                =
                g(p,H_p(n),n)}

            \have {36} {F_p=H_p}

            \open
            \have {37} {n\in\mathbb{N}}
            \have {38} {f(p,n)=F_p(n)}
            \have {39} {H_p(n)=h(p,n)}
            \have {40} {f(p,n)=h(p,n)}
            \close

            \have {41} {\forall n\in\mathbb{N}
                \quad
                f(p,n)=h(p,n)}

            \close

            \have {42} {\forall p\in P
                \ \forall n\in\mathbb{N}
                \quad
                f(p,n)=h(p,n)}

            \have {43} {f=h}

            \close

            \have {44} {\exists !f:P\times\mathbb{N}\to A
                \quad
                \Big(
                \forall p\in P
                \quad
                f(p,0) = a(p)
                \land
                \forall p\in P
                \forall n \in \mathbb{N}
                \quad
                f(p,s(n))
                =
                g(p,f(p,n),n)
                \Big)}

        \end{fitchproof}

        \medskip

        Therefore, there exists a unique function $f:P\times\mathbb{N}\to A$ such that

        \[
            \forall p\in P
            \quad
            f(p,0)=a(p)
        \]

        and

        \[
            \forall p\in P
            \ \forall n\in\mathbb{N}
            \quad
            f(p,s(n))
            =
            g(p,f(p,n),n).
        \]

    \end{proof}

\end{theorem}

\section{Arithmetic of Natural numbers}

\begin{theorem}\textbf{Summation} there is a unique function ``$+ :\mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{N}$'' s.t.

    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item       $\forall m \in \mathbb{N} \quad m+0 = m$
        \item       $\forall m,n \in \mathbb{N} \quad m+s(n) = s(m +n)  $
    \end{enumerate}
    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}\textbf{Multiplication} there is a unique function ``$\times :\mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{N}$'' s.t.

    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item       $\forall m \in \mathbb{N} \quad m \times 0 = 0$
        \item       $\forall m,n \in \mathbb{N} \quad m \times s(n) = m \times n+ m   $
    \end{enumerate}
    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.
\end{theorem}
we denote also ``Multiplication'' with ``$m \cdot n$'' and ``$mn$'' too.

\section{Peano arithmetic}

assume a structure ``$\mathfrak{N} := (N , + , \cdot , s , 0)$''(here ``$+ ,
    \cdot , s , 0 $'' are arbitrary functions and constants and doesn't have meaning
we defined before). with these properties.

\begin{enumerate}
    \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
    \item       $\forall x , y  \in N \quad s(x ) = s( y ) \to x = y   $
    \item       $\forall x  \in N \quad s(x ) \not= 0$
    \item       $\forall x  \in N \quad x +0 = x $
    \item       $\forall x , y  \in N \quad x +s( y ) = s(x  + y )  $
    \item       $\forall x  \in N \quad x  \cdot 0 = 0$
    \item       $\forall x , y  \in N \quad x  \cdot s( y ) = x  \cdot  y + x    $
    \item        $\phi(0) \land \forall  y  \in N \quad \Big( \phi( y ) \to \phi(s( y )) \Big) \to \forall  y \in N \quad \phi( y )$
          (this is true for arbitrary ``$\phi$'')
\end{enumerate}
we call ``$\mathfrak{N}$'' a model of ``Peano arithmetic''.

\begin{lemma}prove for ``$(N , +)$''
    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item       $\forall x , y , z  \in N \quad (x + y )+ z  = x +( y + z )  $ (associative $+$)
        \item       $\forall x  \in N \quad  x + 0 = x = 0 + x   $ (identity  $+$)
        \item       $\forall x , y , z  \in N \quad x  +  z  =  y  +  z   \to x  =  y  $ (cancellative  $+$)
        \item       $\forall x , y  \in N \quad x + y  =  y + x $  (commutative(abelian)  $+$)
    \end{enumerate}

    \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]

        \open
        \have {14} { x , y , z  \in N }   \by{$acp $}{}
        \have {14} { (x + y )+ 0 = x + y  = x +( y + 0 )}    \by{Ax. 3}{}
        \open
        \have {14} { (x + y )+ z  = x +( y + z )}                 \by{$acp $}{}
        \have {14} { (x + y )+ s(z) =  s \big((x + y )+ z \big) =  s \big(x + ( y + z ) \big) = x + s( y + z ) = x +( y + s(z) )}     \by{Ax. 4 , $3$}{}
        \close
        \have {14} { (x + y )+ z  = x +( y + z )}   \by{Ax. Ind}{}

        \close
        \have {14} {\forall x , y , z  \in N \quad (x + y )+ z  = x +( y + z )}     \by{$ \to I , \forall I$}{}

    \end{fitchproof}

    \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]

        \open
        \have {14} { x   \in N }   \by{$acp $}{}
        \have {14} {  0 + 0  = 0 }                 \by{Ax. 3}{}
        \open
        \have {14} {  0 + x  = x }     \by{$acp $}{}
        \have {14} {  0 + s(x)  = s(0 + x)  = s(x) }     \by{Ax. 3 , $4$}{}
        \close
        \have {14} { 0 + x  = x }     \by{Ax. Ind}{}
        \close
        \have {14} {\forall x  \in N \quad x  + 0 = 0 + x  = x}     \by{$ \to I , \forall I$}{}

    \end{fitchproof}

    \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]

        \open
        \have {14} { x , y   \in N }   \by{$acp $}{}
        % \have {14} {  0 + x  = x }     \by{$acp $}{}
        \have {14} {  y  + s(0) = s(y + 0) =  s(y)  = s(y) + 0 }                 \by{Ax. 3}{}
        \open
        \have {14} { y + s( x ) = s(y) + x }     \by{$acp $}{}
        \have {14} { y + s( s(x) ) = s( y + s(x)) = s( s(y) + x )    = s(y) + s(x) }     \by{Ax. 4 , $3$}{}
        \close
        \have {14} { x  + y = y + x }     \by{Ax. Ind}{}
        \close
        \have {14} {\forall x , y \in N \quad  y + s( x ) = s(y) + x }     \by{$ \to I , \forall I$}{}

    \end{fitchproof}

    \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]

        \open
        \have {14} { x , y   \in N }   \by{$acp $}{}
        % \have {14} {  0 + x  = x }     \by{$acp $}{}
        \have {14} {  x  + 0  = 0 + x }                 \by{ Lemma 7.5.1.2}{}
        \open
        \have {14} { x  + y = y + x }     \by{$acp $}{}
        \have {14} { x  + s(y) = s(x + y) = s( y + x ) =  y + s( x ) = s(y) + x }     \by{Ax. Ind}{}
        \close
        \have {14} { x  + y = y + x }     \by{Ax. Ind}{}
        \close
        \have {14} {\forall x , y \in N \quad x  + y = y + x  }     \by{$ \to I , \forall I$}{}

    \end{fitchproof}

    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.\twemoji{1f600}

\end{lemma}

\begin{lemma}prove for ``$(N , + , \cdot)$''
    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item       $\forall x , y , z  \in N \quad (x y) z  = x (y z)  $ (associative $\times$)
        \item       $\forall x  \in N \quad x  \cdot 1 = 1 \cdot x  = x $ (identity  $\times$)
        \item       $\forall x  , y    \in N \quad   x   y  = 0   \to  x  = 0  \lor   y  = 0 $ (zero divisor free $\times$)
        \item       $\forall x , y  \in N \quad x y = y x$ (commutative(abelian)  $\times$)
        \item       $\forall x  , y  , z   \in N \quad \Big(x    ( y   +  z  ) = x     y   +  x     z   \Big)
                  \land     \Big((x   +  y  )   z   = x     z   +   y     z   \Big)  $ (distributive $\times$)
    \end{enumerate}
    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.\twemoji{1f600}

    \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]

        \open
        \have {14} { x   \in N }   \by{$acp $}{}
        \have {14} {  0  \cdot  0 = 0  }                 \by{Ax. 5}{}
        \open
        \have {14} { 0 \cdot  x =  0 }     \by{$acp $}{}
        \have {14} { 0 \cdot  s(x) = 0 \cdot x + 0 =  0 }
        \close
        \have {14} { 0 \cdot  x =  0 }     \by{Ax. Ind}{}
        \close
        \have {14} {\forall x , y \in N \quad  0 \cdot  x =  0  }     \by{$ \to I , \forall I$}{}

    \end{fitchproof}

    \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]

        \open
        \have {14} { x , y   \in N }   \by{$acp $}{}
        % \have {14} {  0 + x  = x }     \by{$acp $}{}
        \have {14} {  0 + y 0 = s(y) \cdot 0  }                 \by{Ax. 5}{}
        \open
        \have {14} { x + y x = s(y) \cdot x }     \by{$acp $}{}
        \have {14} { s(x) + y x = x + s( y x ) = s( x +  y x ) = s( s(y) \cdot x)  = s(y) \cdot s(x) }
        \close
        \have {14} { x + y x = s(y) \cdot x }     \by{Ax. Ind}{}
        \close
        \have {14} {\forall x , y \in N \quad x + y x = s(y) \cdot x  }     \by{$ \to I , \forall I$}{}

    \end{fitchproof}

    \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]

        \open
        \have {14} { x , y   \in N }   \by{$acp $}{}
        % \have {14} {  0 + x  = x }     \by{$acp $}{}
        \have {14} {  x  \cdot  0 = 0  }                 \by{Ax. 5}{}
        \have {14} {  x  \cdot  0  = 0 \cdot  x }                 \by{ Lemma }{}
        \open
        \have {14} { x   y = y  x }     \by{$acp $}{}
        \have {14} { x  \cdot s(y) = x y + x =  y x + x = x + y x   = s(y) \cdot x }
        \close
        \have {14} { x   y = y  x }     \by{Ax. Ind}{}
        \close
        \have {14} {\forall x , y \in N \quad x   y = y  x  }     \by{$ \to I , \forall I$}{}

    \end{fitchproof}

\end{lemma}

Now define ``$ x < y : \exists z \not=0 \quad x + z = y $''. You can prove for
natural numbers
\[  \forall m,n \in \mathbb{N} \quad m < n \leftrightarrow \exists k \not=0 \quad m + k = n \]

\begin{lemma}prove for ``$(N , + , \times , <)$''
    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item       $\forall x , y , z \in N \quad x < y \to x + z < y + z  $
        \item       $\forall x , y , z \in N \quad x < y \land 0 < z \to x z < y z  $
    \end{enumerate}
    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.\twemoji{1f600}
\end{lemma}

as you can see ``$(\mathbb{N} , + , \times , s , 0)$'' is a model of peano
arithmetic. so properties are true for them.

\chapter{From Natural numbers to Complex numbers }

\section{Integers``$\mathbb{Z}$''}

we learned about ``$\mathbb{N}$'' before and we constructed structure
``$(\mathbb{N} , + , \cdot , <)$''. if you assume ``$\mathbb{N} \times
    \mathbb{N}$'' we can define an equivalence relation ``$ (n_1 , m_1) \sim (n_2 ,
    m_2) : n_1 + m_2 = n_2 + m_1 $''(check it's an equivalence relation).

\begin{definition}\textbf{Integers``$\mathbb{Z}$''} is a set
    \[ \mathbb{Z} := \mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N} / \sim\]
    we denote it's members with ``$z_0 , z_1 ,...$''
\end{definition}

in this system ``$ 0 := [(0,0)]$'' and ``$ \forall n \in \mathbb{N} \quad n :=
        [(n,0)]$'' and ``$\forall n \in \mathbb{N} \quad -n := [(0,n)]$''. in
general``$\forall m,n \in \mathbb{N} \quad m-n := [(m,n)]$''. so we can say ``$
    \mathbb{N} \subseteq \mathbb{Z}$''.

\begin{theorem}\textbf{Summation} there is a unique function ``$+_\mathbb{Z} :\mathbb{Z} \times \mathbb{Z} \to \mathbb{Z}$'' s.t.

    \[ \forall m_1 , m_2 , n_1 , n_2 \in \mathbb{N} \quad [(m_1 , n_1)] +_\mathbb{Z}  [(m_2 , n_2)]  = [(m_1 + m_2, n_1 + n_2)] \]

    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}\textbf{Multiplication} there is a unique function ``$\times_\mathbb{Z} :\mathbb{Z} \times \mathbb{Z} \to \mathbb{Z}$'' s.t.

    \[ \forall m_1 , m_2 , n_1 , n_2 \in \mathbb{N} \quad [(m_1 , n_1)] \times_\mathbb{Z} [(m_2 , n_2)]  = [(m_1 m_2  + n_1 n_2 , m_1 n_2 + m_2 n_1)] \]

    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.
\end{theorem}

\begin{definition}\textbf{Order in $\mathbb{Z}$}
    \[ [(m_1 , n_1)] <_\mathbb{Z} [(m_2 , n_2)]  : m_1 + n_2  < m_2 + n_1 \]
\end{definition}

this lemma let us use ``$<$'' instead of ``$<_\mathbb{Z}$'', ``$+$'' instead of
``$+_\mathbb{Z}$'' and ``$\times$'' instead of ``$\times_\mathbb{Z}$''.

\begin{lemma}prove.
    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item       $/ \therefore   \forall m,n \in \mathbb{N} \quad m <_\mathbb{Z} n  \leftrightarrow m  < n$
        \item       $/ \therefore   \forall m,n \in \mathbb{N} \quad m +_\mathbb{Z} n  = m  + n$
        \item       $/ \therefore   \forall m,n \in \mathbb{N} \quad m \times_\mathbb{Z} n  = m  \times n$
    \end{enumerate}
    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.
\end{lemma}

we denote ``Multiplication'' with ``$z \cdot y$'' and ``$z y$'' too.

\begin{lemma}prove for  ``$ (\mathbb{Z} , +  )  $''
    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item       $\forall x ,y ,z  \in \mathbb{Z} \quad (x + y) + z  = x + (y + z )  $ (associative $+ $)
        \item       $\forall z \in \mathbb{Z} \quad z +  0 = 0 +  z = z$ (identity  $+ $)
        \item       $\forall z \in \mathbb{Z} \quad \exists y \in \mathbb{Z} \quad z +  y = y +  z = 0$  (inverse  $+ $)
        \item       $\forall x ,y  \in \mathbb{Z} \quad x + y  = y + x $   (commutative(abelian)  $+ $)
    \end{enumerate}
    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.\twemoji{1f600}
\end{lemma}

\begin{lemma}prove for  ``$ (\mathbb{Z} , + , \times )  $''
    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item       $\forall x ,y ,z  \in \mathbb{Z} \quad (x   y )  z  = x  (y   z )  $ (associative $\times$)
        \item       $\forall z \in \mathbb{Z} \quad z \cdot 1 = 1 \cdot  z = z$ (identity  $\times$)
        \item       $\forall x ,y   \in \mathbb{Z} \quad   x y = 0   \to  x = 0  \lor  y = 0 $ (zero divisor free $\times$)
        \item       $\forall x ,y  \in \mathbb{Z} \quad x   y  = y   x $  (commutative(abelian)  $\times$)
        \item       $\forall x ,y ,z  \in \mathbb{Z} \quad \Big(x   (y  + z ) = x   y  +  x   z  \Big)
                  \land     \Big((x  + y )  z  = x   z  +  y   z  \Big)  $ (distributive $\times$)
    \end{enumerate}
    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.\twemoji{1f600}
\end{lemma}

\begin{theorem}
    \textbf{Order of Integers($\mathbb{Z}$)} prove for  ``$ (\mathbb{Z} ,+ , \times , <_\mathbb{Z}  )  $ ''
    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item       is linearly ordered.
        \item       $ \Big( \forall x \in \mathbb{Z} \quad   \exists y \in \mathbb{Z} \quad  x < y \Big)
                  \land \Big( \forall x \in \mathbb{Z} \quad   \exists y \in \mathbb{Z} \quad   y < x \Big)  $ (doesn't have endpoints)
        \item       $\forall x ,y ,z \in \mathbb{Z} \quad x  < y  \to x  + z < y  + z  $
        \item       $\forall x ,y ,z \in \mathbb{Z} \quad x  < y  \land 0 < z \to x  z < y  k  $
    \end{enumerate}
    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.\twemoji{1f600}
\end{theorem}

\section{Rational numbers ``$\mathbb{Q}$''}

we learned about ``$\mathbb{Z}$'' before and we constructed structure
``$(\mathbb{Z} , + , \cdot , <)$''. if you assume ``$\mathbb{Z} \times
    (\mathbb{Z} - \{0\})$'' we can define an equivalence relation ``$ (y_1 , x_1 )
    \sim (y_2 , x_2 ) : y_1 \cdot x_2 = y_2 \cdot x_1 $''(check it's an equivalence
relation).

\begin{definition}\textbf{Rational numbers``$\mathbb{Q}$''} is a set
    \[ \mathbb{Q} := \mathbb{Z} \times (\mathbb{Z} - \{0\}) / \sim\]
    we denote it's members with ``$q_0 , q_1 ,...$''
\end{definition}

in this system ``$ 0 := [(0,1)]$'' and ``$ 1 := [(1,1)]$'' and ``$ \forall x
    \in \mathbb{Z} \quad x := [(x,1)]$'' and ``$\forall x \in \mathbb{Z} \quad
    \frac{1}{x} := [(1,x)]$''. in general ``$\forall x,y \in \mathbb{Z} \quad
    \frac{x}{y} := [(x,y)]$''. so we can say ``$ \mathbb{N} \subseteq \mathbb{Z}
    \subseteq \mathbb{Q} $''.

\begin{theorem}\textbf{Summation} there is a unique function ``$+_\mathbb{Q} :\mathbb{Q} \times \mathbb{Q} \to \mathbb{Q}$'' s.t.

    \[ \forall x  , y  , z , u  \in \mathbb{Z} \quad [(x  , z)] +_\mathbb{Q} [(y  , u)]  = [(x u  + z y ,  z  u)] \]

    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}\textbf{Multiplication} there is a unique function ``$\times_\mathbb{Q} :\mathbb{Q} \times \mathbb{Q} \to \mathbb{Q}$'' s.t.

    \[ \forall x  , y  , z , u  \in \mathbb{Z} \quad [(x  , z)] \times_\mathbb{Q} [(y  , u)]  = [(x  y , z u)] \]

    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.
\end{theorem}

\begin{definition}\textbf{Order in $\mathbb{Q}$} .\\
    if ($z > 0 , u > 0$) or ($z < 0 , u < 0$)
    \[ [(x  , z)] <_\mathbb{Q} [(y  , u)]  : x  \cdot u  < y  \cdot z \]
    if ($z > 0 , u < 0$) or ($z < 0 , u > 0$)
    \[ [(x  , z)] <_\mathbb{Q} [(y  , u)]  : x  \cdot u  > y  \cdot z \]
\end{definition}

this lemma let us use ``$<$'' instead of ``$<_\mathbb{Q}$'', ``$+$'' instead of
``$+_\mathbb{Q}$'' and ``$\times$'' instead of ``$\times_\mathbb{Q}$''.

\begin{lemma}prove.
    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item       $/ \therefore   \forall z,y \in \mathbb{Z} \quad z <_\mathbb{Q} y  \leftrightarrow z  < y$
        \item       $/ \therefore   \forall z,y \in \mathbb{Z} \quad z +_\mathbb{Q} y  = z  + y$
        \item       $/ \therefore   \forall z,y \in \mathbb{Z} \quad z \times_\mathbb{Q} y  = z  \times y$
    \end{enumerate}
    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.
\end{lemma}

we denote ``Multiplication'' with ``$x \cdot y$'' and ``$x y$'' too.

\begin{lemma}prove for  ``$ (\mathbb{Q} , +)  $''
    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item       $\forall x ,y ,z  \in \mathbb{Q} \quad (x +y )+z  = x +(y +z )  $ (associative $+$)
        \item       $\forall x \in \mathbb{Q} \quad x + 0 = 0 + x = x$  (identity  $+$)
        \item       $\forall x \in \mathbb{Q} \quad \exists y \in \mathbb{Q} \quad x + y = y + x = 0$  (inverse  $+$)
        \item       $\forall x ,y  \in \mathbb{Q} \quad x +y  = y +x $  (commutative(abelian)  $+$)
    \end{enumerate}
    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.\twemoji{1f600}
\end{lemma}

\begin{lemma}prove for  ``$ (\mathbb{Q} , + , \times  )  $''
    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item       $\forall x ,y ,z  \in \mathbb{Q} \quad (x   y )  z  = x  (y   z )  $  (associative $\times$)
        \item       $\forall x \in \mathbb{Q} \quad x \cdot 1 = 1 \cdot x = x$  (identity  $\times$)
        \item       $\forall x  \in \mathbb{Q} - \{0\} \quad \exists y \in \mathbb{Q} \quad x  y = y  x = 1$  (inverse  $\times$)
        \item       $\forall x ,y  \in \mathbb{Q} \quad x   y  = y   x $  (commutative(abelian)  $\times$)
        \item       $\forall x ,y ,z  \in \mathbb{Q} \quad \Big(x   (y  + z ) = x   y  +  x   z  \Big)
                  \land     \Big((x  + y )  z  = x   z  +  y   z  \Big)  $ (distributive $\times$)
              % \item       $\forall x ,y   \in \mathbb{Q} \quad   x y = 0   \to  x = 0  \lor  y = 0 $ (zero divisor free $\times$)
    \end{enumerate}
    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.\twemoji{1f600}
\end{lemma}

\begin{theorem}
    \textbf{Order of Rational numbers($\mathbb{Q}$)} prove for  ``$ (\mathbb{Q} , <_\mathbb{Q}  )  $''
    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item        is linearly ordered.
        \item       $ \Big( \forall x \in \mathbb{Q} \quad   \exists y \in \mathbb{Q} \quad  x < y \Big)
                  \land \Big( \forall x \in \mathbb{Q} \quad   \exists y \in \mathbb{Q} \quad   y < x \Big)  $ (doesn't have endpoints)
        \item       $  \forall x,y  \in \mathbb{Q} \quad  \exists z \in \mathbb{Q}  \quad  x \not= y \to  x < z < y  $ (dense)
        \item       $\forall x ,y ,z \in \mathbb{Q} \quad x  < y  \to x  + z < y  + z  $
        \item       $\forall x ,y ,z \in \mathbb{Q} \quad x  < y  \land 0 < z \to x  z < y  k  $
    \end{enumerate}
    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.\twemoji{1f600}

\end{theorem}

\section{Real numbers ``$\mathbb{R}$''}

before we we get to lesson define function ``$|\_| : \mathbb{Q} \times
    \mathbb{Q} \to \mathbb{Q}$'' like this.
\[  |x| :=
    \left\{
    \begin{array}{l}
        x  \quad 0 \leq x \\
        -x  \quad  x < 0
    \end{array}
    \right.
\]

if you are not familiar to this notation it means ``$ |\_| := \{ (x,y) \in
    \mathbb{Q} \times \mathbb{Q} \mid (0 \leq x \to y=x ) \land ( x < 0 \to y = -x
    ) \} $''. learn this notation because we want to use later.

we learned about ``$\mathbb{Q}$'' before and we constructed structure
``$(\mathbb{Q} , + , \cdot , <)$''. now assume $\mathbb{Q}^\mathbb{N}$(we know
what does it mean by the definition. it means all the sequences of
$\mathbb{Q}$). we don't want it all we just want a subset.

\begin{definition}\textbf{Cauchy sequence} is a sequence like ``$(a_i)_{i \in \mathbb{N}} \in \mathbb{Q}^\mathbb{N}$'' s.t. elements of it get infinitely
    close to each other i.e.
    \[  Cauchy(a_i)_{i \in \mathbb{N}} : \forall \varepsilon > 0 \quad \exists N  \quad \forall n,m > N  \quad | a_n - a_m | < \varepsilon   \]
\end{definition}

we usually give ``$N$'' natural numbers but it's not necessary.

we define an equivalence relation ``$ (a_i)_{i \in \mathbb{N}} \sim (b_i)_{i
        \in \mathbb{N}} : \forall \varepsilon > 0 \quad \exists N \quad \forall n > N
    \quad | a_n - b_n | < \varepsilon $''(check it's an equivalence relation). we
denote every $[(a_i)_{i \in \mathbb{N}}]$ with $[a_i]_{i \in \mathbb{N}}$.

\begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]

    \have {14} {  a_n - a_n = 0}
    \have {14} { | a_n - a_n | < \varepsilon }
    \have {14} { n > N\to | a_n - a_n | < \varepsilon }
    \have {14} { \forall \varepsilon > 0 \quad \exists N \quad \forall n > N \quad | a_n - a_n | < \varepsilon}    \by{$\forall I , \exists I , \forall I $}{}
    % \have {14} { \vdots}     

    \have {14} {(a_i)_{i \in \mathbb{N}} \sim (a_i)_{i\in \mathbb{N}} }      \by{$def. \sim $}{}

\end{fitchproof}

\begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]

    \have {14} {(a_i)_{i \in \mathbb{N}} \sim (b_i)_{i\in \mathbb{N}} }        \by{$ass$}{}
    \have {14} {\forall \varepsilon > 0 \quad \exists N \quad \forall n > N \quad | a_n - b_n | < \varepsilon}      \by{$def. \sim $}{}
    \have {14} {\forall \varepsilon > 0 \quad \exists N \quad \forall n > N \quad | b_n - a_n | < \varepsilon}

    % \have {14} { \vdots}     

    \have {14} {  (b_i)_{i \in \mathbb{N}} \sim (a_i)_{i\in \mathbb{N}} }       \by{$def. \sim $}{}

\end{fitchproof}

\begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]

    \have {14} {(a_i)_{i \in \mathbb{N}} \sim (b_i)_{i\in \mathbb{N}} }       \by{$ass$}{}
    \have {14} {  (b_i)_{i \in \mathbb{N}} \sim (c_i)_{i\in \mathbb{N}} }        \by{$ass$}{}
    \have {14} {\forall \varepsilon > 0 \quad \exists N \quad \forall n > N \quad | a_n - b_n | < \varepsilon}      \by{$def. \sim $}{}
    \have {14} {\forall \varepsilon > 0 \quad \exists N \quad \forall n > N \quad | b_n - c_n | < \varepsilon}      \by{$def. \sim $}{}
    \have {14} {  \exists N \quad \forall n > N \quad | a_n - b_n | < \frac{\varepsilon}{2} }      \by{$def. \sim $}{}
    \have {14} {  \exists N \quad \forall n > N \quad | b_n - c_n | < \frac{\varepsilon}{2} }      \by{$def. \sim $}{}

    \open
    \have {14} {  \forall n > N \quad | a_n - b_n | < \frac{\varepsilon}{2} }      \by{$acp $}{}
    \open
    \have {14} {  \forall n > N' \quad | b_n - c_n | < \frac{\varepsilon}{2} }      \by{$acp $}{}
    \open
    \have {14} {  n > max\{ N , N' \}  }      \by{$acp $}{}
    \have {14} {   n > N \to | a_n - b_n | < \frac{\varepsilon}{2} }      \by{$ \forall E $}{}
    \have {14} {   n > N' \to | b_n - c_n | < \frac{\varepsilon}{2} }      \by{$ \forall E $}{}
    \have {14} {   | a_n - b_n | < \frac{\varepsilon}{2} }      \by{$ \to E $}{}
    \have {14} {    | b_n - c_n | < \frac{\varepsilon}{2} }      \by{$ \to E $}{}
    \have {14} {     | a_n - b_n | + | b_n - c_n | < \varepsilon }
    \have {14} {    | a_n - c_n | < | a_n - b_n | + | b_n - c_n | }
    \have {14} {    | a_n - c_n | < \varepsilon }
    \close
    \have {14} {   n > max\{ N , N' \} \to | a_n - c_n | < \varepsilon }      \by{$\to I $}{}
    \have {14} {  \forall n > max\{ N , N' \} \quad  | a_n - c_n | < \varepsilon }      \by{$\forall I $}{}
    \have {14} {  \exists N \quad \forall n > N \quad | a_n - c_n | < \varepsilon }      \by{$\exists I$}{}
    \close
    \close
    \have {14} {  \exists N \quad \forall n > N \quad | a_n - c_n | < \varepsilon }      \by{$\exists E$}{}
    \have {14} {\forall \varepsilon > 0 \quad \exists N \quad \forall n > N \quad | a_n - c_n | < \varepsilon}      \by{$\forall I$}{}
    \have {14} {  (a_i)_{i \in \mathbb{N}} \sim (c_i)_{i\in \mathbb{N}} }          \by{$def. \sim $}{}

\end{fitchproof}

\begin{definition}\textbf{Real numbers ``$\mathbb{R}$''} is a set
    \[ \mathbb{R} :=  Cauchy/ \sim  \]
    we denote it's members with ``$r_0 , r_1 ,...$''
\end{definition}

in this system ``$ 0 := [0]_{i \in \mathbb{N}}$'' and ``$ 1 := [1]_{i \in
            \mathbb{N}}$'' and ``$ \forall q \in \mathbb{Q} \quad q := [q]_{i \in
            \mathbb{N}}$''. so we can say ``$ \mathbb{N} \subseteq \mathbb{Z} \subseteq
    \mathbb{Q} \subseteq \mathbb{R}$''.

\begin{theorem}\textbf{Summation} there is a unique function ``$+_\mathbb{R} :\mathbb{R} \times \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}$'' s.t.

    \[  [a_i]_{i \in \mathbb{N}} +_\mathbb{R} [b_i]_{i \in \mathbb{N}}  = [a_i + b_i]_{i \in \mathbb{N}} \]

    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}\textbf{Multiplication} there is a unique function ``$\times_\mathbb{R} :\mathbb{R} \times \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}$'' s.t.

    \[  [a_i]_{i \in \mathbb{N}} \times_\mathbb{R} [b_i]_{i \in \mathbb{N}}  = [a_i  b_i]_{i \in \mathbb{N}} \]

    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.
\end{theorem}

\begin{definition}\textbf{Order in $\mathbb{R}$} .\\
    \[  [a_i]_{i \in \mathbb{N}} <_\mathbb{R} [b_i]_{i \in \mathbb{N}}  :
        \exists \varepsilon > 0 \quad \exists N  \quad \forall n > N  \quad  b_n - a_n  > \varepsilon  \]
\end{definition}

this lemma let us use ``$<$'' instead of ``$<_\mathbb{R}$'', ``$+$'' instead of
``$+_\mathbb{R}$'' and ``$\times$'' instead of ``$\times_\mathbb{R}$''.

\begin{lemma}prove.
    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item       $/ \therefore   \forall x,y \in \mathbb{Q} \quad x <_\mathbb{R} y  \leftrightarrow x  < y$
        \item       $/ \therefore   \forall x,y \in \mathbb{Q} \quad x +_\mathbb{R} y  = x  + y$
        \item       $/ \therefore   \forall x,y \in \mathbb{Q} \quad x \times_\mathbb{R} y  = x  \times y$
    \end{enumerate}
    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.
\end{lemma}

we denote ``Multiplication'' with ``$x \cdot y$'' and ``$x y$'' too.

\begin{lemma}prove.
    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item       $\forall x ,y ,z  \in \mathbb{R} \quad (x +y )+z  = x +(y +z )  $  (associative $+$)
        \item       $\forall x \in \mathbb{R} \quad x + 0 = 0 + x = x$  (identity  $+$)
        \item       $\forall x \in \mathbb{R} \quad \exists y \in \mathbb{R} \quad x + y = y + x = 0$ (inverse  $+$)
        \item       $\forall x ,y  \in \mathbb{R} \quad x +y  = y +x $  (commutative(abelian)  $+$)
    \end{enumerate}
    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.\twemoji{1f600}
\end{lemma}

\begin{lemma}prove.
    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item       $\forall x ,y ,z  \in \mathbb{R} \quad (x   y )  z  = x  (y   z )  $  (associative $\times$)
        \item       $\forall x \in \mathbb{R} \quad x \cdot 1 = 1 \cdot x = x$  (identity  $\times$)
        \item       $\forall x  \in \mathbb{R} - \{0\} \quad \exists y \in \mathbb{R} \quad x  y = y  x = 1$  (inverse  $\times$)
        \item       $\forall x ,y  \in \mathbb{R} \quad x   y  = y   x $  (commutative(abelian)  $\times$)
        \item       $\forall x ,y ,z  \in \mathbb{R} \quad \Big(x   (y  + z ) = x   y  +  x   z  \Big)
                  \land     \Big((x  + y )  z  = x   z  +  y   z  \Big)  $  (distributive $\times$)
              % \item       $\forall x ,y   \in \mathbb{R} \quad   x y = 0   \to  x = 0  \lor  y = 0 $ (zero divisor free $\times$)
    \end{enumerate}
    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.\twemoji{1f600}
\end{lemma}

\begin{theorem}
    \textbf{Linear order of Real numbers($\mathbb{R}$)} prove   ``$ (\mathbb{R} , <_\mathbb{R}  )  $
    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item        is linearly ordered.
        \item       $ \Big( \forall x \in \mathbb{R} \quad   \exists y \in \mathbb{R} \quad  x < y \Big)
                  \land \Big( \forall x \in \mathbb{R} \quad   \exists y \in \mathbb{R} \quad   y < x \Big)  $ (doesn't have endpoints)
        \item       $  \forall x,y  \in \mathbb{R} \quad \exists z \in \mathbb{R} \quad  x \not= y \to   x < z < y  $ (dense)
        \item       $  \forall X  \subseteq \mathbb{R} \quad  (X \not= \emptyset \land \exists y \in \mathbb{R} \quad Uppreb(X,y))
                  \to (\exists y \in \mathbb{R} \quad  sup(X)=y)  $ (complete)
        \item       $\forall x ,y ,z \in \mathbb{R} \quad x  < y  \to x  + z < y  + z  $
        \item       $\forall x ,y ,z \in \mathbb{R} \quad x  < y  \land 0 < z \to x  z < y  k  $
    \end{enumerate}
    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.\twemoji{1f600}
\end{theorem}

\section{Complex numbers``$\mathbb{C}$''}

we learned about ``$\mathbb{R}$'' before and we constructed structure
``$(\mathbb{R} , + , \cdot , <)$''.

\begin{definition}\textbf{Complex numbers``$\mathbb{C}$''} is a set
    \[ \mathbb{C} := \mathbb{R} \times \mathbb{R} \]
    we denote it's members with ``$z_0 , x ,...$''
\end{definition}

in this system ``$ 0 := (0,0)$'' and ``$ \forall r \in \mathbb{R} \quad r :=
    (r,0)$'' . in general ``$\forall x,y \in \mathbb{R} \quad x + iy := (x,y)$''.
so we can say ``$\mathbb{N} \subseteq \mathbb{Z} \subseteq \mathbb{Q} \subseteq
    \mathbb{R} \subseteq \mathbb{C}$''.

\begin{theorem}\textbf{Summation} there is a unique function ``$+_\mathbb{C} :\mathbb{C} \times \mathbb{C} \to \mathbb{C}$'' s.t.

    \[ \forall x , y , z , u \in \mathbb{R} \quad (x , z) +_\mathbb{C}  (y , u)  = (x + y, z + u) \]

    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}\textbf{Multiplication} there is a unique function ``$\times_\mathbb{C} :\mathbb{C} \times \mathbb{C} \to \mathbb{C}$'' s.t.

    \[ \forall x , y , z , u \in \mathbb{R} \quad (x , z) \times_\mathbb{C} (y , u)  = (x y  - z u , x u + y z) \]

    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.
\end{theorem}

this lemma let us use ``$+$'' instead of ``$+_\mathbb{C}$'' and ``$\times$''
instead of ``$\times_\mathbb{C}$''.

\begin{lemma}prove.
    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item       $/ \therefore   \forall x,y \in \mathbb{R} \quad x +_\mathbb{C} y  = x  + y$
        \item       $/ \therefore   \forall x,y \in \mathbb{R} \quad x \times_\mathbb{C} y  = x  \times y$
    \end{enumerate}
    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.
\end{lemma}

we denote ``Multiplication'' with ``$z \cdot y$'' and ``$z y$'' too.

\begin{lemma}prove for  ``$ (\mathbb{C} , +  )  $''
    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item       $\forall x ,y ,z  \in \mathbb{C} \quad (x + y )+ z  = x + (y + z )  $ (associative $+ $)
        \item       $\forall z \in \mathbb{C} \quad z +  0 = 0 +  z = z$ (identity  $+ $)
        \item       $\forall z \in \mathbb{C} \quad \exists y \in \mathbb{C} \quad z +  y = y +  z = 0$  (inverse  $+ $)
        \item       $\forall x ,y  \in \mathbb{C} \quad x + y  = y + x $   (commutative(abelian)  $+ $)
    \end{enumerate}
    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.
\end{lemma}

\begin{lemma}prove for  ``$ (\mathbb{C} , + , \times )  $''
    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item       $\forall x ,y ,z  \in \mathbb{C} \quad (x   y )  z  = x  (y   z )  $ (associative $\times$)
        \item       $\forall z \in \mathbb{C} \quad z \cdot 1 = 1 \cdot  z = z$ (identity  $\times$)
        \item       $\forall x ,y  \in \mathbb{C} \quad x   y  = y   x $  (commutative(abelian)  $\times$)
        \item       $\forall x ,y ,z  \in \mathbb{C} \quad \Big(x   (y  + z ) = x   y  +  x   z  \Big)
                  \land     \Big((x  + y )  z  = x   z  +  y   z  \Big)  $ (distributive $\times$)
              % \item       $\forall x ,y   \in \mathbb{C} \quad   x y = 0   \to  x = 0  \lor  y = 0 $ (zero divisor free $\times$)
    \end{enumerate}
    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.
\end{lemma}

\begin{theorem}\textbf{Exponents} there is a unique function ``$(\_)^{(\_)} :\mathbb{C} \times \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{C}$'' s.t.

    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item       $\forall x \in \mathbb{C} \quad x ^ 0 = 1$
        \item       $\forall x \in \mathbb{C} \quad \forall n \in \mathbb{N} \quad  x ^ {s(n)} = x ^ n \cdot x   $
    \end{enumerate}
    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.
\end{theorem}

\begin{lemma}prove for ``$(\mathbb{C} , + , \cdot , (\_)^{(\_)} )$''
    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item       $\forall x   \in \mathbb{Z} \quad \forall m ,n  \in \mathbb{N} \quad  x^m  \cdot  x^n = x^{m  + n}  $
        \item       $\forall x   \in \mathbb{Z} \quad \forall m ,n  \in \mathbb{N} \quad (x^m)^n = x^{m n}  $
    \end{enumerate}
    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.
\end{lemma}

\begin{theorem}\textbf{Exponents} there is a unique function ``$(\_)^{(\_)} :\mathbb{C} \times \mathbb{Z} \to \mathbb{C}$'' s.t.

    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item       $\forall x \in \mathbb{C} \quad x ^ 0 = 1$
        \item       $\forall x \in \mathbb{C} \quad \forall m ,n  \in \mathbb{N} \quad  x ^ {m-n} = \frac{x ^ m}{x^n}    $
    \end{enumerate}
    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.
\end{theorem}

\begin{lemma}prove for ``$(\mathbb{C} , + , \cdot  )$''
    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item       $\forall x   \in \mathbb{C} \quad \forall y ,z  \in \mathbb{Z} \quad  x^y  \cdot  x^z = x^{y  + z}  $
        \item       $\forall x   \in \mathbb{C} \quad \forall y ,z  \in \mathbb{Z} \quad (x^y)^z = x^{y z}  $
    \end{enumerate}
    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.
\end{lemma}

\begin{theorem}\textbf{Big Sum} if ``$a : \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{C}$'' there is a unique function ``$\sum_{i = 0}^{(\_)} (\_)  :\mathbb{C} \times \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{C}$'' s.t.

    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item       $\forall a \quad  \sum_{i = 0}^{0} (a_0)  = a_0$
        \item       $\forall a \quad \forall n \in \mathbb{N} \quad  \sum_{i = 0}^{s(n)} a_{s(n)} = \sum_{i = 0}^{n} a_n + a_{s(n)}  $
    \end{enumerate}
    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.
\end{theorem}

\begin{lemma}prove for ``$(\mathbb{C} , + , \cdot  )$''
    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item       $\forall n   \in \mathbb{N} \quad \forall a , b   \quad  \sum_{i = 0}^n a_n + \sum_{i = 0}^n b_n = \sum_{i = 0}^n (a_n + b_n )  $
        \item       $\forall n   \in \mathbb{N} \quad \forall a   \quad k \sum_{i = 0}^n a_n = \sum_{i = 0}^n ka_n $
    \end{enumerate}
    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.
\end{lemma}

\begin{theorem}\textbf{Big Product} if ``$a : \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{C}$'' there is a unique function ``$\prod_{i = 0}^{(\_)} (\_)  :\mathbb{C} \times \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{C}$'' s.t.

    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item       $\forall a \quad  \prod_{i = 0}^0 (a_0)  = a_0$
        \item       $\forall a \quad \forall n \in \mathbb{N} \quad  \prod_{i = 0}^{s(n)} a_{s(n)} = \prod_{i = 0}^n a_n \cdot a_{s(n)}  $
    \end{enumerate}
    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.
\end{theorem}

\begin{lemma}prove for ``$(\mathbb{C} , + , \cdot  )$''
    \begin{enumerate}
        \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt} % No extra space between items
        \item       $\forall n   \in \mathbb{N} \quad \forall a , b   \quad  \prod_{i = 0}^n a_n  \prod_{i = 0}^n b_n = \prod_{i = 0}^n (a_n  b_n )  $
        \item       $\forall n   \in \mathbb{N} \quad \forall a   \quad  (\prod_{i = 0}^n a_n)^k = \prod_{i = 0}^n (a_n)^k $
    \end{enumerate}
    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.
\end{lemma}

\chapter{Finite and Infinite sets}

\section{Equipotent and Order in sets}

we talked about ``Isomorphism'' before now assume two Structures $\mathfrak{A}
    := (A)$ and $\mathfrak{B} := (B)$. because these Structures have only a set
``Isomorphism'' between them only means there is a bijection function $f:A
    \xrightarrow[surj]{inj} B $.

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Equipotent}
    in  situation above we say ``$A$'' and ``$B$'' are ``Equipotent''.  we denote it with ``$A \cong B$'' i.e.
    \[ A \cong B : \exists f  (f:A \xrightarrow[surj]{inj} B) \]
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma} ``$\cong$'' is a equivalence relation in ``$(V , \cong)$''\\

    \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
        \have {14} {  id : X \xrightarrow[surj]{inj} X }
        \have {14} {  \exists f \quad f : X \xrightarrow[surj]{inj} X }   \by{$ \exists I$}{}
        \have {14} {  X \cong X }                                              \by{$ def. 2$}{}
    \end{fitchproof}

    \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
        \have {14} {  X \cong Y }                     \by{$ ass $}{}
        \have {14} {  \exists f \quad f : X \xrightarrow[surj]{inj} Y }   \by{$ def. 1$}{}

        \open
        \have {14} {  f : X \xrightarrow[surj]{inj} Y }        \by{$ acp $}{}
        \have {14} {  f^{-1} : Y \xrightarrow[surj]{inj} X }           \by{$ lemma$}{}
        \have {14} {  \exists f \quad f : Y \xrightarrow[surj]{inj} X }           \by{$ \exists I$}{}
        \have {14} {  Y \cong X }                                \by{$ def. 5$}{}
        \close
        \have {14} {  Y \cong X }                               \by{$ \exists E$}{}

    \end{fitchproof}

    \begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
        \have {14} {  X \cong Y }               \by{$ ass $}{}
        \have {14} {  Y \cong Z }                  \by{$ ass $}{}

        \have {14} {  \exists f \quad f : X \xrightarrow[surj]{inj} Y }         \by{$ def. 1$}{}
        \have {14} {  \exists f \quad f : Y \xrightarrow[surj]{inj} Z }           \by{$ def. 2$}{}
        \open
        \have {14} {  f : X \xrightarrow[surj]{inj} Y }                      \by{$ acp $}{}
        \open
        \have {14} {  g : Y \xrightarrow[surj]{inj} Z }                        \by{$ acp $}{}
        \have {14} {  g \circ f : X \xrightarrow[surj]{inj} Z }                     \by{$ lemma$}{}
        \have {14} {  \exists f \quad f : X \xrightarrow[surj]{inj} Z }
        \have {14} {  X \cong Z }
        \close
        \close
        \have {14} {  X \cong Z }

    \end{fitchproof}

\end{lemma}

so ``$ V / \cong $'' is a partition for ``$V$'' and every partition equals to a
$[A]_{\cong}$ we denote it by $[A]$.

\begin{definition}
    \textbf{Embedding} if there is a  function $f:A \xrightarrow[]{inj} B $.
    in  situation above we say ``$A$'' is less than or equipotent ``$B$''.   we denote it with ``$A \precsim  B$'' i.e.
    \[ A \precsim  B : \exists f  (f:A \xrightarrow[]{inj} B) \]
\end{definition}

similarly we define ``$[A] \preceq [B] : \forall X \in [A] , Y \in [B] \quad X
    \precsim Y$''

\begin{lemma} $A \precsim  B \therefore / \therefore [A] \preceq  [B] $\\

\end{lemma}

\begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]
    \have {14} {  A \precsim  B   }
    \open
    \have {14} {  X \in [A]}
    \have {14} {  Y \in [B]}
    \have {14} {  X \cong A }
    \have {14} {  Y \cong B }
    \have {14} {  \exists f \quad f : X \xrightarrow[surj]{inj} A }         \by{$ def. 4$}{}
    \have {14} {  \exists f \quad f : A \xrightarrow[]{inj} B }         \by{$ def. 1$}{}
    \have {14} {  \exists f \quad f : B \xrightarrow[surj]{inj} Y }         \by{$ def. 5$}{}
    \open
    \have {14} {  f : X \xrightarrow[surj]{inj} A }         \by{$ acp $}{}
    \open
    \have {14} {   h : A \xrightarrow[]{inj} B }         \by{$ acp $}{}
    \open
    \have {14} {  g : B \xrightarrow[surj]{inj} Y }         \by{$ acp $}{}
    \have {14} {  g \circ h \circ f : X \xrightarrow[]{inj} Y }         \by{$ lemma $}{}
    \have {14} {  \exists f \quad f : X \xrightarrow[]{inj} Y }         \by{$ \exists I$}{}
    \have {14} {  X \precsim  Y   }                                \by{$ def. 13$}{}
    \close
    \close
    \close
    \have {14} {  X \precsim  Y   }                                    \by{$ \exists E$}{}
    \close
    \have {14} {  [A] \preceq  [B] }
\end{fitchproof}

\begin{theorem} \textbf{Schroeder-Bernstein} $ X \precsim  Y , Y \precsim  X / \therefore X \cong  Y$\\
    Proof Is Left As An Exercise To The Reader.
\end{theorem}

\[
    h(x) :=
    \begin{cases}
        f(g(y)) &
        \exists i \quad y \in A_i
        \\
        y       &
        \exists i \quad y \in B_i
    \end{cases}
\]

\[
    C_0 := Y
\]
\[
    C_{s(n)} := f \circ g [C_n]
\]

\[
    D_0 := f[X]
\]
\[
    D_{s(n)} := f \circ g [D_n]
\]

\[
    A_n := C_n - D_n
\]
\[
    B_n := D_n - C_s(n)
\]

\textbf{Claim 1: $ \bigcup_{i \in \mathbb{N}} A_i \cap  \bigcup_{i \in \mathbb{N}} B_i \not= \emptyset$}

\textbf{Claim 2: $ Func(h)$}

\textbf{Claim 3: $ injective(h)$}

\textbf{Claim 4: $ h : Y \xrightarrow[surj]{inj} f[X]$}

\begin{fitchproof}[arrayenv=tabular]

    % \open
    \have {14} {  h : Y \xrightarrow[surj]{inj} f[X] }
    \have {14} {  Y \cong f[X] }
    \have {14} {  X \cong f[X] }
    \have {14} {  X \cong Y }
    % \close

\end{fitchproof}
