\chapter{hyperreal numbers($ {}^*\mathbb{N}  , {}^*\mathbb{Z}, {}^*\mathbb{Q} , {}^*\mathbb{R} $)  }
\section{Filters}

Filters are mathematical objects used to formalize the idea of ``large subsets'' of a set.

\begin{definition}[Filter]

Let $X$ be a nonempty set. A \emph{filter} $\mathcal{F}$ on $X$ is a collection of subsets of $X$ such that:

\begin{enumerate}

    \item $\emptyset\notin\mathcal{F}$.

    \item $X\in\mathcal{F}$.

    \item If $A,B\in\mathcal{F}$, then

    $$
    A\cap B\in\mathcal{F}.
    $$

    \item If $A\in\mathcal{F}$ and $A\subseteq B\subseteq X$, then

    $$
    B\in\mathcal{F}.
    $$

\end{enumerate}

\end{definition}

Intuitively, the sets in $\mathcal{F}$ are considered ``large''. The intersection of two large sets is still large, and every superset of a large set is also large.

\begin{example}[Trivial Filter]

For any set $X$,

$$
\mathcal{F}=\{X\}
$$

is a filter on $X$.

\end{example}

\begin{example}[Principal Filter]

Fix $A\subseteq X$ with $A\neq\emptyset$. Define

$$
\mathcal{F}_A:=\{B\subseteq X:A\subseteq B\}.
$$

Then $\mathcal{F}_A$ is a filter on $X$. This is called the \emph{principal filter generated by $A$}.

\end{example}
\begin{example}[Fr\'echet Filter]

Let $X=\mathbb{N}$. Define

$$
\mathcal{F}
=
\{A\subseteq\mathbb{N}:\mathbb{N}\setminus A\text{ is finite}\}.
$$

Then $\mathcal{F}$ is a filter. Its elements are the cofinite subsets of $\mathbb{N}$. This is called the \emph{Fr\'echet filter}.

\end{example}

\section{Ultrafilters}

Ultrafilters are maximal filters.

\begin{definition}[Ultrafilter]

A filter $\mathcal{U}$ on $X$ is called an \emph{ultrafilter} if it is maximal among filters; that is, whenever $\mathcal{F}$ is a filter on $X$ and $\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathcal{F}$, then

$$
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{U}.
$$

\end{definition}

There is a very important characterization of ultrafilters.

\begin{theorem}[Ultrafilter Characterization]

Let $\mathcal{U}$ be a filter on $X$. Then $\mathcal{U}$ is an ultrafilter if and only if for every subset $A\subseteq X$, exactly one of the following holds:

$$
A\in\mathcal{U},
\quad
\text{or}
\quad
X\setminus A\in\mathcal{U}.
$$

\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}

Suppose first that $\mathcal{U}$ is an ultrafilter. Let $A\subseteq X$. We cannot have both $A\in\mathcal{U}$ and $X\setminus A\in\mathcal{U}$, since then $\emptyset=A\cap(X\setminus A)\in\mathcal{U}$, which is impossible. Suppose that neither $A$ nor $X\setminus A$ belongs to $\mathcal{U}$. Then the filter generated by $\mathcal{U}\cup\{A\}$ is strictly larger than $\mathcal{U}$, contradicting the maximality of $\mathcal{U}$. Hence exactly one of them belongs to $\mathcal{U}$.

Conversely, suppose that for every $A\subseteq X$, exactly one of $A$ and $X\setminus A$ belongs to $\mathcal{U}$. Let $\mathcal{F}$ be a filter on $X$ such that $\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathcal{F}$. If $\mathcal{F}\neq\mathcal{U}$, choose $A\in\mathcal{F}\setminus\mathcal{U}$. By assumption, $X\setminus A\in\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathcal{F}$. Therefore

$$
A\cap(X\setminus A)=\emptyset\in\mathcal{F},
$$

a contradiction. Hence $\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{U}$, so $\mathcal{U}$ is an ultrafilter.

\end{proof}

Thus an ultrafilter makes a complete decision about every subset of $X$: exactly one of $A$ and $X\setminus A$ is ``large''.
\begin{example}

Fix $x\in X$. Define

$$
\mathcal{U}_x:=\{A\subseteq X:x\in A\}.
$$

Then $\mathcal{U}_x$ is an ultrafilter. It is called the \emph{principal ultrafilter at $x$}.

\end{example}

\begin{proof}

First, $\emptyset\notin\mathcal{U}_x$ and $X\in\mathcal{U}_x$. If $A,B\in\mathcal{U}_x$, then $x\in A\cap B$, so $A\cap B\in\mathcal{U}_x$. If $A\in\mathcal{U}_x$ and $A\subseteq B$, then $x\in B$, so $B\in\mathcal{U}_x$. Thus $\mathcal{U}_x$ is a filter.

Moreover, for every $A\subseteq X$, either $x\in A$ or $x\notin A$. Hence either

$$
A\in\mathcal{U}_x,
\qquad
X\setminus A\in\mathcal{U}_x.
$$

By the Ultrafilter Characterization, $\mathcal{U}_x$ is an ultrafilter.

\end{proof}

\section{Nonprincipal Ultrafilters}

An ultrafilter on $\mathbb{N}$ is called \emph{nonprincipal} if it contains no
finite set.

Equivalently, it extends the Fr\'echet filter.

Nonprincipal ultrafilters cannot be explicitly constructed in ordinary
mathematics; their existence usually requires the Axiom of Choice.

\begin{definition}[Extension of a Filter]
    Let $\mathcal{F}$ and $\mathcal{G}$ be filters on a set $X$.

    We say that $\mathcal{G}$ extends $\mathcal{F}$ if
    \[
        \mathcal{F}\subseteq \mathcal{G}.
    \]

    This means every set considered ``large'' by $\mathcal{F}$ is also considered
    ``large'' by $\mathcal{G}$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Ultrafilter Lemma]
    Every filter on a set $X$ can be extended to an ultrafilter.
\end{theorem}

In other words, if $\mathcal{F}$ is a filter on $X$, then there exists an
ultrafilter $\mathcal{U}$ such that
\[
    \mathcal{F}\subseteq \mathcal{U}.
\]

So every filter can be enlarged until it becomes maximal.
\begin{example}
    Let
    \[
        \mathcal{F}=\{A\subseteq\mathbb{N}:\mathbb{N}\setminus A\text{ is finite}\}.
    \]
    This is the Fr\'echet filter. It is not an ultrafilter, because for the set of even numbers
    \[
        E=\{0,2,4,6,\dots\},
    \]
    neither $E\in\mathcal{F}$ nor $\mathbb{N}\setminus E\in\mathcal{F}$. By the Ultrafilter Lemma, there exists an ultrafilter $\mathcal{U}$ such that $\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{U}$. Since $\mathcal{U}$ is an ultrafilter, exactly one of
    \[
        E\in\mathcal{U},
        \quad
        \text{or}
        \quad
        \mathbb{N}\setminus E\in\mathcal{U}
    \]
    holds.
\end{example}

The Ultrafilter Lemma is usually proved using Zorn's Lemma.

\begin{theorem}[Ultrafilter Lemma]
    Let $\mathcal{F}$ be a filter on a set $X$. Then there exists an ultrafilter $\mathcal{U}$ on $X$ such that
    \[
        \mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{U}.
    \]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    Let
    \[
        \mathcal{P}=\{\mathcal{G}:\mathcal{G}\text{ is a filter on }X\text{ and }\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{G}\}.
    \]
    We partially order $\mathcal{P}$ by inclusion. Let $\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{P}$ be a chain and define
    \[
        \mathcal{H}=\bigcup_{\mathcal{G}\in\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{G}.
    \]
    Clearly $\emptyset\notin\mathcal{H}$ and $X\in\mathcal{H}$. If $A,B\in\mathcal{H}$, then $A\in\mathcal{G}_1$ and $B\in\mathcal{G}_2$ for some $\mathcal{G}_1,\mathcal{G}_2\in\mathcal{C}$. Since $\mathcal{C}$ is a chain, one of $\mathcal{G}_1\subseteq\mathcal{G}_2$ or $\mathcal{G}_2\subseteq\mathcal{G}_1$ holds, so $A,B$ belong to the larger filter and hence
    \[
        A\cap B\in\mathcal{H}.
    \]
    If $A\in\mathcal{H}$ and $A\subseteq B\subseteq X$, then $A\in\mathcal{G}$ for some $\mathcal{G}\in\mathcal{C}$, and since $\mathcal{G}$ is a filter, $B\in\mathcal{G}\subseteq\mathcal{H}$. Thus $\mathcal{H}$ is a filter. Moreover,
    \[
        \mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{H},
    \]
    so $\mathcal{H}\in\mathcal{P}$. Therefore every chain in $\mathcal{P}$ has an upper bound, and by Zorn's Lemma, $\mathcal{P}$ has a maximal element, say $\mathcal{U}$.

    We claim that $\mathcal{U}$ is an ultrafilter. Suppose $\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathcal{V}$, where $\mathcal{V}$ is a filter on $X$. Then
    \[
        \mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathcal{V},
    \]
    so $\mathcal{V}\in\mathcal{P}$. By the maximality of $\mathcal{U}$,
    \[
        \mathcal{V}=\mathcal{U}.
    \]
    Therefore $\mathcal{U}$ is an ultrafilter extending $\mathcal{F}$.
\end{proof}
\begin{theorem}
    Let $\mathcal{U}$ be a nonprincipal ultrafilter on $\mathbb{N}$. Then
    \[
        \mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{Fr}}\subseteq\mathcal{U}.
    \]
    That is, every cofinite subset of $\mathbb{N}$ belongs to $\mathcal{U}$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    Let $A\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{Fr}}$. Then $\mathbb{N}\setminus A$ is finite. Suppose, toward a contradiction, that $A\notin\mathcal{U}$. Since $\mathcal{U}$ is an ultrafilter, exactly one of
    \[
        A\in\mathcal{U},
        \quad
        \text{or}
        \quad
        \mathbb{N}\setminus A\in\mathcal{U}
    \]
    holds. Hence $\mathbb{N}\setminus A\in\mathcal{U}$. Thus $\mathcal{U}$ contains a finite set, say
    \[
        F=\{n_1,\ldots,n_k\}\in\mathcal{U}.
    \]

    Suppose that $\{n_i\}\notin\mathcal{U}$ for every $i$. Then, since $\mathcal{U}$ is an ultrafilter,
    \[
        \mathbb{N}\setminus\{n_i\}\in\mathcal{U}
    \]
    for every $i$. By closure under finite intersections,
    \[
        \mathbb{N}\setminus F
        =
        \bigcap_{i=1}^k(\mathbb{N}\setminus\{n_i\})
        \in\mathcal{U}.
    \]
    Since also $F\in\mathcal{U}$, we obtain
    \[
        F\cap(\mathbb{N}\setminus F)=\emptyset\in\mathcal{U},
    \]
    a contradiction. Therefore $\{n_i\}\in\mathcal{U}$ for some $i$, so $\mathcal{U}$ is principal, contradicting our assumption.

    Hence $A\in\mathcal{U}$. Since $A$ was arbitrary,
    \[
        \mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{Fr}}\subseteq\mathcal{U}.
    \]
\end{proof}

\section{Definition of ${}^\ast\mathbb{N}$, ${}^\ast\mathbb{Z}$, ${}^\ast\mathbb{Q}$, and ${}^\ast\mathbb{R}$}

\begin{definition}[Ultrapower Construction]
    Let $X$ be a set and let $\mathcal{U}$ be a nonprincipal ultrafilter on $\omega$. Consider the set
    \[
        X^\omega
    \]
    of all functions $a:\omega\to X$. We usually write such a function as
    \[
        a=(a_i)_{i\in\omega},
    \]
    where $a_i\in X$ for every $i\in\omega$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
    Define a relation $\sim$ on $X^\omega$ by
    \[
        a\sim b
    \]
    if
    \[
        \{i\in\omega:a_i=b_i\}\in\mathcal{U}.
    \]
    In words, two sequences are equivalent if they agree on a set of indices belonging to the ultrafilter.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}
    The relation $\sim$ is an equivalence relation on $X^\omega$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    \textbf{Reflexivity.} For every $a\in X^\omega$,
    \[
        \{i\in\omega:a_i=a_i\}=\omega\in\mathcal{U},
    \]
    so $a\sim a$.

    \textbf{Symmetry.} If $a\sim b$, then
    \[
        \{i\in\omega:a_i=b_i\}
        =
        \{i\in\omega:b_i=a_i\},
    \]
    so $b\sim a$.

    \textbf{Transitivity.} Suppose $a\sim b$ and $b\sim c$. Then
    \[
        A=\{i\in\omega:a_i=b_i\}\in\mathcal{U},
        \qquad
        B=\{i\in\omega:b_i=c_i\}\in\mathcal{U}.
    \]
    Since $\mathcal{U}$ is a filter, $A\cap B\in\mathcal{U}$. If $i\in A\cap B$, then $a_i=b_i=c_i$, so
    \[
        A\cap B\subseteq\{i\in\omega:a_i=c_i\}.
    \]
    Since $\mathcal{U}$ is closed upward,
    \[
        \{i\in\omega:a_i=c_i\}\in\mathcal{U},
    \]
    and therefore $a\sim c$.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}
    The quotient
    \[
        X^\omega/\sim
    \]
    is called the \emph{ultrapower} of $X$ modulo $\mathcal{U}$. The equivalence class of $a\in X^\omega$ is denoted by $[a]$.
\end{definition}

We now apply this construction to the standard number systems.

\begin{definition}
    The set of hypernatural numbers is
    \[
        {}^\ast\mathbb{N}=\mathbb{N}^\omega/\sim.
    \]
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
    The set of hyperintegers is
    \[
        {}^\ast\mathbb{Z}=\mathbb{Z}^\omega/\sim.
    \]
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
    The set of hyperrational numbers is
    \[
        {}^\ast\mathbb{Q}=\mathbb{Q}^\omega/\sim.
    \]
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}
    The set of hyperreal numbers is
    \[
        {}^\ast\mathbb{R}=\mathbb{R}^\omega/\sim.
    \]
\end{definition}

The algebraic operations are defined componentwise. For example, if $[a],[b]\in{}^\ast\mathbb{R}$, then
\[
    [a]+[b]=[(a_i+b_i)_{i\in\omega}],
    \qquad
    [a]\cdot[b]=[(a_ib_i)_{i\in\omega}].
\]
Similarly, the order relation is defined by
\[
    [a]<[b]
\]
if
\[
    \{i\in\omega:a_i<b_i\}\in\mathcal{U}.
\]

\begin{theorem}
    The standard structures $\mathbb{N}$, $\mathbb{Z}$, $\mathbb{Q}$, and $\mathbb{R}$ embed into ${}^\ast\mathbb{N}$, ${}^\ast\mathbb{Z}$, ${}^\ast\mathbb{Q}$, and ${}^\ast\mathbb{R}$, respectively.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
    We prove the statement for $\mathbb{R}$. Define
    \[
        \iota:\mathbb{R}\to{}^\ast\mathbb{R},
        \qquad
        \iota(r)=[(r,r,r,\dots)].
    \]
    Suppose $\iota(r)=\iota(s)$. Then
    \[
        \{i\in\omega:r=s\}\in\mathcal{U}.
    \]
    If $r\neq s$, then $\{i\in\omega:r=s\}=\emptyset$, which cannot belong to a filter. Hence $r=s$, so $\iota$ is injective.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}
    Define $a_i=i$ for every $i\in\omega$. Then
    \[
        [a]\in{}^\ast\mathbb{N}
    \]
    is larger than every standard natural number. Indeed, for every $n\in\mathbb{N}$,
    \[
        \{i\in\omega:n<a_i\}
        =
        \{i\in\omega:n<i\}
    \]
    is cofinite, hence belongs to $\mathcal{U}$. Therefore
    \[
        n<[a].
    \]
\end{example}
\section{The Ultrapower Construction of the Hyperreals}

Let \(r=(r_n)_{n\in\mathbb N}\) and \(s=(s_n)_{n\in\mathbb N}\) be two sequences of real numbers. We denote the set \(\{n\in\mathbb N:r_n=s_n\}\) by \(\llbracket r=s\rrbracket\). Thus

$$
\llbracket r=s\rrbracket=\{n\in\mathbb N:r_n=s_n\}.
$$

The set \(\llbracket r=s\rrbracket\) may be thought of as the interpretation, or value, of the statement ``\(r=s\)''. Instead of assigning a truth value to the statement, we assign the set of indices at which it is true.

Let \(\mathcal F\) be a free ultrafilter on \(\mathbb N\). We define \(r\sim s\) if and only if

$$
\llbracket r=s\rrbracket\in\mathcal F.
$$

In this case we say that \(r=s\) holds \textbf{almost everywhere modulo \(\mathcal F\)}.

The same idea applies to other assertions. We define

$$
\llbracket r<s\rrbracket=\{n\in\mathbb N:r_n<s_n\},
\qquad
\llbracket r>s\rrbracket=\{n\in\mathbb N:r_n>s_n\},
$$

and

$$
\llbracket r\leq s\rrbracket=\{n\in\mathbb N:r_n\leq s_n\}.
$$

Thus, for example, \(\llbracket r<s\rrbracket\in\mathcal F\) means that \(r_n<s_n\) for \(\mathcal F\)-almost all \(n\). We call these sets the \textbf{Statement Values} of the corresponding statements.

\begin{theorem}

The relation \(\sim\) is an equivalence relation on \(\mathbb R^{\mathbb N}\).

\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}

For every \(r\in\mathbb R^{\mathbb N}\), \(\llbracket r=r\rrbracket=\mathbb N\in\mathcal F\), so \(r\sim r\).

If \(r\sim s\), then \(\llbracket r=s\rrbracket=\llbracket s=r\rrbracket\in\mathcal F\), so \(s\sim r\).

Finally, suppose \(r\sim s\) and \(s\sim t\). Then \(\llbracket r=s\rrbracket,\llbracket s=t\rrbracket\in\mathcal F\), so

$$
\llbracket r=s\rrbracket\cap\llbracket s=t\rrbracket\in\mathcal F.
$$

Since

$$
\llbracket r=s\rrbracket\cap\llbracket s=t\rrbracket
\subseteq
\llbracket r=t\rrbracket,
$$

and \(\mathcal F\) is closed upward, \(\llbracket r=t\rrbracket\in\mathcal F\). Hence \(r\sim t\).

\end{proof}

\begin{definition}

The equivalence class of \(r\in\mathbb R^{\mathbb N}\) under \(\sim\) is denoted by

$$
[r]=\{s\in\mathbb R^{\mathbb N}:r\sim s\}.
$$

\end{definition}

\begin{definition}

For \(r,s\in\mathbb R^{\mathbb N}\), define addition and multiplication on \({}^\ast\mathbb R\) by

$$
[r]+[s]=[r+s],
\qquad
[r]\cdot[s]=[r\cdot s],
$$

where \((r+s)_n=r_n+s_n\) and \((r\cdot s)_n=r_ns_n\).

We define the order relation by

$$
[r]<[s]
\iff
\llbracket r<s\rrbracket\in\mathcal F.
$$

\end{definition}

These definitions are well-defined. If \(r\sim r'\) and \(s\sim s'\), then \(r+s\sim r'+s'\) and \(r\cdot s\sim r'\cdot s'\). Similarly,

$$
[r]<[s]\iff[r']<[s'].
$$

\begin{theorem}[Almost-All Criterion]

For sequences \(r=(r_n)\), \(s=(s_n)\), and \(t=(t_n)\), we have

$$
[r]=[s]
\iff
r_n=s_n
\quad\text{for \(\mathcal F\)-almost all }n,
$$

$$
[r]<[s]
\iff
r_n<s_n
\quad\text{for \(\mathcal F\)-almost all }n,
$$

$$
[r]+[s]=[t]
\iff
r_n+s_n=t_n
\quad\text{for \(\mathcal F\)-almost all }n,
$$

and

$$
[r]\cdot[s]=[t]
\iff
r_ns_n=t_n
\quad\text{for \(\mathcal F\)-almost all }n.
$$

The same principle holds for many other mathematical statements and is the basis of the \textbf{Transfer Principle}.

\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}

The structure

$$
({}^\ast\mathbb R,+,\cdot,<)
$$

is an ordered field with zero \([0]\) and unity \([1]\).

\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}

The ring properties follow directly from the corresponding properties of \(\mathbb R\). In particular, the additive inverse of \([r]\) is \(-[r]=[-r]\).

It remains to show that every nonzero element has a multiplicative inverse. Suppose \([r]\neq[0]\). Then \(\llbracket r=0\rrbracket\notin\mathcal F\). Since \(\mathcal F\) is an ultrafilter,

$$
J=\{n\in\mathbb N:r_n\neq0\}\in\mathcal F.
$$

Define

$$
s_n=
\begin{cases}
\dfrac1{r_n},&n\in J,\\
0,&n\notin J.
\end{cases}
$$

Then \(\llbracket r\cdot s=1\rrbracket=J\in\mathcal F\), so \([r]\cdot[s]=[1]\). Thus \([s]\) is the multiplicative inverse of \([r]\).

Finally, since \(\mathcal F\) is an ultrafilter, for any \(r,s\), exactly one of

$$
\llbracket r<s\rrbracket,\qquad
\llbracket r=s\rrbracket,\qquad
\llbracket s<r\rrbracket
$$

belongs to \(\mathcal F\). Hence the order on \({}^\ast\mathbb R\) is linear. Therefore

$$
({}^\ast\mathbb R,+,\cdot,<)
$$

is an ordered field.

\end{proof}

\begin{definition}

We identify every real number \(r\in\mathbb R\) with the equivalence class of the constant sequence \((r,r,r,\ldots)\). Thus

$$
r=[(r,r,r,\ldots)].
$$

Under this identification,

$$
\mathbb R\subseteq{}^\ast\mathbb R.
$$

\end{definition}

For example, consider

$$
\varepsilon=
\left[
\left(
1,\frac12,\frac13,\frac14,\ldots
\right)
\right].
$$

For every \(n\in\mathbb N\), \(0<\varepsilon<1/n\), and therefore \(\varepsilon\) is a positive infinitesimal.

Similarly,

$$
\omega=[(1,2,3,4,\ldots)]
$$

satisfies

$$
\omega>n
\qquad\forall n\in\mathbb N.
$$

Therefore \(\omega\) is a positive infinite hyperreal number.

\begin{definition}

Let \(f:A\to\mathbb R\), where \(A\subseteq\mathbb R\). The \textbf{Nonstandard Extension} of \(f\) is the function

$$
{}^\ast f:{}^\ast A\to{}^\ast\mathbb R
$$

defined by

$$
{}^\ast f([x_n])=[f(x_n)],
$$

where

$$
{}^\ast A=\{[x_n]:x_n\in A\}.
$$

\end{definition}

The definition is well-defined. If \([x_n]=[y_n]\), then

$$
\{n\in\mathbb N:x_n=y_n\}\in\mathcal F.
$$

Therefore

$$
\{n\in\mathbb N:f(x_n)=f(y_n)\}\in\mathcal F,
$$

so \([f(x_n)]=[f(y_n)]\). Thus the value of \({}^\ast f([x_n])\) does not depend on the representative chosen for \([x_n]\).

Moreover, if \(x\in A\) is standard, then

$$
{}^\ast f(x)=f(x).
$$

Indeed,

$$
{}^\ast f([(x,x,x,\ldots)])
=[(f(x),f(x),f(x),\ldots)]
=f(x).
$$

Therefore \({}^\ast f\) is genuinely an extension of \(f\).

The Almost-All Criterion is the fundamental reason why this construction allows ordinary statements about real numbers to be extended to nonstandard numbers. For example, if \(f(x)=x^2\) and \(\omega=[(1,2,3,\ldots)]\), then

$$
{}^\ast f(\omega)=[(1,4,9,\ldots)].
$$

Thus expressions such as

$$
{}^\ast f(a+\varepsilon)
$$

are well-defined even when \(a+\varepsilon\notin\mathbb R\).

This is the construction that will allow us to formulate limits, continuity, derivatives, and integrals using infinitesimals.
\section{(Un)limited, Infinitesimal, and Appreciable Numbers}

A hyperreal number \(b\in{}^\ast\mathbb R\) is called:

\begin{itemize}

\item \textbf{limited} if \(r<b<s\) for some \(r,s\in\mathbb R\);

\item \textbf{positive unlimited} if \(r<b\) for every \(r\in\mathbb R\);

\item \textbf{negative unlimited} if \(b<r\) for every \(r\in\mathbb R\);

\item \textbf{unlimited} if it is positive unlimited or negative unlimited;

\item \textbf{positive infinitesimal} if \(0<b<r\) for every \(r\in\mathbb R_{>0}\);

\item \textbf{negative infinitesimal} if \(r<b<0\) for every \(r\in\mathbb R_{<0}\);

\item \textbf{infinitesimal} if it is positive infinitesimal, negative infinitesimal, or \(0\);

\item \textbf{appreciable} if it is limited but not infinitesimal.

\end{itemize}

Thus all real numbers and all infinitesimals are limited. The only infinitesimal real number is \(0\), while every other real number is appreciable. An appreciable number is therefore a hyperreal number that is neither infinitely small nor infinitely large.

The above definitions can also be expressed using the natural numbers. For \(b\in{}^\ast\mathbb R\),

$$
b\text{ is limited}
\iff
|b|<n
\quad\text{for some }n\in\mathbb N,
$$

$$
b\text{ is unlimited}
\iff
|b|>n
\quad\forall n\in\mathbb N,
$$

$$
b\text{ is infinitesimal}
\iff
|b|<\frac1n
\quad\forall n\in\mathbb N,
$$

and

$$
b\text{ is appreciable}
\iff
\frac1n<|b|<m
\quad\text{for some }n,m\in\mathbb N.
$$

In particular, if \(\varepsilon\neq0\) is an infinitesimal, then

$$
|\varepsilon|<\frac1n
\qquad\forall n\in\mathbb N.
$$

For example,

$$
\varepsilon=
\left[
\left(
1,\frac12,\frac13,\ldots
\right)
\right]
$$

is a positive infinitesimal, while

$$
\omega=[(1,2,3,\ldots)]
$$

is a positive unlimited hyperreal number.

We denote the set of unlimited hypernaturals by

$$
{}^\ast\mathbb N_\infty
=
{}^\ast\mathbb N-\mathbb N.
$$

Similarly, we denote the sets of positive and negative unlimited hyperreals by

$$
{}^\ast\mathbb R_\infty^+
=
\{x\in{}^\ast\mathbb R:x\text{ is positive unlimited}\}
$$

and

$$
{}^\ast\mathbb R_\infty^-
=
\{x\in{}^\ast\mathbb R:x\text{ is negative unlimited}\}.
$$

This notation may be adapted to an arbitrary subset \(X\subseteq{}^\ast\mathbb R\). For example,

$$
X_\infty
=
\{x\in X:x\text{ is unlimited}\},
$$

$$
X^+
=
\{x\in X:x>0\},
\qquad
X^-
=
\{x\in X:x<0\}.
$$

We will also use

$$
\mathcal L
=
\{x\in{}^\ast\mathbb R:x\text{ is limited}\}
$$

for the set of all limited hyperreal numbers, and

$$
\mathcal I
=
\{x\in{}^\ast\mathbb R:x\text{ is infinitesimal}\}
$$

for the set of all infinitesimals.

\section{Arithmetic of Hyperreals}

Let \(\varepsilon,\delta\) be infinitesimals, let \(b,c\) be appreciable, and let \(H,K\) be unlimited hyperreal numbers. Then the following properties hold.

\textbf{Sums:} \(\varepsilon+\delta\) is infinitesimal, \(b+\varepsilon\) is appreciable, \(b+c\) is limited (possibly infinitesimal), and \(H+\varepsilon\) and \(H+b\) are unlimited.

\textbf{Opposites:}

$$
-\varepsilon\text{ is infinitesimal},
\qquad
-b\text{ is appreciable},
\qquad
-H\text{ is unlimited}.
$$

\textbf{Products:} \(\varepsilon\delta\) and \(\varepsilon b\) are infinitesimal, \(bc\) is appreciable, and \(bH\) and \(HK\) are unlimited.

\textbf{Reciprocals:} If \(\varepsilon\neq0\), then \(1/\varepsilon\) is unlimited. If \(b\neq0\), then \(1/b\) is appreciable. If \(H\neq0\), then \(1/H\) is infinitesimal.

\textbf{Quotients:} If \(\varepsilon,\delta\) are nonzero infinitesimals, then \(\varepsilon/\delta\) may be infinitesimal, appreciable, or unlimited. If \(b\neq0\) is appreciable and \(\varepsilon\) is infinitesimal, then \(\varepsilon/b\) is infinitesimal. If \(b,c\neq0\) are appreciable, then \(b/c\) is appreciable. If \(H\) is unlimited and \(b\neq0\) is appreciable, then \(H/b\) is unlimited. If \(H\neq0\) is unlimited and \(b\) is appreciable, then \(b/H\) is infinitesimal. If \(H,K\neq0\) are unlimited, then \(H/K\) may be infinitesimal, appreciable, or unlimited.

\textbf{Roots:} If \(\varepsilon>0\) is infinitesimal, then \(\sqrt{\varepsilon}\) is infinitesimal. If \(b>0\) is appreciable, then \(\sqrt b\) is appreciable. If \(H>0\) is unlimited, then \(\sqrt H\) is unlimited.

\textbf{Indeterminate Forms:} The expressions

$$
\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta},
\qquad
\frac{H}{K},
\qquad
\varepsilon H,
\qquad
H+K
$$

do not have a determined classification from the information given alone.

For example, if \(\varepsilon=1/\omega\), then

$$
\varepsilon\omega=1,
$$

which is appreciable. On the other hand, if \(\varepsilon=1/\sqrt{\omega}\), then

$$
\varepsilon\omega=\sqrt{\omega},
$$

which is unlimited. Thus arithmetic involving unlimited and infinitesimal numbers must be considered carefully.

In \({}^\ast\mathbb R\), the terminology is interpreted as follows:

\begin{itemize}

\item \textbf{Finite} means limited (bounded by a real number).

\item \textbf{Infinite} means unlimited (larger in magnitude than every real number).

\end{itemize}

This distinction is relative to \(\mathbb R\) inside \({}^\ast\mathbb R\): a number may be finite in this sense while still being nonstandard.

\section{Halos, Galaxies, and Real Comparisons}

Let \(x,y\in{}^\ast\mathbb R\).

\begin{definition}[Infinitesimal Closeness]

We say that \(x\) is \emph{infinitely close} to \(y\), and write

$$
x\approx y,
$$

if \(x-y\) is infinitesimal.

\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Halo]

The \emph{halo} of \(x\in{}^\ast\mathbb R\) is the set

$$
\operatorname{hal}(x)
=
\{y\in{}^\ast\mathbb R:y\approx x\}.
$$

\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Galaxy]

The \emph{galaxy} of \(x\in{}^\ast\mathbb R\) is the set

$$
\operatorname{gal}(x)
=
\{y\in{}^\ast\mathbb R:x-y\text{ is limited}\}.
$$

\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}

Every halo is contained in a galaxy.

\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}

If \(y\in\operatorname{hal}(x)\), then \(x-y\) is infinitesimal, hence limited. Therefore \(y\in\operatorname{gal}(x)\).

\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}

For standard \(r,s\in\mathbb R\),

$$
r\approx s
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
r=s.
$$

\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}

If \(r\approx s\), then \(r-s\) is a real infinitesimal, hence \(r-s=0\). Therefore \(r=s\).

\end{proof}
\section{Standard Parts}

\begin{theorem}

Every limited hyperreal $b$ is infinitely close to exactly one real number. This real number is called the \textbf{**standard part**} of $b$ and is denoted by $\operatorname{st}(b)$. Thus
$$
b\approx\operatorname{st}(b).
$$

\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}

Let
$$
A=\{r\in\mathbb R:r<b\}.
$$
Since $b$ is limited, there exist real numbers $r,s$ such that $r<b<s$. Therefore $A$ is nonempty and bounded above by $s$. By the completeness of $\mathbb R$, $A$ has a least upper bound
$$
c=\sup A\in\mathbb R.
$$

We show that $b\approx c$. Let $\epsilon>0$ be any positive real number. Since $c$ is an upper bound of $A$, we cannot have $c+\epsilon\in A$. Therefore
$$
b\leq c+\epsilon.
$$
On the other hand, $c-\epsilon$ cannot be an upper bound of $A$, since $c$ is the least upper bound. Hence there exists some $a\in A$ such that $c-\epsilon<a<b$. Therefore
$$
c-\epsilon<b.
$$
Thus
$$
c-\epsilon<b\leq c+\epsilon,
$$
and consequently
$$
|b-c|\leq\epsilon.
$$
Since this holds for every positive real $\epsilon$, we have
$$
b\approx c.
$$

It remains to prove uniqueness. Suppose $b\approx c'$ for some $c'\in\mathbb R$. Since also $b\approx c$, we obtain
$$
c\approx c'.
$$
But $c,c'\in\mathbb R$, and the only real infinitesimal is $0$. Hence
$$
c=c'.
$$
Therefore there exists exactly one real number infinitely close to $b$. We define this number to be
$$
\operatorname{st}(b)=c.
$$

\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}

Let $b,c$ be limited hyperreal numbers and let $n\in\mathbb N$. Then
$$
\operatorname{st}(b\pm c)=\operatorname{st}(b)\pm\operatorname{st}(c),
$$
$$
\operatorname{st}(bc)=\operatorname{st}(b)\operatorname{st}(c),
$$
and
$$
\operatorname{st}\left(\frac bc\right)=\frac{\operatorname{st}(b)}{\operatorname{st}(c)}
\qquad\text{if }\operatorname{st}(c)\neq0.
$$
Also,
$$
\operatorname{st}(b^n)=\operatorname{st}(b)^n,
$$
$$
\operatorname{st}(|b|)=|\operatorname{st}(b)|,
$$
and, if $b\geq0$,
$$
\operatorname{st}(\sqrt b)=\sqrt{\operatorname{st}(b)}.
$$
Furthermore,
$$
b\leq c\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\operatorname{st}(b)\leq\operatorname{st}(c).
$$

\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}

Exercise.

\end{proof}

These properties show that the standard-part map
$$
\operatorname{st}:\mathcal L\to\mathbb R,
$$
where $\mathcal L$ denotes the set of all limited hyperreal numbers, is an order-preserving ring homomorphism onto $\mathbb R$. Its kernel is precisely the set of infinitesimals:
$$
\ker(\operatorname{st})=\{b\in\mathcal L:\operatorname{st}(b)=0\}=\mathcal I.
$$

Thus two limited hyperreal numbers have the same standard part exactly when they are infinitely close:
$$
\operatorname{st}(b)=\operatorname{st}(c)\iff b\approx c.
$$

Equivalently, the equivalence classes of the relation $\approx$ on the limited hyperreals are precisely the sets
$$
\operatorname{hal}(b)=\{c\in\mathcal L:c\approx b\},
$$
called the \textbf{**halos**} of $b$.

Therefore the quotient of the ring of limited hyperreals by the ideal of infinitesimals is isomorphic to the real number field.

\begin{theorem}

The quotient ring
$$
\mathcal L/\mathcal I
$$
is isomorphic to $\mathbb R$ by the correspondence
$$
\operatorname{hal}(b)\longmapsto\operatorname{st}(b).
$$
In particular,
$$
\mathcal I
$$
is a maximal ideal of the ring $\mathcal L$.

\end{theorem}

\section{Shadows and Completeness}

\begin{lemma}

Every limited $x\in{}^\ast\mathbb R$ is infinitely close to a unique real number.

\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}

This follows from the Standard Part Theorem.

\end{proof}


\chapter{New beginning for calculus }
\section{Limits}



First we have to fix a ``Main variable''. It is almost all the time $x$.

(sometimes it is ``$t$'').

\begin{definition}
Let $ f:A\to\mathbb{R} $
where $A\subseteq\mathbb{R}$, and let $a$ be a limit point of $A$.
We say that $L\in\mathbb{R}$ is the \textbf{Limit} of $f$ as $x$
approaches $a$ if

\[
\forall\varepsilon>0\ \exists\delta>0\
\forall x\in A
\left(
0<|x-a|<\delta
\to
|f(x)-L|<\varepsilon
\right).
\]

We write
\[
\lim_{x\to a}f(x)=L.
\]

\end{definition}




\begin{theorem}[Infinitesimal Characterization of Limits]
Let$
f:A\to\mathbb{R},
\qquad
A\subseteq\mathbb{R},
$and let $a$ be a limit point of $A$.Then$\lim_{x\to a}f(x)=L$
if and only if, for every nonzero infinitesimal$\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}(\mathrm{Ord})$
such that
$a+\varepsilon\in A,$
we have
${}^*f(a+\varepsilon)\approx L.$
Equivalently,
$$
\lim_{x\to a}f(x)=L
\leftrightarrow
\operatorname{st}
\left(
{}^*f(a+\varepsilon)
\right)
=L.
$$

\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}

Suppose first that $\lim_{x\to a}f(x)=L$. Let $\varepsilon$ be a nonzero infinitesimal such that $a+\varepsilon\in A$. We prove that

$$
{}^*f(a+\varepsilon)\approx L.
$$

Let $\varepsilon>0$. By the definition of the limit, there exists $\delta>0$ such that $0<|x-a|<\delta$ implies $|f(x)-L|<\varepsilon$. Since $\varepsilon$ is infinitesimal, $|\varepsilon|<\delta$. Therefore

$$
0<|(a+\varepsilon)-a|<\delta.
$$

Hence

$$
|{}^*f(a+\varepsilon)-L|<\varepsilon.
$$

Since this holds for every $\varepsilon>0$,

$$
{}^*f(a+\varepsilon)\approx L.
$$

Conversely, suppose that for every nonzero infinitesimal $\varepsilon$ such that $a+\varepsilon\in{}^*A$, we have ${}^*f(a+\varepsilon)\approx L$. Suppose, toward a contradiction, that $\lim_{x\to a}f(x)\neq L$. Then there exists $\varepsilon>0$ such that for every $\delta>0$ there exists $x\in A$ such that $0<|x-a|<\delta$ and $|f(x)-L|\geq\varepsilon$.

In particular, for every $n\in\mathbb{N}$ there exists $x_n\in A$ such that

$$
0<|x_n-a|<\frac1n
$$

and

$$
|f(x_n)-L|\geq\varepsilon.
$$

By the nonstandard extension, taking an infinite index $N$, we obtain

$$
0<|x_N-a|<\frac1N.
$$

Since $N$ is infinite, $1/N$ is infinitesimal. Therefore $\varepsilon:=x_N-a$ is a nonzero infinitesimal. But

$$
|{}^*f(a+\varepsilon)-L|
=
|f(x_N)-L|
\geq\varepsilon,
$$

so ${}^*f(a+\varepsilon)\not\approx L$, contradicting the assumption. Therefore $\lim_{x\to a}f(x)=L$.

Finally,

$$
{}^*f(a+\varepsilon)\approx L
$$

is equivalent to

$$
\operatorname{st}\bigl({}^*f(a+\varepsilon)\bigr)=L.
$$

\end{proof}


\begin{theorem}[Uniqueness of Limit]

If $\lim_{x\to a}f(x)=L$ and $\lim_{x\to a}f(x)=M$, then $L=M$.

\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}

Let $\varepsilon$ be a nonzero infinitesimal such that $a+\varepsilon\in{}^*A$. By the Infinitesimal Characterization of Limits,

$$
\operatorname{st}\bigl({}^*f(a+\varepsilon)\bigr)=L
$$

and

$$
\operatorname{st}\bigl({}^*f(a+\varepsilon)\bigr)=M.
$$

Hence

$$
L=M.
$$

\end{proof}


\begin{theorem}[Limit Laws]

Suppose $\lim_{x\to a}f(x)=L$ and $\lim_{x\to a}g(x)=M$. Then $\lim_{x\to a}(f(x)+g(x))=L+M$, $\lim_{x\to a}(f(x)-g(x))=L-M$, and $\lim_{x\to a}f(x)g(x)=LM$. If $M\neq0$, then $\lim_{x\to a}\frac{f(x)}{g(x)}=\frac{L}{M}$. Moreover, for every $c\in\mathbb{R}$, $\lim_{x\to a}cf(x)=cL$.

\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}

Let $\varepsilon$ be a nonzero infinitesimal such that $a+\varepsilon\in A$. By the Infinitesimal Characterization of Limits,

$$
{}^*f(a+\varepsilon)\approx L
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
{}^*g(a+\varepsilon)\approx M.
$$

Therefore, by the Standard Part Theorem,

$$
\operatorname{st}\bigl({}^*f(a+\varepsilon)+{}^*g(a+\varepsilon)\bigr)
=L+M.
$$

Similarly,

$$
\operatorname{st}\bigl({}^*f(a+\varepsilon)-{}^*g(a+\varepsilon)\bigr)
=L-M.
$$

For the product,

$$
\operatorname{st}\bigl({}^*f(a+\varepsilon){}^*g(a+\varepsilon)\bigr)
=
\operatorname{st}({}^*f(a+\varepsilon))
\operatorname{st}({}^*g(a+\varepsilon))
=LM.
$$

If $M\neq0$, then $\operatorname{st}({}^*g(a+\varepsilon))=M\neq0$, and hence ${}^*g(a+\varepsilon)\neq0$. Therefore,

$$
\operatorname{st}\left(
\frac{{}^*f(a+\varepsilon)}
{{}^*g(a+\varepsilon)}
\right)
=
\frac{\operatorname{st}({}^*f(a+\varepsilon))}
{\operatorname{st}({}^*g(a+\varepsilon))}
=
\frac{L}{M}.
$$

Finally, for every $c\in\mathbb{R}$,

$$
\operatorname{st}\bigl(c{}^*f(a+\varepsilon)\bigr)
=
c\operatorname{st}({}^*f(a+\varepsilon))
=cL.
$$

Thus all the stated limit laws hold.

\end{proof}


\begin{theorem}[Squeeze Theorem]

Suppose that for all $x$ sufficiently close to $a$, $f(x)\leq g(x)\leq h(x)$. If $\lim_{x\to a}f(x)=L$ and $\lim_{x\to a}h(x)=L$, then $\lim_{x\to a}g(x)=L$.

\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}

Let $\varepsilon$ be a nonzero infinitesimal such that $a+\varepsilon\in{}^*A$. Since the inequality holds for all $x$ sufficiently close to $a$, by the Transfer Principle,

$$
{}^*f(a+\varepsilon)
\leq
{}^*g(a+\varepsilon)
\leq
{}^*h(a+\varepsilon).
$$

By the Infinitesimal Characterization of Limits,

$$
{}^*f(a+\varepsilon)\approx L
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
{}^*h(a+\varepsilon)\approx L.
$$

Hence, by the Squeeze Principle for infinitesimals,

$$
{}^*g(a+\varepsilon)\approx L.
$$

Therefore, by the Infinitesimal Characterization of Limits,

$$
\lim_{x\to a}g(x)=L.
$$

\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Absolute Value]

If $\lim_{x\to a}f(x)=L$, then $\lim_{x\to a}|f(x)|=|L|$.

\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}

Since ${}^*f(a+\varepsilon)\approx L$,

$$
\left|
|{}^*f(a+\varepsilon)|-|L|
\right|
\leq
|{}^*f(a+\varepsilon)-L|.
$$

Therefore,

$$
|{}^*f(a+\varepsilon)|\approx|L|.
$$

Hence, by the Infinitesimal Characterization of Limits,

$$
\lim_{x\to a}|f(x)|=|L|.
$$

\end{proof}
\begin{theorem}[Composition of Limits]

Let $\lim_{x\to a}g(x)=b$ and $\lim_{y\to b}f(y)=L$. Suppose that $g(x)\neq b$ whenever $x$ is sufficiently close to $a$ and $x\neq a$. Then $\lim_{x\to a}f(g(x))=L$.

\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}

Let $\varepsilon$ be a nonzero infinitesimal such that $a+\varepsilon\in{}^*A$. Since $\lim_{x\to a}g(x)=b$,

$$
{}^*g(a+\varepsilon)\approx b.
$$

By the Transfer Principle and the assumption on $g$, we have ${}^*g(a+\varepsilon)\neq b$. Therefore, by the Infinitesimal Characterization of $\lim_{y\to b}f(y)=L$,

$$
{}^*f\bigl({}^*g(a+\varepsilon)\bigr)\approx L.
$$

Since ${}^*f\bigl({}^*g(a+\varepsilon)\bigr)={}^*(f\circ g)(a+\varepsilon)$,

$$
{}^*(f\circ g)(a+\varepsilon)\approx L.
$$

Hence, by the Infinitesimal Characterization of Limits,

$$
\lim_{x\to a}f(g(x))=L.
$$

\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Polynomial Limit]

Let $p(x)=a_0+a_1x+\cdots+a_nx^n$. Then $\lim_{x\to a}p(x)=p(a)$.

\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}

By the Limit Laws,

$$
\lim_{x\to a}x^k=a^k
$$

for every $k\in\mathbb{N}$. Therefore,

$$
\lim_{x\to a}p(x)
=
a_0+a_1a+\cdots+a_na^n
=
p(a).
$$

\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Rational Function Limit]

Let $r(x)=\frac{p(x)}{q(x)}$ where $p$ and $q$ are polynomials. If $q(a)\neq0$, then

$$
\lim_{x\to a}r(x)=\frac{p(a)}{q(a)}.
$$

\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}

Let $\varepsilon$ be a nonzero infinitesimal such that $a+\varepsilon\in{}^*A$. By the Polynomial Limit Theorem,

$$
\operatorname{st}\bigl({}^*p(a+\varepsilon)\bigr)=p(a)
$$

and

$$
\operatorname{st}\bigl({}^*q(a+\varepsilon)\bigr)=q(a)\neq0.
$$

Hence ${}^*q(a+\varepsilon)\neq0$. By the Standard Part Theorem,

$$
\operatorname{st}\left(
\frac{{}^*p(a+\varepsilon)}
{{}^*q(a+\varepsilon)}
\right)
=
\frac{
\operatorname{st}({}^*p(a+\varepsilon))
}{
\operatorname{st}({}^*q(a+\varepsilon))
}
=
\frac{p(a)}{q(a)}.
$$

Therefore,

$$
\lim_{x\to a}r(x)=\frac{p(a)}{q(a)}.
$$

\end{proof}


\begin{definition}
Let

$$
f:A\to\mathbb{R}
$$

and let $a$ be a limit point of $A$.

We say that $f$ has the \textbf{Right Limit} $L$ at $a$ if

$$
\forall\varepsilon>0\ \exists\delta>0\ \forall x\in A
\left(
a<x<a+\delta
\Longrightarrow
|f(x)-L|<\varepsilon
\right).
$$

We write

$$
\lim_{x\to a^+}f(x)=L.
$$

Similarly, we say that $f$ has the \textbf{Left Limit} $L$ at $a$ if

$$
\forall\varepsilon>0\ \exists\delta>0\ \forall x\in A
\left(
a-\delta<x<a
\Longrightarrow
|f(x)-L|<\varepsilon
\right).
$$

We write

$$
\lim_{x\to a^-}f(x)=L.
$$

\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Infinitesimal Characterization of One-Sided Limits]
We have

$$
\lim_{x\to a^+}f(x)=L
$$

if and only if

$$
{}^*f(a+\varepsilon)\approx L
$$

for every positive infinitesimal $\varepsilon$ such that

$$
a+\varepsilon\in A.
$$

Similarly,

$$
\lim_{x\to a^-}f(x)=L
$$

if and only if

$$
{}^*f(a+\varepsilon)\approx L
$$

for every negative infinitesimal $\varepsilon$ such that

$$
a+\varepsilon\in A.
$$

\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}[One-Sided Limits]
The following are equivalent:

$$
\lim_{x\to a}f(x)=L
$$

and

$$
\lim_{x\to a^-}f(x)
=
\lim_{x\to a^+}f(x)
=
L.
$$

\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
If

$$
\lim_{x\to a}f(x)=L,
$$

then every nonzero infinitesimal is either positive or negative.
Therefore

$$
{}^*f(a+\varepsilon)\approx L
$$

for both positive and negative infinitesimal $\varepsilon$.

Hence

$$
\lim_{x\to a^-}f(x)=L
$$

and

$$
\lim_{x\to a^+}f(x)=L.
$$

Conversely, suppose

$$
\lim_{x\to a^-}f(x)=L
$$

and

$$
\lim_{x\to a^+}f(x)=L.
$$

Every nonzero infinitesimal $\varepsilon$ is either positive or negative.
Therefore

$$
{}^*f(a+\varepsilon)\approx L.
$$

Hence

$$
\lim_{x\to a}f(x)=L.
$$

\end{proof}

\begin{definition}
We say that

$$
f(x)
$$

has the \textbf{Limit $+\infty$} at $a$ if

$$
\forall M>0\ \exists\delta>0\ \forall x\in A
\left(
0<|x-a|<\delta
\Longrightarrow
f(x)>M
\right).
$$

We write

$$
\lim_{x\to a}f(x)=+\infty.
$$

Similarly,

$$
\lim_{x\to a}f(x)=-\infty
$$

if

$$
\forall M>0\ \exists\delta>0\ \forall x\in A
\left(
0<|x-a|<\delta
\Longrightarrow
f(x)<-M
\right).
$$

\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Infinitesimal Characterization of Infinite Limits]
We have

$$
\lim_{x\to a}f(x)=+\infty
$$

if and only if

$$
{}^*f(a+\varepsilon)
$$

is positive infinite for every nonzero infinitesimal $\varepsilon$ such that

$$
a+\varepsilon\in A.
$$

Similarly,

$$
\lim_{x\to a}f(x)=-\infty
$$

if and only if

$$
{}^*f(a+\varepsilon)
$$

is negative infinite for every such $\varepsilon$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{definition}
Let

$$
f:A\to\mathbb{R}
$$

where $A\subseteq\mathbb{R}$ is unbounded above.

We say that

$$
\lim_{x\to\infty}f(x)=L
$$

if

$$
\forall\varepsilon>0\ \exists M>0\ \forall x\in A
\left(
x>M
\Longrightarrow
|f(x)-L|<\varepsilon
\right).
$$

\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Infinitesimal Characterization of Limits at Infinity]
We have

$$
\lim_{x\to\infty}f(x)=L
$$

if and only if

$$
f(H)\approx L
$$

for every positive infinite

$$
H\in\mathbb{R}(\mathrm{Ord})
$$

such that

$$
H\in A.
$$

Equivalently,

$$
\lim_{x\to\infty}f(x)=L
\iff
\operatorname{st}(f(H))=L.
$$

\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Suppose

$$
\lim_{x\to\infty}f(x)=L.
$$

Let $H$ be positive infinite.

Suppose that

$$
f(H)\not\approx L.
$$

Then there exists

$$
\varepsilon>0
$$

such that

$$
|f(H)-L|\geq\varepsilon.
$$

By the definition of the limit, there exists $M>0$ such that

$$
x>M
$$

implies

$$
|f(x)-L|<\varepsilon.
$$

Since $H$ is positive infinite,

$$
H>M.
$$

Therefore

$$
|f(H)-L|<\varepsilon,
$$

a contradiction.

Hence

$$
f(H)\approx L.
$$

Conversely, suppose that

$$
f(H)\approx L
$$

for every positive infinite $H\in A$.

Suppose that

$$
\lim_{x\to\infty}f(x)\neq L.
$$

Then there exists $\varepsilon>0$ such that for every $M>0$ there exists
$x\in A$ satisfying

$$
x>M
$$

and

$$
|f(x)-L|\geq\varepsilon.
$$

Thus, for every $n\in\mathbb{N}$, there exists $x_n\in A$ such that

$$
x_n>n
$$

and

$$
|f(x_n)-L|\geq\varepsilon.
$$

Taking an infinite index $N$ gives

$$
x_N>N.
$$

Hence $x_N$ is positive infinite. But

$$
|f(x_N)-L|\geq\varepsilon,
$$

so

$$
f(x_N)\not\approx L,
$$

contradicting the assumption.

Therefore

$$
\lim_{x\to\infty}f(x)=L.
$$

\end{proof}

\begin{definition}
We say that

$$
\lim_{x\to\infty}f(x)=+\infty
$$

if

$$
\forall M>0\ \exists N>0\ \forall x\in A
\left(
x>N
\Longrightarrow
f(x)>M
\right).
$$

Similarly,

$$
\lim_{x\to\infty}f(x)=-\infty
$$

if

$$
\forall M>0\ \exists N>0\ \forall x\in A
\left(
x>N
\Longrightarrow
f(x)<-M
\right).
$$

\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Infinitesimal Characterization of Infinite Limits at Infinity]
We have

$$
\lim_{x\to\infty}f(x)=+\infty
$$

if and only if

$$
f(H)
$$

is positive infinite for every positive infinite $H\in A$.

Similarly,

$$
\lim_{x\to\infty}f(x)=-\infty
$$

if and only if

$$
f(H)
$$

is negative infinite for every positive infinite $H\in A$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}[Sequential Characterization of Limits]
Let

$$
f:A\to\mathbb{R}
$$

and let $a$ be a limit point of $A$.

Then

$$
\lim_{x\to a}f(x)=L
$$

if and only if, for every sequence

$$
(x_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
$$

in $A$ such that

$$
x_n\neq a
$$

and

$$
\lim_{n\to\infty}x_n=a,
$$

we have

$$
\lim_{n\to\infty}f(x_n)=L.
$$

\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}[Limit of a Monotone Function]
Suppose that $f$ is monotone on an interval.

Then the one-sided limits of $f$ exist at every interior point of the
interval, possibly taking the values $+\infty$ or $-\infty$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}[Limit of Powers]
If

$$
\lim_{x\to a}f(x)=L,
$$

then for every

$$
n\in\mathbb{N},
$$

$$
\lim_{x\to a}f(x)^n=L^n.
$$

\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
This follows by repeated application of the Limit Laws.
\end{proof}

```

One thing I would **not** put in this section yet is L'Hôpital's rule, Taylor's theorem, or anything involving derivatives. Those belong after your Derivatives section.

The most important conceptual result of this section is the chain

\[
\boxed{
\lim_{x\to a}f(x)=L
\iff
{}^*f(a+\varepsilon)\approx L
\iff
\operatorname{st}({}^*f(a+\varepsilon))=L
}
\]

which you can then reuse directly in your definitions of **continuity, derivative, and eventually the Riemann integral**.
```



\begin{theorem}[Infinitesimal Characterization of Limits]
Let
\[
f:A\to\mathbb{R}
\]
and let $a$ be a limit point of $A$.

Then
\[
\lim_{x\to a}f(x)=L
\]
if and only if, for every nonzero infinitesimal
\[
\varepsilon\footnotemark \in\mathbb{R}(\mathrm{Ord})
\]

\footnotetext{\label{fn:infinitesimal}
In this book, the symbol $\varepsilon$ denotes the distinguished
infinitesimal traditionally used in nonstandard analysis. The symbol
$\varepsilon$ is reserved for an arbitrary (variable) infinitesimal and Real positives.}


such that
\[
a+\varepsilon\in A,
\]
we have
\[
{}^*f(a+\varepsilon)\approx L.
\]

Equivalently,
\[
\lim_{x\to a}f(x)=L
\leftrightarrow
\operatorname{st}
\bigl(
{}^*f(a+\varepsilon)
\bigr)
=L
\]
for every nonzero infinitesimal $\varepsilon$ such that
$a+\varepsilon\in A$.
\end{theorem}


\begin{proof}
Suppose first that
\[
\lim_{x\to a}f(x)=L.
\]

We must prove that
\[
{}^*f(a+\varepsilon)\approx L
\]
for every nonzero infinitesimal $\varepsilon$.

Assume, toward a contradiction, that there exists a nonzero
infinitesimal $\varepsilon$ such that
\[
{}^*f(a+\varepsilon)\not\approx L.
\]

Then
\[
|{}^*f(a+\varepsilon)-L|
\]
is not infinitesimal. Hence there exists
\[
\delta_0>0
\]
such that
\[
|{}^*f(a+\varepsilon)-L|\geq\delta_0.
\]

Since
\[
\lim_{x\to a}f(x)=L,
\]
there exists $\eta>0$ such that
\[
0<|x-a|<\eta
\]
implies
\[
|f(x)-L|<\delta_0.
\]

But $\varepsilon$ is infinitesimal, so
\[
0<|\varepsilon|<\eta.
\]

Taking
\[
x=a+\varepsilon
\]
gives
\[
|{}^*f(a+\varepsilon)-L|<\delta_0,
\]
contradicting
\[
|{}^*f(a+\varepsilon)-L|\geq\delta_0.
\]

Therefore
\[
{}^*f(a+\varepsilon)\approx L.
\]

Conversely, suppose that for every nonzero infinitesimal $\varepsilon$,
\[
{}^*f(a+\varepsilon)\approx L.
\]

Suppose, toward a contradiction, that
\[
\lim_{x\to a}f(x)\neq L.
\]

Then, by the negation of the classical definition of limit, there exists
\[
\delta_0>0
\]
such that for every $\eta>0$ there exists
\[
x\in A
\]
satisfying
\[
0<|x-a|<\eta
\]
and
\[
|f(x)-L|\geq\delta_0.
\]

Using this statement for
\[
\eta=\frac1n
\]
for every $n\in\mathbb{N}$, we obtain points $x_n\in A$ such that
\[
0<|x_n-a|<\frac1n
\]
and
\[
|f(x_n)-L|\geq\delta_0.
\]

By the nonstandard extension, this sequence has an infinite-index
term $x_N$ satisfying
\[
0<|x_N-a|<\frac1N.
\]

Since $N$ is infinite,
\[
\frac1N
\]
is infinitesimal. Hence
\[
\varepsilon:=x_N-a
\]
is a nonzero infinitesimal.

But
\[
|{}^*f(a+\varepsilon)-L|
=
|f(x_N)-L|
\geq\delta_0,
\]
so
\[
{}^*f(a+\varepsilon)\not\approx L,
\]
contradicting our assumption.

Therefore
\[
\lim_{x\to a}f(x)=L.
\]

Finally,
\[
{}^*f(a+\varepsilon)\approx L
\]
is equivalent to
\[
\operatorname{st}({}^*f(a+\varepsilon))=L.
\]

Hence
\[
\lim_{x\to a}f(x)=L
\iff
\operatorname{st}
\bigl(
{}^*f(a+\varepsilon)
\bigr)
=L.
\]
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}
Let
\[
f:A\to\mathbb{R}
\]
where $A\subseteq\mathbb{R}$ is unbounded above.

We say that $L\in\mathbb{R}$ is the \textbf{Limit} of $f$ as $x$
approaches infinity if
\[
\forall\varepsilon>0\ \exists M>0\
\forall x\in A
\left(
x>M
\Longrightarrow
|f(x)-L|<\varepsilon
\right).
\]

We write
\[
\lim_{x\to\infty}f(x)=L.
\]

\end{definition}


\begin{definition}
Let
\[
x\in\mathbb{R}(\mathrm{Ord}).
\]

We say that $x$ is \textbf{Infinite} if
\[
|x|>n
\qquad
\forall n\in\mathbb{N}.
\]

If
\[
x>n
\qquad
\forall n\in\mathbb{N},
\]
we say that $x$ is a \textbf{Positive Infinite} number.

\end{definition}


\begin{theorem}[Infinitesimal Characterization of the Limit at Infinity]
Let
\[
f:A\to\mathbb{R}
\]
where $A\subseteq\mathbb{R}$ is unbounded above.

Then
\[
\lim_{x\to\infty}f(x)=L
\]
if and only if, for every positive infinite
\[
H\in\mathbb{R}(\mathrm{Ord})
\]
such that
\[
H\in A,
\]
we have
\[
f(H)\approx L.
\]

Equivalently,
\[
\lim_{x\to\infty}f(x)=L
\]
if and only if
\[
\operatorname{st}(f(H))=L
\]
for every positive infinite $H\in A$.
\end{theorem}


\begin{proof}
Suppose first that
\[
\lim_{x\to\infty}f(x)=L.
\]

Let $H$ be a positive infinite number.

We want to prove
\[
f(H)\approx L.
\]

Suppose, toward a contradiction, that
\[
f(H)\not\approx L.
\]

Then there exists
\[
\varepsilon>0
\]
such that
\[
|f(H)-L|\geq\varepsilon.
\]

Since
\[
\lim_{x\to\infty}f(x)=L,
\]
there exists $M>0$ such that
\[
x>M
\]
implies
\[
|f(x)-L|<\varepsilon.
\]

But $H$ is positive infinite, so
\[
H>M.
\]

Therefore
\[
|f(H)-L|<\varepsilon,
\]
which is a contradiction.

Hence
\[
f(H)\approx L.
\]

Conversely, suppose that for every positive infinite $H\in A$,
\[
f(H)\approx L.
\]

Suppose, toward a contradiction, that
\[
\lim_{x\to\infty}f(x)\neq L.
\]

Then there exists
\[
\varepsilon>0
\]
such that for every $M>0$ there exists
\[
x\in A
\]
satisfying
\[
x>M
\]
and
\[
|f(x)-L|\geq\varepsilon.
\]

In particular, for every
\[
n\in\mathbb{N},
\]
there exists $x_n\in A$ such that
\[
x_n>n
\]
and
\[
|f(x_n)-L|\geq\varepsilon.
\]

By the nonstandard extension, taking an infinite index $N$ gives
\[
x_N>N
\]
and
\[
|f(x_N)-L|\geq\varepsilon.
\]

Since $N$ is infinite,
\[
x_N
\]
is positive infinite.

Let
\[
H:=x_N.
\]

Then
\[
H
\]
is positive infinite, but
\[
|f(H)-L|\geq\varepsilon.
\]

Therefore
\[
f(H)\not\approx L,
\]
contradicting the assumption.

Hence
\[
\lim_{x\to\infty}f(x)=L.
\]

Finally,
\[
f(H)\approx L
\]
is equivalent to
\[
\operatorname{st}(f(H))=L.
\]

Therefore
\[
\lim_{x\to\infty}f(x)=L
\iff
\operatorname{st}(f(H))=L.
\]
\end{proof}

\section{Continuity}

First we have to fix a ``Main point''. It is almost all the time $a$.

\begin{definition}
Let
\[
f:A\to\mathbb{R}
\]
where $A\subseteq\mathbb{R}$, and let
\[
a\in A.
\]

We say that $f$ is \textbf{Continuous} at $a$ if
\[
\forall\varepsilon>0\ \exists\delta>0\
\forall x\in A
\left(
|x-a|<\delta
\Longrightarrow
|f(x)-f(a)|<\varepsilon
\right).
\]

\end{definition}


\begin{theorem}[Continuity and Limits]
Let
\[
f:A\to\mathbb{R}
\]
and let $a\in A$ be a limit point of $A$.

Then $f$ is continuous at $a$ if and only if
\[
\lim_{x\to a}f(x)=f(a).
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Suppose first that $f$ is continuous at $a$.

Then, by definition,
\[
\forall\varepsilon>0\ \exists\delta>0\
\forall x\in A
\left(
|x-a|<\delta
\Longrightarrow
|f(x)-f(a)|<\varepsilon
\right).
\]

Since $a$ is a limit point of $A$, this is precisely the definition of
\[
\lim_{x\to a}f(x)=f(a).
\]

Conversely, suppose
\[
\lim_{x\to a}f(x)=f(a).
\]

Then
\[
\forall\varepsilon>0\ \exists\delta>0\
\forall x\in A
\left(
0<|x-a|<\delta
\Longrightarrow
|f(x)-f(a)|<\varepsilon
\right).
\]

If
\[
x=a,
\]
then
\[
|f(x)-f(a)|=0<\varepsilon.
\]

Therefore
\[
|x-a|<\delta
\Longrightarrow
|f(x)-f(a)|<\varepsilon.
\]

Hence $f$ is continuous at $a$.
\end{proof}


\begin{theorem}[Infinitesimal Characterization of Continuity]
Let
\[
f:A\to\mathbb{R}
\]
and let
\[
a\in A.
\]

Then $f$ is continuous at $a$ if and only if, for every infinitesimal
\[
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}(\mathrm{Ord}),
\]
such that
\[
a+\varepsilon\in A,
\]
we have
\[
{}^*f(a+\varepsilon)\approx f(a).
\]

Equivalently,
\[
f\text{ is continuous at }a
\]
if and only if
\[
\operatorname{st}
\left(
{}^*f(a+\varepsilon)
\right)
=
f(a)
\]
for every infinitesimal $\varepsilon$ such that
$a+\varepsilon\in A$.
\end{theorem}


\begin{proof}
Suppose first that $f$ is continuous at $a$.

Let
\[
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}(\mathrm{Ord})
\]
be infinitesimal and suppose
\[
a+\varepsilon\in A.
\]

We want to prove
\[
{}^*f(a+\varepsilon)\approx f(a).
\]

Suppose, toward a contradiction, that
\[
{}^*f(a+\varepsilon)\not\approx f(a).
\]

Then there exists
\[
\varepsilon>0
\]
such that
\[
|{}^*f(a+\varepsilon)-f(a)|\geq\varepsilon.
\]

Since $f$ is continuous at $a$, there exists $\delta>0$ such that
\[
|x-a|<\delta
\]
implies
\[
|f(x)-f(a)|<\varepsilon.
\]

Since $\varepsilon$ is infinitesimal,
\[
|\varepsilon|<\delta.
\]

Therefore
\[
|(a+\varepsilon)-a|<\delta,
\]
and hence
\[
|{}^*f(a+\varepsilon)-f(a)|<\varepsilon,
\]
a contradiction.

Thus
\[
{}^*f(a+\varepsilon)\approx f(a).
\]

Conversely, suppose that for every infinitesimal $\varepsilon$,
\[
{}^*f(a+\varepsilon)\approx f(a).
\]

Suppose, toward a contradiction, that $f$ is not continuous at $a$.

Then there exists
\[
\varepsilon>0
\]
such that for every $\delta>0$ there exists
\[
x\in A
\]
such that
\[
|x-a|<\delta
\]
and
\[
|f(x)-f(a)|\geq\varepsilon.
\]

In particular, for every
\[
n\in\mathbb{N},
\]
there exists $x_n\in A$ such that
\[
|x_n-a|<\frac1n
\]
and
\[
|f(x_n)-f(a)|\geq\varepsilon.
\]

By the nonstandard extension, taking an infinite index $N$ gives
\[
|x_N-a|<\frac1N.
\]

Since $N$ is infinite,
\[
\frac1N
\]
is infinitesimal.

Therefore
\[
\varepsilon:=x_N-a
\]
is infinitesimal.

But
\[
|{}^*f(a+\varepsilon)-f(a)|
=
|f(x_N)-f(a)|
\geq\varepsilon,
\]
so
\[
{}^*f(a+\varepsilon)\not\approx f(a).
\]

This contradicts our assumption.

Therefore $f$ is continuous at $a$.

Finally,
\[
{}^*f(a+\varepsilon)\approx f(a)
\]
is equivalent to
\[
\operatorname{st}
\left(
{}^*f(a+\varepsilon)
\right)
=
f(a).
\]

Hence the two definitions are equivalent.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Algebra of Continuous Functions]
Let $f$ and $g$ be continuous at $a$. Then
\[
f+g,
\qquad
f-g,
\qquad
fg
\]
are continuous at $a$.

If
\[
g(a)\neq0,
\]
then
\[
\frac{f}{g}
\]
is continuous at $a$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Since $f$ and $g$ are continuous at $a$, for every infinitesimal
$\varepsilon$,
\[
{}^*f(a+\varepsilon)\approx f(a)
\]
and
\[
{}^*g(a+\varepsilon)\approx g(a).
\]

By the Limit Laws,
\[
{}^*f(a+\varepsilon)+{}^*g(a+\varepsilon)
\approx
f(a)+g(a),
\]
\[
{}^*f(a+\varepsilon)-{}^*g(a+\varepsilon)
\approx
f(a)-g(a),
\]
and
\[
{}^*f(a+\varepsilon){}^*g(a+\varepsilon)
\approx
f(a)g(a).
\]

If $g(a)\neq0$, then
\[
\frac{{}^*f(a+\varepsilon)}{{}^*g(a+\varepsilon)}
\approx
\frac{f(a)}{g(a)}.
\]

Therefore the stated functions are continuous at $a$.
\end{proof}


\begin{theorem}[Composition of Continuous Functions]
Let
\[
g:A\to\mathbb{R}
\]
be continuous at $a$, and let
\[
f:B\to\mathbb{R}
\]
be continuous at $g(a)$, where
\[
g(A)\subseteq B.
\]

Then
\[
f\circ g
\]
is continuous at $a$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Since $g$ is continuous at $a$,
\[
{}^*g(a+\varepsilon)\approx g(a)
\]
for every infinitesimal $\varepsilon$.

Since $f$ is continuous at $g(a)$,
\[
f({}^*g(a+\varepsilon))
\approx
f(g(a)).
\]

Therefore
\[
(f\circ g)(a+\varepsilon)
\approx
(f\circ g)(a).
\]

Hence $f\circ g$ is continuous at $a$.
\end{proof}


\begin{theorem}[Continuous Functions Have Continuous Limits]
Let
\[
f:A\to\mathbb{R}
\]
be continuous at $a$. Then
\[
\lim_{x\to a}f(x)=f(a).
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
This follows from the Continuity and Limits Theorem.
\end{proof}


\section{Derivatives}

first we have to fix a ``Main variable'' it's almost all the time ``$x$''.
(sometimes it's ``$t$'').
\begin{definition} assume $f : \mathbb{R} (Ord) \to \mathbb{R} (Ord)$
    we define \textbf{Difrential $(d_\varepsilon(f))$}
    \[ d_\varepsilon(f) := f( x + \varepsilon ) -  f(x) \]
\end{definition}





Main reason we choose ``$x$'' in definition because it's ``Main variable''. you
can prove $\forall x \in \mathbb{R} (Ord) \quad d_\varepsilon(x) = \varepsilon$.

\begin{definition} we define \textbf{Derivative}

    
Let $ f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}$
and let \(  \varepsilon  \in\mathbb{R} (Ord)\) be a nonzero infinitesimal:
\[
 \varepsilon \neq 0,
\qquad
| \varepsilon |<\frac{1}{n}
\quad\forall n\in\mathbb{N}.
\]

Define


\[
\frac{df}{dx} = f'(x) =
\operatorname{st}
\left(
\frac{d_\varepsilon(f)}{d_\varepsilon(x)}
\right)
\]

Equivalently,
\[
\frac{df}{dx}
=
\operatorname{st}
\left(
\frac{f(x + \varepsilon )-f(x)}{ \varepsilon }.
\right)
\]


More generally,

    \[ \frac{d}{dx}  :=  \{ \big(f , st(\frac{d_\varepsilon(f)}{d_\varepsilon(x)}) \big) \mid f : \mathbb{R} (Ord) \to \mathbb{R} (Ord) \} \]
    there is a biggest set like $A$ s.t. $ \frac{d}{dx} A$ is a function (you can
    prove it as an exercise). we call that set ``Derivatible funsions'' and that
    function ``Derivative''.
\end{definition}


\section{Partial Derivatives}

first we have to fix a ``Main variable'' it's almost all the time ``$x$''.
(sometimes it's ``$t$'').
\begin{definition} assume $f : \mathbb{R} (Ord)^n  \to \mathbb{R} (Ord)$
    we define \textbf{Partial Difrential $(d_\varepsilon(f))$}
    \[ \partial_{\varepsilon , x_i } (f) := f(x_1,\ldots,x_i+ \varepsilon ,\ldots,x_n) - f(x_1,\ldots,x_i,\ldots,x_n) \]
\end{definition}

Main reason we choose ``$x$'' in definition because it's ``Main variable''. you
can prove $\forall x \in \mathbb{R} (Ord) \quad \partial_{\varepsilon , x }(x) = \varepsilon$.

\begin{definition} we define \textbf{Partial Derivative}

    
Let$f:\mathbb{R}^n\to\mathbb{R},
\qquad
f=f(x_1,\ldots,x_n).$
and let \(  \varepsilon  \in\mathbb{R} (Ord)\) be a nonzero infinitesimal:
\[
 \varepsilon \neq 0,
\qquad
| \varepsilon |<\frac{1}{n}
\quad\forall n\in\mathbb{N}.
\]

 define

\[
\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_i}
=
\operatorname{st}
\left(
\frac{\partial_{\varepsilon , x_i }(f)}{\partial_{\varepsilon , x_i }(x_i)}
\right)
\]

Equivalently,
\[
\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_i}
=
\operatorname{st}
\left(
\frac{
f(x_1,\ldots,x_i+ \varepsilon ,\ldots,x_n)
-
f(x_1,\ldots,x_i,\ldots,x_n)
}{ \varepsilon }
\right)
\]

More generally,
    \[ \frac{ \partial }{\partial x_i }  :=  \{ \big(f , st(\frac{\partial_{\varepsilon , x_i }(f)}{\partial_{\varepsilon , x_i }(x_i)}) \big) \mid f : \mathbb{R} (Ord) \to \mathbb{R} (Ord) \} \]
\end{definition}



\section{Riemann Integral}



\begin{definition}

Let $f:[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}$. A \textbf{Partition} of $[a,b]$ is a finite ordered set $P=\{x_0,x_1,\ldots,x_n\}$ such that

$$
a=x_0<x_1<\cdots<x_n=b.
$$

We define the \textbf{Mesh} of $P$ by

$$
\|P\|:=\max_{1\leq i\leq n}(x_i-x_{i-1}).
$$

For every partition $P$, define $M_i:=\sup\{f(x):x\in[x_{i-1},x_i]\}$ and $m_i:=\inf\{f(x):x\in[x_{i-1},x_i]\}$. The \textbf{Upper Darboux Sum} of $f$ with respect to $P$ is

$$
U(f,P):=\sum_{i=1}^{n}M_i(x_i-x_{i-1}),
$$

and the \textbf{Lower Darboux Sum} of $f$ with respect to $P$ is

$$
L(f,P):=\sum_{i=1}^{n}m_i(x_i-x_{i-1}).
$$

We say that $f$ is \textbf{Riemann Integrable} on $[a,b]$ if $f$ is bounded and

$$
\inf_P U(f,P)=\sup_P L(f,P).
$$

In this case, we define

$$
\int_a^b f(x)\,dx
:=\inf_P U(f,P)
=\sup_P L(f,P).
$$

\end{definition}
\begin{theorem}[Convergence of Riemann Sums]

Suppose that $f:[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}$ is Riemann integrable. For each $n\in\mathbb{N}$, let

$$
x_i=a+\frac{i(b-a)}{n},
\qquad 0\leq i\leq n.
$$

Then

$$
\lim_{n\to\infty}
\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}f(x_i)\frac{b-a}{n}
=
\int_a^b f(x)\,dx.
$$

\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}

Let $P_n=\{x_0,x_1,\ldots,x_n\}$. Since $f$ is Riemann integrable,

$$
\lim_{n\to\infty}U(f,P_n)
=
\lim_{n\to\infty}L(f,P_n)
=
\int_a^b f(x)\,dx,
$$

because $\|P_n\|=(b-a)/n\to0$.

Since $f(x_i)$ lies between the infimum and supremum of $f$ on $[x_i,x_{i+1}]$,

$$
L(f,P_n)
\leq
\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}f(x_i)\frac{b-a}{n}
\leq
U(f,P_n).
$$

Therefore, by the Squeeze Theorem,

$$
\int_a^b f(x)\,dx
=
\lim_{n\to\infty}
\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}f(x_i)\frac{b-a}{n}
$$

\end{proof}

Since the Riemann sum converges to $\int_a^b f(x)\,dx$, by the Standard Part Theorem, for an infinite $N$ and $\varepsilon=\frac{b-a}{N}$,

$$
\int_a^b f(x)\,dx.
=
\operatorname{st}
\left(
\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}f(x_i)\varepsilon
\right)
$$

 let $\varepsilon=\frac{b-a}{N}$ and $x_i=a+i\varepsilon$. we can prove for every choice of sample points $\xi_i\in[x_i,x_{i+1}]$,

$$
\int_a^b f(x)\,dx.
=
\operatorname{st}
\left(
\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}f(\xi_i)\varepsilon
\right)
$$


More generally,
\[
\int_a^b
:=
\left\{
\left(
f,\,
\operatorname{st}
\left(
\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}
f(\xi_i)\varepsilon
\right)
\right)
\middle|
f\text{ is Riemann integrable on }[a,b]
\right\}.
\]

There is a biggest set $A$ such that $\int_a^b A$ is a function
(you can prove it as an exercise). We call that set
``Riemann Integrable Functions'' and that function
``Riemann Integral''.


\chapter{Ordinal Integral{\small(more powerful than Lebesgue Integral)\small}}


\[
\lim_{n\to\alpha} f(n)
:=
\left[f(n)\right]_{n\in\alpha}
\]

\[
\lim_{n\to\alpha + m} f(n)
:=
\left[f(n+m)\right]_{n\in\alpha}
\]

\[
f:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
\]

\[
\text{Extended } f\longrightarrow f^*
\]

\[
f(\alpha)
=
\lim_{n\to\alpha}f(n)
\]

\[
\text{Trivial Extension}
\]

\[
\sum_{i=0}^{2^{\aleph_0}}i
=
\lim_{n\to 2^{\aleph_0}}
\sum_{i=0}^{n}i
=
\left[
\sum_{i=0}^{n}i
\right]_{n\in 2^{\aleph_0}}
=
\left[
\frac{n(n+1)}{2}
\right]_{n\in 2^{\aleph_0}}
=
\frac{2^{\aleph_0}(2^{\aleph_0}+1)}{2}
\]

\[
\sum_{i=1}^{\aleph_1+5}i^2
=
\lim_{n\to\aleph_1+5}
\sum_{i=0}^{n}i^2
=
\left[
\sum_{i=0}^{n}i^2
\right]_{n\in\aleph_1+5}
=
\left[
\frac{n(n+1)(2n+1)}{6}
\right]_{n\in\aleph_1+5}
\]

\[
=
\frac{(\aleph_1+5)(\aleph_1+6)(2\aleph_1+11)}{6}
\]

\[
D:[0,1]\to\{0,1\}
\]

\[
D(x)=
\begin{cases}
1,&x\in\mathbb{Q}^{c},\\
0,&x\in\mathbb{Q}.
\end{cases}
\]

\[
\int_0^1D(x)\,dx\;?
\]

\[
\exists (y_i)_{i\in\omega}
\qquad
\{y_i\}_{i\in\omega}
=
\mathbb{Q}\cap[0,1]
\]

\[
\exists (x_i)_{i\in 2^{\aleph_0}}
\qquad
\{x_i\}_{i\in 2^{\aleph_0}}
=
\mathbb{Q}^{c}\cap[0,1]
\]

\[
f:2^{\aleph_0}+\omega\to[0,1]
\]

\[
f(i)=
\begin{cases}
D(y_i),&i\in\omega,\\
D(x_i),&i\in 2^{\aleph_0}.
\end{cases}
\]

\[
\int_0^1D(x)\,dx
=
\operatorname{st}
\left(
\frac{\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{2^{\aleph_0}+\omega}f(i)}
{2^{\aleph_0}+\omega}
\right)
=
\operatorname{st}
\left(
\frac{2^{\aleph_0}}
{2^{\aleph_0}+\omega}
\right)
=
1
\]

\[
\int_a^b f(x)\,dx
=
\operatorname{st}
\left(
\frac{\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{\alpha}y(i)}
{\alpha}
\right)
\]

\[
y(i)=f(x_i),
\qquad
\{x_i\}_{i\in\alpha}=[a,b].
\]
